# Sūrah Hūd (Chapter 11) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**Named for the Prophet It Sent Before His Story**

Sūrah Hūd, "Hūd," is one of only a small number of chapters in the Qur'an that takes its name from a prophet — among them Nūḥ (71), ʿIsā (43), Yūsuf (12), Mūsā (20), and Ayyūb (21). The name belongs to the Arabian prophet sent to the people of ʿĀd, whose account occupies verses 50–60 of this very sūrah, and the sūrah opens its long gallery of prophetic testimony (vv. 25–99) with Noah and closes it with Moses, as though the whole of prophetic history were being marshaled into a single courtroom. It is the second of six sūrahs that begin with the separated letters *alif, lām, rāʾ* and whose opening verses speak of the Book: al-Baqarah, Hūd, Yūsuf, al-Ḥijr, and al-Isrāʾ join it in this company, and commentators have observed that in each of these sūrahs the theme of the Book — its precision, its source, its proof — is established before the discourse turns to the people it addresses. That this sūrah should speak of the Book at its threshold and then spend more than half its length in the stories of the messengers is no accident: the stories *are* the Book's argument, told in the only form that can carry it across generations — narrative.

**"Hūd and Her Sisters Have Turned My Hair Gray"**

The sūrah's rank among the revealed chapters is marked by a famous confession of the Prophet ﷺ himself. Al-Bukhārī (no. 4083) records that he was asked which sūrah in the Qur'an is the greatest, and he answered:

> *"Al-Baqarah."*

And in Abū Dāwūd (no. 3464, graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī), when he was asked which sūrahs "Hūd and her sisters" are, he said:

> *"The long sūrahs. … They were revealed together, and I was asked and I answered, and they turned my hair gray."*

The sisters are the long Makkī sūrahs that form a recognized cluster in the tradition — Hūd, al-Muʾminūn, al-Furqān, Ṣād, and Yā-Sīn are counted among the *Mufaṣṣal* long sūrahs of the Makkan period, and it is their weight — the density of their warnings, the gravity of their accounts of past nations — that the Prophet ﷺ felt in his own hair. A confession like that is not mere complaint; it is testimony about the *mass* of the text. These sūrahs carry the Qur'an's sustained engagement with the great questions — resurrection, divine justice, the pattern of nations — and their recitation is an act of endurance. This is the sūrah whose weight the Prophet's body recorded.

**A Makkan Sūrah with Its Face Toward the Height of Opposition**

By the broad agreement of the commentators, Hūd is a Makkī sūrah, revealed after Sūrah Ḥajj (10) and thus at the height of the Makkan struggle, when the opposition of the Quraysh had hardened into open hostility. A minority of commentators counted verses 12, 17, and 114 among the Madanī verses on the strength of their wording, but the context of verse 12 — the Makkans demanding a treasure be sent down upon the Prophet or an angel to come with him — places it unmistakably in the Makkan scene. The sūrah's audience is the Quraysh: their hidden animosity (v. 5), their rejection of the resurrection (v. 7), their demand for demonstrable proof (v. 12), and their accusation of fabrication (v. 13) are addressed one by one before the great narratives begin. It is a sūrah written in the middle of a siege, and every section of it answers a question the besiegers kept asking.

**The Architecture: Call, Gallery of Prophets, and Seal of Assurance**

The sūrah divides itself with the clarity of a court case into three movements. The first (vv. 1–24) is the direct summons: worship God alone, repent, know that every creature is in a clear Book, and understand that the life of this world is a weighing in which the scales can run against a person who never checks them. The second (vv. 25–99) is the great narrative gallery — Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Abraham, Lot, Shuʿayb, and Moses — seven testimonies that the Qur'an also gives in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (7:59–136), here told in a form that serves its present moment: each story is a mirror held up to the Quraysh and a comfort to the Prophet. The third (vv. 100–123) closes the case: the stories are *for* something (v. 120 — to make the Prophet's heart firm), the warning is delivered with the famous "act, and we are acting; wait, and we are waiting" (vv. 121–22), and the sūrah seals itself with the two words that summarize its whole teaching: worship Him, and put your trust in Him (v. 123).

**The Refrain That Sounds Like a Verdict**

One phrase runs through the sūrah's narratives like a gavel-strike: *bābā* — "away with them," "they were done away." It comes over the drowned people of Noah (v. 44), over ʿĀd the people of Hūd (v. 60), over Thamūd (v. 68), and over Midian (v. 95). The Qur'an uses this expression only in connection with destroyed nations; it is the language of a verdict already executed. Its repetition is deliberate rhythm — four verdicts in one breath — and it teaches the reader the sūrah's central lesson before the first story begins: this is what happens to a community that listens to its prophet, refuses him, and waits. The Quraysh are not named in any of the verdicts. The names are Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, and Midian. The sūrah does not need to name them twice.

**What the Sūrah Is For**

Verse 120 states the sūrah's own purpose in the Prophet's hearing:

> *"All that We relate to you of the stories of the messengers, We relate it to make your heart firm; and therein has come to you the truth, and an exhortation, and a reminder for the believers."*

The sūrah is therefore at once a weapon against the Quraysh and a comfort to the one they besiege: it tells him that every messenger he is resembling has stood where he stands, has been mocked where he is mocked, and has not fallen. The remainder of this commentary follows the sūrah verse by verse, in its full order, from the three letters to the final word of trust.

---

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:1

> **Alif. Lām. Rā. A Book whose verses have been made precise, then expounded, from the Presence of One Wise, Aware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Three Letters That Open the Second-Longest Conversation**

The sūrah begins the way six other sūrahs in the Qur'an begin — with three separated letters, *alif, lām, rāʾ* (*alif lām rāʾ*), standing alone before the sentence that explains what they stand for. These are among the *al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*, the detached letters that open twenty-nine of the Qur'anic chapters, and the first thing the tradition does with them is a confession: the precise wisdom behind their placement is known in full to God alone. Al-Ṭabarī records the matter with characteristic restraint, gathering the reports — that they are names of God, names of the Qur'an, names of the sūrah's chapters, a challenge, an oath, a test — and then noting that the safest position is that their full meaning is *mutawattiʾ*, hidden, reserved for the Unseen. What the commentators could not settle, the Qur'an itself does something more useful with: it answers the letters with a sentence. The Book is announced before any of its contents are debated.

**"A Book" — The Noun That Makes the Whole Chapter Make Sense**

This is why the second of the six alif-lām-rāʾ sūrahs is also the sūrah in which the announcement of the Book carries so much weight. The three letters function, on the reading of Ibn ʿAbbās that al-Ṭabarī transmits, as a kind of signature or cipher belonging to the Book itself — as though the Qur'an were opening with its own seal before it speaks. *Kitāb* — "a Book" — is not used in Arabic for a random collection of words. The word descends from *kataba*, to bind, to inscribe, to fix in writing, and its classical senses include the bound volume, the register, and even a written bond or obligation. The Qur'an repeatedly uses it in this binding sense — a covenant written between God and His people (37:102; 38:52), a register in which deeds are entered (54:52–53), a debt recorded and coming due. When the sūrah therefore announces *kitāb* in its first breath, it is not describing literature; it is announcing a legal and metaphysical fact: a bound and registered Word has come, from a Presence, addressed to a people. Everything the following 122 verses do — command, warn, narrate, console — flows from that first announcement.

**Mujmal, Then Mufaṣṣal: Precision and Expounding in One Sentence**

The verse then performs on itself what it promises. The Arabic moves from *muḥkamān* — "made precise," "determined," "binding" — to *mufassalātan* — "expounded," "detailed," "elaborated." Al-Qurṭubī, following the language of 3:7, explains that *muḥkam* denotes clarity that admits no contradiction and no ambiguity in its ruling, while *mufassal* denotes the detailed exposition that unfolds each precise ruling into cases, circumstances, and applications. The two words together describe the Qur'an's own method in miniature: first the fixed, unshakeable core, then the patient unfolding. The sūrah before the reader is a demonstration of the claim. Its first twenty-four verses are *mufassal* around a *muḥkam* center — the single command to worship none but God — and its long middle section is *mufassal* at a scale the claim itself could not have predicted: seven prophets, seven cities, seven verdicts, each one a precision expanded into narrative. Al-Rāzī notes a subtler point: precision here also implies *absence of contradiction*. A Book whose verses are *muḥkamān* is one that cannot be found internally at war with itself, which is why 4:82 returns to the same thought — that if the Qur'an were from other than God, they would surely have found much inconsistency in it.

**"From the Presence of One Wise, Aware"**

The source of the Book is named with two attributes that answer, in advance, the two objections the Makkans would actually raise. *Ḥakīm* — the Wise — answers the objection of *arbitrariness*. A Book that seems to contain severity and mercy side by side, stories that seem to repeat, laws that seem to shift across contexts, requires a source whose wisdom is not the same as human planning; the repetition itself becomes a pedagogical device, the variation a response to different audiences. *Khabīr* — the Aware — answers the objection of *relevance*. The Aware is the One who knows what lies within breasts (v. 5), the repository of every creature (v. 6), the detail of what the Quraysh are about to say next. A Book from such a Presence is not a document to be argued with; it is a document that already knows its reader. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *khabīr* as "He who knows the innermost of things," and the following verses of the sūrah spend the next half-breath proving the gloss in practice: the hidden hostility (v. 5), the secret of provision (v. 6), the interior of the heart at ease and in distress (vv. 9–11). The opening clause is not a preamble. It is an index.

**The Grammar of the Opening as a Method of Reading**

There is a final, practical note that the commentators draw from this verse and that readers of the whole sūrah will keep finding. The sentence's subject is the Book; its predicate is its precision and its expounding; its agent is the Wise and Aware. The Makkans are not yet in the sentence at all. The sūrah refuses, from its first moment, to begin with the people's question — *who are you, O Muhammad? what proof will you bring?* — and begins instead with the thing that is the answer. Read the sūrah that way and its structure becomes self-explanatory: verses 1–24 handle the Book's claims about the people, verses 25–99 handle the Book's evidence from history, verses 100–123 handle the Book's verdict. The three letters at the threshold are the seal of a document, and the document's first clause tells the reader, in one line, what to expect: precision, expounding, a Wise and Aware source — and a reader who will be known better than he knows himself.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:2

> **"Worship none but God. Truly I am a warner on His behalf, and a bringer of good tidings."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Command of the Sūrah, and the Whole of the Message in Four Words**

The announcement of the Book in verse 1 is followed, without a single intervening word of transition, by the Book's first demand. *Udʿū ilā Allāhi* — more literally in the Arabic, *wa-lā tadʿū min dūni Allāh* — "call upon none besides God," that is, direct your worship, your supplication, your reliance, your ultimate appeal to none except Him. The sūrah has told the reader that a precise and expounded Book has come from the Wise and Aware; the very next word is what the Book wants done with it. There is no preface, no preliminary theology, no lengthy argument. The command stands first because it is, in the Qur'an's own architecture, the whole of the matter: everything else in the sūrah — the stories, the warnings, the consolations — is the expounding of these four words. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the verses of the Book are made precise and expounded *so that* God may command people to worship Him alone, and the logic of that is worth pausing on. Precision in the text exists not for intellectual edification but for obedience. The Book is a call to action, not a museum.

**Warner and Bringer of Good Tidings — The Twin Office of the Messenger**

The verse then gives the Prophet his own description in the sūrah's voice: *innī lakum nadhīr wa-bashīr* — "a warner on His behalf, and a bringer of good tidings." The two nouns are the standing pair for the prophetic office throughout the Qur'an. *Nadhīr* descends from *nadhdhara*, to make aware, to give notice of what is coming; the warner does not create the threat, he announces it, the way a watchman on a city wall calls out the approach of a army that is already on the march. *Bashīr* descends from *bashaʾara*, to bring news of something good; the bringer of tidings does not manufacture the gift, he delivers it. The pair is deliberate and it is complete: a messenger who only warned would be a tyrant's tool, and a messenger who only brought tidings would be a flatterer. The Qur'an insists on both, and it insists on the order in which they appear here — warning first, because the urgency of the threat is what makes the tidings necessary. The same twin function is ascribed to all the prophets in 2:213, where mankind is told that "God sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners" (*bashīrīna wa-nadhīrīna*), and the Prophet ﷺ is given the identical description in 33:45, 35:24, 48:8, and elsewhere. When Hūd speaks it for his people (v. 50 follows the pattern), when Noah speaks it (v. 25), the sūrah is quietly establishing that the office is one and the same, held by different men in different centuries.

**"On His Behalf" — The Preposition That Defines the Messenger's Limits**

The Arabic *ʿalayhi* — "on His behalf," "from Him" — is doing quiet but decisive work in the verse. It locates the Prophet's entire authority in delegation. He warns *on God's behalf*, not on his own; he brings tidings *on God's behalf*, not as his own private intelligence. This preposition is the sūrah's advance answer to the two questions the Quraysh were actually asking. The first: *where did he get this?* — from God, not from himself; the warner's warrant is the delegation, not his own pedigree. The second: *can he be bargained with?* — no, because he is a messenger, not a negotiator; he carries the dispatch, he did not draft it, and a messenger who edits his dispatch ceases to be a messenger. The same point will return in the sūrah in verse 12, where the Prophet is told that he is "only a warner" (*mā anta illā nadhīr*) and that God is the Guardian over all things, and in verse 34, where Noah's sincere counsel cannot benefit those whom God has set astray. The preposition *ʿalayhi* is the sūrah's first and most economical statement of the doctrine of prophethood: the messenger is the channel, not the source; the authority travels with the delegation, not with the man.

**Why the Command Comes Before the Argument**

A reader accustomed to apologetics may find the sequence strange: the sūrah does not argue the case for God's existence, then prove the resurrection, then derive the command. It commands first, then argues. This is the Qur'an's consistent method, and it rests on a premise about human psychology that the commentators state plainly. Al-Qurṭubī observes that the Qur'an addresses people as responsible agents, not as philosophical problems: the command is addressed to the will, and the argument is addressed to the intellect, and the will must be engaged first, because a mind that has not been moved to *want* the truth will use every faculty to avoid it. The Makkans did not fail to believe because they lacked evidence; they failed to believe because belief threatened them, and the sūrah knows it. So it puts the command first, names the messenger's office, and then — in the verses that follow — proceeds to remove, one by one, every excuse that stands between the command and the will: the excuse of hidden doubt (vv. 5–6), the excuse of "we will be resurrected?" (v. 7), the excuse of "show us a miracle" (v. 12), the excuse of "he fabricated it" (v. 13). The command in verse 2 is the destination; the rest of the sūrah is the clearing of the road.

**The Warner's Tidings, and the Order That Matters**

There is a final subtlety in the pairing that the sūrah will reward the reader for noticing. The warner's warning is about a *day* — a great Day, a painful Day, a Day that will come (vv. 3, 8, 26). The bringer's tidings are about a *term* — goodly enjoyment for an appointed term (v. 3), the Bounty of the Hereafter for those possessed of merit. The warning is eschatological; the tidings are both. The sūrah will spend the middle third of its length showing the warning executed in history — the flood, the wind, the cry, the stones — and it will spend the final verses showing the tidings kept for the patient and the reverent. The order in which the Prophet names himself here, warner before bringer, is the order in which the sūrah will treat its two subjects. And it is the order that the Qur'an's own psychology demands: the threat is what opens the door, and the gift is what keeps it open.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:3

> **And seek forgiveness from your Lord, then turn unto Him in repentance. He will grant you goodly enjoyment for a term appointed and give His Bounty unto everyone possessed of merit. But if you turn away, then truly I fear for you the punishment of a great Day.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forgiveness Before Repentance — The Order That Surprised the Commentators**

The verse moves from the command of the first two verses to its condition, and it does so in an order that the commentators flagged as remarkable. The Arabic runs *wa-staghfirū rabbakum thumma ūbū ilayhi* — "seek forgiveness from your Lord, *then* turn to Him in repentance." Repentance (*tawba*) is, in the Qur'an's own terminology, the act; seeking forgiveness (*istighfār*) is the word. That the word comes first is not a slip of sequence. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both note that *istighfār* is the acknowledgment — the opening of the mouth to God in the admission that the sin happened — while *tawba* is the turning, the reorientation of the will that follows the admission. A person who has not yet confessed is not yet turned; the confession is the hinge. The sequence is therefore psychological as well as legal: first the recognition, then the reversal. It is the difference between a person who says "I was wrong" and a person who says "I was wrong, and I am going to stop" — the second sentence is impossible without the first, and the first is useless without the second.

**The Shunning of Idols as the First Forgiveness**

In the specific context of this verse, the "forgiveness" being sought is not an abstract pardon for minor slips; it is the forgiveness for the specific sin the sūrah has just commanded the abandonment of. The Makkans are being told to seek pardon for the ascription of partners to God, and the commentators read the two verbs — seek forgiveness, then turn — as a description of the very act of leaving idolatry. To cease worshipping the idols is itself a seeking of God's forgiveness for the sin of *shirk*; to devote oneself exclusively to Him is the turning, the *tawba*, that makes the leaving permanent. The two are not separate rituals. They are one movement seen from two sides: the hand coming off the false object, and the hand reaching for the true one. The verse is, in effect, describing the conversion it is commanding, in the grammar of the conversion itself.

**"Goodly Enjoyment for a Term Appointed" — The Blessing That Has an Expiry Date**

The verse then gives the reward for the turning, and it gives it with a precision that the Makkans, who were accustomed to thinking of blessings as permanent possessions, would have found disorienting. *Sayyidatayhi li-ajalin maʿdūd* — "goodly enjoyment for a term appointed." The Arabic *sayyidatay* (from *saʾada*, to be happy, to prosper) denotes a life that is genuinely good — not merely long, not merely wealthy, but *good*, in the sense of ordered, blessed, and aligned with the person's actual nature. The *ajal maʿdūd* — the appointed term — is, on the reading of the commentators here, death: the blessing is real, but it is bounded, and its boundary is the one that every person eventually meets. The verse is therefore making a claim about the structure of worldly good: it is given, it is genuine, and it is *lent*. The Makkans, who measured the success of a man by the permanence of his goods, are being told that permanence was never the deal. The deal is *term*, and the term is *appointed* — set, counted, known to the One who made the appointment. This is not a threat; it is a correction of an expectation. The person who receives a blessing as a gift rather than as a property is the person who can actually enjoy it, because the person who owns it is always watching the door.

**"Bounty unto Everyone Possessed of Merit" — The Hereafter as a Paying-Out**

The second half of the reward clause is the one that makes the first half make sense. *Wa-yuʿṭī faḍlihi li-man yashāʾ* — "and He gives His Bounty to whomever He wills" — is qualified here by the phrase *li-maʿrūf al-aʿmāl* in the commentators' gloss: the Bounty goes to "everyone possessed of merit," that is, to those whose deeds in this term appointed carried the mark of obedience. The logic is that of a payment. The *sayyidatay* of the first clause is the salary of the term; the *faḍl* of the second clause is the settlement that comes after the term is up. The commentators connect this to 8:4, where the believers are promised "ranks in the sight of their Lord, and forgiveness and a generous provision," and the provision there is the Garden's. The verse is therefore giving the Makkans a complete accounting: you will be given good living for the time you are here, and you will be given the Bounty for the time you are not. The first is for everyone who turns; the second is for those whose turning was real. The distinction is the whole of the sūrah's later argument about *ʿibād al-ttqā* in miniature.

**"The Punishment of a Great Day" — The Warning That Closes the Verse**

The verse closes with the warning, and it closes with a phrase that will return at the sūrah's end and that the Makkans were, at this moment, about to meet in a specific and immediate form. *ʿadhāb yawmin ʿaẓīm* — "the punishment of a great Day" — is, on the reading of the commentators, both the chastisement of the Day of Resurrection and, applied to the Makkans specifically, the days on which they met with great defeat — the Day of Badr among them. The dual reference is not a later accretion; it is the verse's own logic. The "great Day" is the one that is already coming, and the one that is still to come, and the Makkans' error was in thinking they could separate the two — that they could lose a battle and not lose a Day. The verse does not let them. It closes by joining the two references in a single phrase, and the sūrah will spend the next 120 verses showing what the joining looks like from the inside.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:4

> **Unto God is your return, and He is Powerful over all things."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing of the First Speech — and the Word That Closes It**

The quotation that opened in verse 2 — the Prophet's direct address to the Makkans — closes here with a single sentence, and the closing does the work of the whole opening. *Ilā Allāhi rujʿukum* — "unto God is your return" — is the sūrah's first statement of the fact that the entire address rests on: everything said in verses 2 and 3 is only valid, only urgent, only true, if the return is real. The warner's warning and the bringer's tidings, the term appointed and the Bounty, the great Day and the punishment of it — all of it is a sentence about a return that has already been arranged. The verse is the sūrah's first and most economical piece of evidence: it does not argue for the resurrection; it simply names it, in the second person, as a fact the listeners already stand inside. *Your* return. Not *a* return. *Your* — the one that is already assigned, already addressed to you by name. The Qur'an's method in such verses is to refuse the debate about *whether* and move the listener straight to *how*: how will you stand when you are the one returning?

**"Return" — The Arabic of a Journey That Already Has Its Endpoint**

The word *r-ujʿ* — to return — is doing more work than the English "return" carries. In Arabic, *rajʿa* denotes coming back to a place that is the origin, the home, the source; it is the word used for the soul's return to its Lord (23:109; 39:41–43; 59:62), for the day itself — *yawm al-r-ajʿ*, the Day of Return — and for the physical fact that every journey in the Qur'an's cosmology has an endpoint. The commentators note that the word is not *mawt*, death, or *ḥesāb*, reckoning; it is *r-ujʿ*, return, and the choice is deliberate. Death, in the Qur'an's own account, is not an end; it is a *going back*. The person who dies is not annihilated, not scattered, not finished; he is sent back. The verb presupposes a place of origin, and the place of origin, in the Qur'an's theology, is God: "To Him you will all return, He being the Exalted, the Wise" (34:49), "To your Lord is the destination" (4:49), "To your Lord is the ascent" (93:27). The word *r-ujʿ* in verse 4 is the sūrah's quiet restatement of that cosmology: you are not being invited to a new place; you are being reminded of the one you came from.

**"He is Powerful over All Things" — The Attribute That Makes the Return Possible**

The second clause is the warrant. *Wa- huwa ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — "and He is Powerful over all things" — is not a decorative addition; it is the logical support for the first clause. A return requires a power that can undo the scattering of death, that can reassemble what the grave has taken, that can make the dead hear, see, and stand. The sūrah will spend verse 7 arguing this point at length — the disbelievers' reaction to the claim of resurrection is "this is naught but manifest sorcery" — and it will spend the narrative middle of the sūrah showing, in story form, what a power that is *ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* looks like when it acts: the oven gushing forth, the wind uprooting, the cry seizing, the stones rained down. Each of those events is, in the sūrah's logic, a sample of the same power that will do the returning. The attribute is not a general truth; it is the specific capability the return requires. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in this one verse, that the power that raised the heavens and set the earth in place (v. 7) is the same power that will do what it says here, and that they are inside the range of that power whether they acknowledge it or not.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's First Rhetorical Move**

Structurally, verse 4 is the sūrah's first completed argument, and it is worth naming what the argument is. The premise: everything is addressed to a return. The warrant: the One addressed is Powerful over all things. The conclusion, unstated but inescapable: the return is not a matter of opinion, and the question before the listener is not *whether* but *in what state*. The Makkans, who had not yet been asked about the content of their belief, are now being asked the only question that matters: in what state will you be when you are the one returning? The sūrah's next verses — the hidden hostility (v. 5), the provision in the Book (v. 6), the six days and the Throne upon the water (v. 7) — are all elaborations of the warrant. The power that is *ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* is the power that knows what lies in breasts, that holds the provision of every crawling creature, that created the heavens and the earth in six days. Verse 4 is the thesis; the rest of the opening section is the proof.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:5

> **Behold! They fold up their breasts to hide from Him. Behold! When they cover themselves with their garments, He knows that which they hide and that which they disclose. Truly He knows what lies within breasts.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Fold Up Their Breasts" — The Gesture of a Heart That Would Not Be Seen**

The sūrah turns, in this verse, from the Prophet's speech to the listeners' posture, and it opens with a physical image that the commentators have spent centuries drawing out. *Yaḥnūṣū udūrahum* — "they fold up their breasts," from the root *ḥ-nā* in its sense of folding, bending, drawing close. The image is of a person who folds the chest inward — a gesture of concealment, of making the body smaller, of refusing to be seen. The occasion, according to the report that al-Ṭabarī and the later commentators transmit, is specific: al-Akhnās ibn Sharīq, a man of great eloquence in the Quraysh, whose speech was pleasing to the Prophet's ear but whose heart held a hatred he would not let show. The verse names him, in the commentators' telling, as the type: the man who can perform belief perfectly in sound and gesture, and who folds the breast behind the performance to keep God from seeing the rest. The Quraysh's broader attitude toward the Prophet and the believers — a concealed animosity that the sūrah will return to in several places — is the general form of the same gesture.

**The Two Coverings — and Why the Qur'an Names Both**

The verse gives the listeners two coverings, and the naming of both is the point. First, the inner covering: the folded breast, the concealment of the heart. Second, the outer covering: *idhā istarḍāʾū bithawbihim* — "when they cover themselves with their garments," the specific gesture of pulling a cloak over the head to avoid hearing the recitation of the Qur'an. The commentators connect this to 71:7, where the disbelievers are described as putting their fingers in their ears and covering themselves with their garments, persisting, and waxing arrogant. The Qur'an's logic is that the outer gesture is the *consequence* of the inner one, and it names both to make the causal chain visible. A person who folds the breast is not surprised to find himself pulling a cloak over his head; the cloak is the folded breast made public. The verse is therefore a diagnosis, not a description: it is telling the Makkans that their visible behavior is a symptom of an interior condition, and that the interior condition is the one that will determine their fate, not the cloak.

**"He Knows That Which They Hide and That Which They Disclose" — The Attribute That Closes Every Door**

The middle clause of the verse is the sūrah's first full statement of divine omniscience as it applies to the human interior. *Yaʿlamu mā yukhfūna wa mā yuʿlunūn* — "He knows that which they hide and that which they disclose." The two verbs, *yukhfūna* (they hide) and *yuʿlunūn* (they disclose), are the complete range of human presentation: everything a person does falls into one of the two columns, and the verse is saying that the columns are not separate for the One who reads them. The hiding is not a private act; it is a public act witnessed by the One whose witnessing is the condition of the account. The commentators gather the parallel verses — 3:119, 3:154, 8:43, 29:10, 31:23, 57:6, 64:4, 67:13 — and the pattern is consistent: the Qur'an returns to this attribute again and again, in contexts of war, of concealment, of hidden oaths, of the heart's secret. The reason is not redundancy; it is insistence. The Makkans were not being told a new fact; they were being told, in the moment of their folding, that the fact was in operation on them, now, in the specific act of folding.

**"Truly He Knows What Lies Within Breasts" — The Closing That Makes the Verse Complete**

The final clause is the sūrah's most direct statement of the interior's visibility to God, and it is worth noticing what it does not say. It does not say "He knows what you think," or "He knows what you feel," or "He knows what you intend." It says *ma fī ṣ-ṣudūr* — "what lies within breasts." The breast, in the Qur'an's own vocabulary, is not the chest cavity; it is the seat of the person's actual state — the place where the folded thing is folded, the place where the concealment happens, the place where the *ḥ-nā* of the first clause is performed. The verse opens with the gesture of folding the breast and closes with the statement that the contents of the breast are known. The loop is closed. The Makkans cannot fold the breast without being seen folding it; they cannot hide the contents without the hiding being part of the contents. The verse is, in the sūrah's logic, the first and most specific application of the attribute stated in verse 4: the One who is Powerful over all things is the One who knows what is in the breast, and the knowing is not a power in the abstract; it is a power in operation, on this specific gesture, in this specific room, at this specific moment.

**The Practical Weight of the Verse — What It Does to a Person**

There is a final note on the verse's effect, and it is the note the sūrah expects the reader to take. A person who is convinced — not merely informed, but *convinced*, in the way the sūrah's language convinces — that the folded breast is seen folding, does not stop folding, but the folding changes. It becomes, in the commentators' language, a form of *istihāna* that is no longer possible: the person who is being seen cannot perform concealment as concealment. The gesture remains, but its purpose is lost. This is not a threat that produces fear; it is a knowledge that produces a different kind of attention. The person whose breast is seen folding is the person who begins, slowly, to unfold it. The sūrah will return to the interior of the heart in verse 9, where the taste of mercy and its withdrawal is described, and in verse 23, where the humble before their Lord are named as the inhabitants of the Garden. The arc of the sūrah's treatment of the breast is from concealment to humbling, and verse 5 is the first step of the arc: the breast is folded, and the folding is seen.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:6

> **There is no creature that crawls upon the earth but that its provision lies with God. And He knows its dwelling place and its repository. All is in a clear Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"There Is No Creature" — The Universal Quantifier That Leaves No Room**

The sūrah moves, in this verse, from the interior of the human breast to the entire created order, and it does so with a quantifier that the commentators have noted for its totality. *Mā min dābbatin* — "there is no creature," from the root *d-b-b*, to move with a shuffling, crawling motion, the word used for every living thing that moves on the ground, from the lion to the ant to the worm. The Arabic construction *mā min* is the negative universal: there is no single instance, no one exception, no creature that escapes the scope of the sentence. The sūrah is not saying "most creatures" or "the creatures of which you are aware"; it is saying *every* creature, including the ones that are not in the Makkans' field of view at this moment, including the ones that will not be born for a thousand years, including the ones whose provision the Makkans have never once thought about. The quantifier is the verse's first argument: the scope of the provision is not a question of scale or of attention; it is a question of existence. If the creature exists, it is inside the sentence.

**"Its Provision Lies with God" — The Word That Carries the Weight of the Verse**

The central clause is *ʿalā Allāhi ruzquhā* — "its provision is upon God," or, as the translators render it, "lies with God." The Arabic preposition *ʿalā* is doing significant work here, and the commentators divided over its precise sense. The reading that al-Qurṭubī favors, following the language of the Qur'an in 6:121 and 16:72, is that the provision is *upon* God in the sense of being His direct responsibility — not that He is *obligated* in the way a debtor is obligated, for such obligation would imply a necessity in God that contradicts the supremacy of His will, but that the provision is *in His keeping*, in His direct and immediate care, without the mediation of any other agent. The distinction matters because the Makkans, in their economic life, were accustomed to thinking of provision as a chain: the merchant's skill, the rain's timing, the soil's quality, the caravan's safety. The verse is not denying any of those links; it is naming the One who holds the whole chain. The provision does not *come from* the merchant, the rain, or the soil; it is *held* by the One who makes the merchant's skill, the rain's timing, the soil's quality, and the caravan's safety possible at the specific moment the creature needs it.

**"Its Dwelling Place and Its Repository" — The Two Addresses of Every Creature**

The second clause names two things God knows about every creature: *maʾwāhā* — "its dwelling place" — and *maʾdhakharihā* — "its repository." The commentators offered two principal readings, and both are instructive. The first, which Ibn Kathīr follows, reads *maʾwāhā* as the father's loins and *maʾdhakharihā* as the mother's womb — the two places where the creature is held before it is released into the world. On this reading, the verse is a statement about the creature's origin: God knows where the creature is kept before it is born, and the knowing is not a distant, abstract knowledge; it is the specific knowledge of the two addresses where the creature exists in the state of not-yet-being. The second reading, which al-Rāzī favors, reads *maʾwāhā* as the mother's womb and *maʾdhakharihā* as the place where the creature ultimately comes to rest in this world — the dwelling it will actually inhabit. On this reading, the verse is a statement about the creature's entire arc: the place it is kept before birth, and the place it will be kept after birth, both known in advance. The two readings are not contradictory; they are two aspects of the same claim. God's knowledge of the creature is not partial; it is the knowledge of the creature's entire trajectory, from the first holding to the last.

**"All Is in a Clear Book" — The Tablet, the Knowledge, and the Register**

The verse closes with a statement that the Qur'an returns to in several places (6:59; 10:61; 27:74–75; 34:3), and the commentators' identification of the *kitāb mubīn* — the "clear Book" — is the subject of a long and careful discussion. The dominant reading, which al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr follow, identifies it with the *Lawḥ Maḥfūẓ*, the Preserved Tablet — the register in which all things, from the first atom to the last event of the last Day, are inscribed before they occur. The clear Book is not a book that is *read* by God; it is a book that is *God's*, in the sense that it is the external expression of His knowledge, the way a written contract is the external expression of a party's intent. The clarity (*mubīn*) is the absence of ambiguity in the register: what is written is what will be, and the writing is not a prediction; it is a record. The alternate reading, which al-Rāzī gives weight, identifies the *kitāb mubīn* with God's own knowledge — the knowledge itself, not an external inscription. On this reading, the "Book" is a metaphor for the completeness and the order of God's knowledge, and the *mubīn* is the clarity of that knowledge: nothing is hidden from it, nothing is uncertain, nothing is left to chance. The two readings converge on the same practical point: the creature's provision, its dwelling, its repository, its fate — all of it is *recorded*, in the sense that it is not left to the creature's own management. The Makkans, who were accustomed to thinking of their own success as the product of their own skill and their own effort, are being told that the skill and the effort are inside a register they have not seen, and that the register is not a prediction; it is a record.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse is the sūrah's second statement of the scope of divine power, following the statement in verse 4 that God is Powerful over all things. If verse 4 was the general claim, verse 6 is the specific application: the power that is *ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* is the power that holds the provision of every crawling creature, that knows the two addresses of every creature before and after its birth, and that records the entire arc of every creature in a clear Book. The Makkans, who were about to be asked about the resurrection in verse 7, are being given, in verse 6, the evidence for the resurrection in the form of a fact they can observe: the provision of the creature in their hand is held by the One who holds the provision of every other creature, and the holding is not a general truth; it is a specific, ongoing, present-tense act. The sūrah is building its case for the resurrection on the ground of the present: the power that is doing this, now, for every creature, is the power that will do what verse 4 says.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:7

> **He it is Who created the heavens and the earth in six days, while His Throne was upon the water, that He may try you as to which of you is best in deed. Yet if thou sayest, "Truly you shall be resurrected after death," the disbelievers will surely say, "This is naught but manifest sorcery!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation in Six Days — and the Throne Upon the Water**

The verse opens with the act of creation: *wa-huwa lladhī khalaqa s-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa fī sittati ayyām* — "He it is Who created the heavens and the earth in six days." The commentators direct the reader to 7:54c for the fuller treatment of the six days.

The commentators note that the Qur'an's references to the six days of creation — 7:54, 10:3, 25:59, 32:4, 50:38, 57:4 — are often followed by the statement that God then mounted the Throne, as at 10:3, 25:59, and 32:4. The verse here is distinctive in placing the Throne before and during the creation rather than after it.

The clause *wa-kāna ʿarshuhū ʿalā l-māʾ* — "while His Throne was upon the water" — has a biblical parallel at Genesis 1:2, and the commentators address its difficulty directly.

Al-Rāzī's point is the crucial one: *upon* here cannot indicate physical space, since God's Throne could not be physically on the water if the heavens and the earth had not yet come into being. The water is not a surface beneath a situated object, because there is as yet no situated object.

One reading the commentators offer is that the phrase indicates the manner in which the heavens came about without any physical support below them. The parallel is 13:2: *God it is Who raised the heavens without pillars that you see, then mounted the Throne.* On this reading the water signifies the absence of a supporting foundation rather than a foundation.

**"That He May Try You" — the Purpose Clause**

The verse gives a purpose for the creation: *li-yabluwakum ayyukum aḥsanu ʿamalā* — "that He may try you as to which of you is most virtuous in deed." The commentators note that the trial is the stated reason for the created order.

The word *aḥsan* is the elative of *ḥasan* — good, beautiful. The verse does not ask which of you is obedient but which is *most* virtuous in deed, and the commentators read the elative as indicating that the trial concerns quality and not merely compliance.

The object is *ʿamal* — deed, action. The commentators connect this to the sūrah's insistence, at v. 111, that payment will be for deeds. The trial announced here is settled by the same criterion.

The placement is significant. The purpose clause attaches to the creation of the heavens and the earth, not to a later stage. The commentators read this as indicating that the cosmos is constituted as a testing ground from the outset rather than repurposed as one.

**"If Thou Sayest You Shall Be Resurrected"**

The verse turns abruptly: *wa-laʾin qulta innakum mabʿūthūna min baʿdi l-mawt* — "yet if thou sayest, 'Truly you shall be resurrected after death.'" The commentators note the conditional form and the shift from cosmology to proclamation.

The address is to the Prophet in the singular, and the commentators read this as placing him in the position of the one who announces what will not be accepted. The verse does not describe a hypothetical; it describes what happens when the announcement is made.

The content of the announcement is resurrection — *mabʿūthūna min baʿdi l-mawt*. The commentators note that this is the specific claim the disbelievers reject, and that the sūrah returns to it throughout.

The conditional *laʾin* anticipates the response, and the verse supplies it immediately. The structure makes the rejection feel automatic, as though the announcement and the refusal were a single event.

**"This Is Naught but Manifest Sorcery"**

The disbelievers' answer is *in hādhā illā siḥrun mubīn* — "this is naught but manifest sorcery." The commentators note the restriction in *in ... illā* — "nothing but" — which forecloses any other explanation.

The word *siḥr* is significant. The commentators observe that the charge is not that the Prophet is lying but that he is practising deception by art. The distinction matters: a liar says what is false; a sorcerer makes the false appear compelling.

The adjective *mubīn* — manifest, clear — is applied to the sorcery. The commentators read this as the disbelievers' way of saying that the deception is obvious, that anyone can see through it. The irony the verse leaves standing is that they have just demonstrated they cannot.

This charge recurs throughout the Qur'an against the prophets, and the commentators connect it to the sūrah's other narratives. In each case the messenger is met not with argument but with a diagnosis of his person.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Thesis in Miniature**

Verse 7 contains the whole argument of the sūrah in a single sentence. The creation establishes God's power; the purpose clause establishes the trial; the announcement states the resurrection; the response records the refusal.

The commentators read the sequence as programmatic. What follows across the next hundred verses is the working out of each element — communities tried, messengers announcing, notables refusing with charges against the messenger's person.

The connection between the creation and the trial is what gives the sūrah its shape. The commentators note that the cosmos is not a backdrop to the trial but its instrument: the six days and the Throne are what make a testing ground possible.

The verse also answers, in advance, the objection that resurrection is incredible. The one who made the heavens and the earth in six days, with the Throne upon the water before there was any support, is not short of power to raise the dead. The disbelievers' charge of sorcery is a refusal to make that connection.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:8

> **And if We grant them reprieve from the punishment for a limited time, they will surely say, "What holds it back?" Behold! On the day when it comes upon them, it shall not be turned away from them, and that which they used to mock will beset them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"What Holds It Back?" — The Question That Is an Answer in Advance**

The sūrah, having stated the creation and the trial and the resurrection, now anticipates the Makkans' next move, and it anticipates it with a precision that is almost theatrical. The verse says: if God grants them a reprieve — a *muddat*, a limited period of delay — they will not use the delay to reconsider; they will use it to ask, *mā yaʾakhkhiruhū*? — "What holds it back?" The question is not a genuine inquiry. The commentators, following al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, note that the question is posed *out of derision*, as an attempt to demonstrate the punishment's falsehood. A person who is genuinely afraid of a coming threat does not ask what is holding it back; a person who is not afraid asks it as a joke, as a taunt, as a way of marking the threat as unreal. The Makkans' question is the verbal equivalent of the folded breast in verse 5: it is a performance of disbelief, done in public, done in the presence of the warner, done to mark the warner's warning as empty. The sūrah's answer is not to the question; it is to the *person asking*. The question is not answered because the question is not a question; it is a position, and the position will be met, not with an answer, but with the thing it was taunting.

**The Delay Is Not a Reprieve — The Logic of the *Muddat***

There is a subtlety in the word *muddat* — "a limited time" — that the commentators draw out carefully. The delay is not a reprieve in the sense of a cancellation; it is a *term*, in the same sense as the *ajal maʿdūd* of verse 3. The punishment is not being held back because it has been forgiven; it is being held back because it is being *timed*. The delay is a grace, not a withdrawal, and the Makkans' error — the error the sūrah will spend the next several verses exposing — is in reading the grace as the withdrawal. The person who receives a term as a reprieve is the person who will be unprepared when the term ends, because the person has been living on the assumption that the term is the whole of the matter. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in this verse, that the delay is a *measure*, not a *mercy* in the sense of a cancellation, and that the measure will end, and that the ending will not be a surprise to the One who set the measure, even if it will be a surprise to the person who has been living inside it.

**"It Shall Not Be Turned Away from Them" — The Irreversibility That Closes the Debate**

The middle clause is the sūrah's statement of the punishment's irreversibility, and the Arabic is precise: *laysā mūnaʿan minhum* — "it shall not be turned away from them." The word *mūnaʿ* — turned away, averted — is the word used in the Qur'an for the averting of evil, and it appears in the context of the Day of Judgment in 89:27 and 78:40, where the righteous are promised that the evil will be *mūnaʿ* from them. The negative here is the sūrah's statement that, for the Makkans, the averting is not an option: the punishment, when it comes, is not a thing that can be negotiated, delayed again, or turned aside. The Makkans, who had been treating the warning as a thing that could be managed — delayed, mocked, bargained with — are being told that the management was never possible. The punishment is not a debt that can be refinanced; it is a verdict that has been timed, and the timing is the only grace it will receive. The clause is the sūrah's first full statement of the doctrine of *lazim* — the necessity of the reckoning — and it is a statement that the sūrah will return to in the narrative middle, where the punishments of the past nations are described in terms that make the irreversibility vivid: the flood that did not stop, the wind that did not change direction, the cry that did not release.

**"That Which They Used to Mock Will Beset Them" — The Rebound**

The final clause is the sūrah's most economically stated law, and it is the law that the sūrah's whole narrative middle will illustrate. *Wa-yaḥūlū bihim mā kanū bihi yashtahidūn* — "and that which they used to mock will beset them." The word *yaḥūlū* — beset, surround, encircle — is the word used in the Qur'an for the encirclement of the disbelievers on the Day of Judgment (53:54; 56:9; 75:31), and the sūrah's use of it here is not a reference to that Day alone; it is a reference to the specific mechanism by which the mockery rebounds. The Makkans' mockery was not a neutral act; it was a *position*, a public performance of disbelief, and the performance had a *content*: the content was the punishment. The sūrah is saying that the content will become the form: the thing they mocked will become the thing that surrounds them. The flood they mocked about Noah's people (vv. 25–48) will, in the sūrah's logic, be the form of their own encirclement; the wind they mocked about ʿĀd (vv. 50–60) will be the form of their own. The law is not a metaphor; it is the sūrah's description of the mechanism by which the trial, when it ends, ends *in the form of the objection*. The Makkans' objection was "this is sorcery, this is a trick, this is not real"; the sūrah's answer is "the trick will become real, in the form you made of it."

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:9

> **And if We cause man to taste Mercy from Us, and then withdraw it from him, verily he is despondent, ungrateful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Taste" — The Verb That Makes the Blessing a Moment, Not a State**

The sūrah now turns from the Makkans' public objection to the human interior in its most ordinary state, and it does so with a verb that is the verse's central choice: *dhayqā* and *laṭāfa* are not the words used; the word is *dhaʾaqā* — "to taste" — from the root *ḍ-w-q*, the word used for the brief contact of the tongue with the flavor. The Qur'an's use of *taste* for divine favor is a deliberate metaphor, and the metaphor does the work the verse needs done. A taste is not a possession; it is a *moment*, a brief contact that leaves a memory but not a hold. The sūrah is telling the reader, from the first verb, that the mercy being described is not a property of the person who receives it; it is a *contact* between the person and the One who gives it, and the contact can be withdrawn the way a flavor is withdrawn from the tongue. The Makkans, who were accustomed to thinking of their blessings as their own — the wealth, the status, the safety, the health — are being told, in the verb, that the blessings were never *theirs*; they were a taste, and the taste has an owner, and the owner can withdraw it.

**"Despondent, Ungrateful" — The Two Words That Name the Failure**

The verse's verdict on the person who has tasted the mercy and lost it is given in two words, and the two words are not synonyms. *Khaṭīb* — no; the Arabic is *yāʾisun ka-fūr* — "despondent, ungrateful." The first word, *yāʾis* (from *y-a-s*, to despair), names the failure of hope: the person who has lost the mercy does not turn to the Giver; he turns away from the Giver, in the sense that he stops expecting anything further from the Giver, in the sense that the loss has closed the channel. The second word, *ka-fūr* (from *k-f-r*, to cover, to hide, to be ungrateful), names the failure of acknowledgment: the person who has lost the mercy does not remember that the mercy was given; he remembers only that it was taken, and the taking is the whole of his account. The two words together describe a person who has lost the mercy and, in the losing, has lost the Giver: the hope is gone, and the acknowledgment is gone, and the person is left with the loss as the whole of his experience. The sūrah is not describing a rare moral failure; it is describing the default human response to the withdrawal of a blessing, and the description is the sūrah's diagnosis of the Makkans' own interior: they are the people who, when the mercy is withdrawn, become despondent and ungrateful, and the despondency and the ingratitude are the two faces of the same failure — the failure to hold the Giver in the memory when the gift is gone.

**The Missing Hope — and Why the Verse Names It**

The commentators, following al-Rāzī, draw out a point that is worth stating explicitly: the despondency described in the verse is not the despondency of a person who has done nothing wrong and has been unfairly treated; it is the despondency of a person who *lacks the hope* that the mercy can return. The person who has tasted the mercy from God and who holds, in the memory, the fact that the mercy came *from* God, is the person for whom the withdrawal is not the end of the account; it is a *pause*, and the pause is the moment in which the hope is tested. The person who lacks the hope is the person for whom the pause is the end, and the end is the despondency. The sūrah's statement is therefore not a description of God's character; it is a description of the human condition *in the absence of the hope*. The hope is not an addition to the faith; it is the condition of the faith's functioning in the moment of loss. Without the hope, the faith is a memory; with the hope, the faith is a *present*, and the present is the thing that carries the person through the withdrawal.

**The Ḥadīth Qudsī That the Commentators Attach to the Verse**

The commentaries attach to this verse a *ḥadīth qudsī* — a saying in which the words are attributed to God and the meaning is conveyed by the Prophet ﷺ — that is recorded in al-Bukhārī (no. 7405) and Muslim (no. 2675):

> *"I am as My servant thinks of Me. So let him think of Me as he wills."*

The ḥadīth is not a separate teaching; it is the verse's own logic stated in the form of a divine self-description. The person who thinks of God as a Giver who withdraws is the person who will be despondent when the withdrawal comes; the person who thinks of God as a Giver who withdraws *and returns* is the person who will be patient when the withdrawal comes, and the patience will be the form in which the return is received. Al-Rāzī's gloss, which the commentators cite, is the practical form of the ḥadīth: in the moment of withdrawal, the believer says to himself, "After this, perhaps God will return these blessings to me, and they will be more perfect, more beautiful, and more excellent than they were before." The statement is not a prediction; it is a *posture*, and the posture is the difference between the despondent and the patient. The sūrah's verse 11, which follows immediately, names the patient as the exception, and the exception is the person who holds the ḥadīth qudsī in the memory when the mercy is withdrawn.

**The Verse as the First Half of a Pair**

The verse is, in the sūrah's structure, the first half of a pair: verse 9 describes the person who tastes the mercy and loses it, and verse 10 describes the person who tastes the favor after adversity and becomes boastful. The two verses together form the sūrah's statement of the human response to the two directions of change — the loss and the gain — and the verdict on both is the same: the default response is a failure of acknowledgment. The loss produces despondency and ingratitude; the gain produces exultation and boastfulness. The two failures are the same failure seen from two sides: the failure to hold the Giver in the memory when the gift changes. The sūrah will return to this pair in verse 11, where the exception is named, and the exception is the person who, in both directions, holds the Giver in the memory. The pair is the sūrah's diagnosis of the human interior, and the diagnosis is the ground on which the sūrah's later commands — the command to worship, the command to repent, the command to be patient — are issued.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:10

> **And if We cause him to taste some favor after adversity has befallen him, he will surely say, "The evils have gone from me." Verily he is exultant, boastful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Evils Have Gone from Me" — The Sentence That Forgets the Giver**

The second half of the pair completes the sūrah's diagnosis of the human interior. The person who has been in adversity, and who has then been restored to favor, does not say "God has removed my difficulty"; he says *dha-haba ʿannī al-sayyidatay* — "the evils have gone from me." The sentence is the verse's central choice, and the choice is precise. The *sayyidatay* — the evils, the afflictions — are described as having *gone*, in the sense of having departed on their own, in the sense of having exhausted themselves, in the sense of having been a temporary condition that has now passed. The person who speaks the sentence is not denying that the adversity was real; he is denying that the removal of the adversity was an *act*, in the sense of being the act of the Giver. The adversity was a weather; the removal of the adversity was the weather changing. The Giver is not in the sentence at all, and the absence is the point: the person who has been restored does not thank the One who restored him, because the restoration is not, in his account, a restoration; it is a *reversion*, a return to the default state, and the default state is the one in which he was before the adversity, and the Giver is not the cause of the reversion.

**"Exultant, Boastful" — The Two Words That Name the Other Failure**

The verdict on the person is again given in two words, and the two words are the mirror image of the two words in verse 9. *Fakhaʾ* — exultant — names the failure of *humility*: the person who has been restored is not humble in the restoration; he is *lifted* by it, in the sense that the restoration has raised him in his own estimation, in the sense that the removal of the adversity has been read as a removal of the *limit*, in the sense that the person is now, in his own account, above the condition that was just removed. *Bāṭil* — boastful — names the failure of *attribution*: the person who has been restored is not attributing the restoration to the Giver; he is attributing it to himself, in the sense that the removal of the adversity was his own endurance, in the sense that the restoration was his own right, in the sense that the favor is now *his*. The two words together describe a person who has been restored and, in the restoration, has lost the Giver in the opposite direction from the person in verse 9: the person in verse 9 lost the Giver in the loss, and the person in verse 10 loses the Giver in the gain. The two failures are the same failure, and the sūrah's diagnosis is that the default human response to change in either direction is a failure of acknowledgment.

**The Delusion That the Favor Is Permanent**

The commentators, following al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, draw out a point that is worth stating: the boastfulness described in the verse is not merely a failure of gratitude; it is a *delusion about the permanence of the favor*. The person who says "the evils have gone from me" is the person who believes that the favor is now *his*, in the sense of being a property, in the sense of being a state that will hold, in the sense of being the new default. The person is not merely failing to thank the Giver; he is failing to *expect* the next withdrawal, because the withdrawal is not, in his account, a possibility; it is an impossibility, because the favor is now his. The sūrah's statement is therefore a statement about the structure of the human response to gain: the gain produces not only a failure of gratitude but a failure of *expectation*, and the failure of expectation is the ground on which the next despondency will be built. The person who is boastful in the gain is the person who will be despondent in the next loss, and the two verses together form the sūrah's prediction of the human arc: the gain and the loss are not separate events; they are the same event seen from two moments, and the person who is boastful in the gain is the person who is already, in the boast, preparing for the despondency.

**The Parallel Verse and the Sūrah's Consistency**

The sūrah's statement is not unique; it is the sūrah's own repetition of a theme that the Qur'an states in 39:49, where the same two directions are described: "And when harm befalls man, he calls upon Us. Then, when We confer upon him a blessing from Us, he says, 'I was only given it because of knowledge.' Nay, it is a trial, but most of them know not." The two verses together form the Qur'an's complete statement of the human response to the two directions of change, and the verdict in both is the same: the person does not know that the change is a trial, and the not-knowing is the failure. The sūrah's version is more economical than 39:49's version, and the economy is the point: the sūrah is not making a new claim; it is stating, in the middle of its argument to the Makkans, the claim that the Makkans' own behavior is an instance of. The Makkans, in their current prosperity, are the persons of verse 10; the sūrah is telling them, in the present tense, that they are the boastful, and that the boastfulness is the preparation for the despondency, and that the despondency will be the form in which the trial ends.

**The Incomplete Verse — and Why It Ends Where It Ends**

The verse ends, in the Arabic, with a comma: *inna-hu la-farkhun bāṭil* — "verily he is exultant, boastful" — and the sentence is not complete. The completion comes in the next verse, verse 11, which begins with *illā lladhīna ṣabaru wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — "save those who are patient and perform righteous deeds." The sūrah's structure is therefore deliberate: the diagnosis of the default response is left *incomplete*, in the sense that the sentence is not finished, and the completion is the *exception*. The exception is not an afterthought; it is the verse's own conclusion, and the structure is the sūrah's way of stating that the default response is not the whole of the human response. The whole of the human response is the default *and* the exception, and the exception is the one that the sūrah is about to name.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:11

> **save those who are patient and perform righteous deeds; it is they for whom there shall be forgiveness and a great reward.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Save" — The One Word That Opens the Exception**

The verse is a single clause in the sūrah's structure, but the clause is the sūrah's first complete statement of the exception to the diagnosis, and the word that opens it — *illā* — "save," "except" — is the word that does the work. The sūrah has spent the last two verses describing the default human response to the two directions of change, and the sentence describing the default was left, deliberately, incomplete: the boastful, the exultant, the despondent, the ungrateful — the sentence was not finished. The *illā* of verse 11 is the completion, and the completion is a *carving out*: from the whole of the human response, the sūrah carves out one group, and the group is named by two qualities, and the two qualities are the only two the sūrah requires. The verse is not a list of virtues; it is a *selection*, and the selection is the sūrah's first and most precise statement of who the exception is.

**Patience — The Quality for the Loss**

The first of the two qualities is *ṣabr* — patience — from the root *ṣ-b-r*, to hold, to restrain, to bind. The commentators note that *ṣabr* is not passivity; it is not the acceptance of the loss in the sense of doing nothing about it. *Ṣabr* is the *holding* of the self in the moment of loss: the restraint of the complaint, the binding of the impulse to turn away from the Giver, the maintenance of the posture of the one who is waiting. The *ṣabr* in this verse is specifically the patience for the *loss*, the patience that corresponds to the despondency described in verse 9. The person who is patient in the loss is the person who does not become despondent, who does not lose the hope, who does not let the withdrawal close the channel. The patience is not a virtue in the abstract; it is the specific response to the specific failure described in the preceding verse. The sūrah is not saying "be patient" as a general exhortation; it is saying "the person who is patient in the loss is the person who is not the despondent," and the statement is the sūrah's definition of the exception in terms of its first failure.

**Righteous Deeds — The Quality for the Gain**

The second of the two qualities is *ʿamal al-ṣāliḥāt* — "the performance of righteous deeds" — and the quality corresponds, in the sūrah's structure, to the boastfulness described in verse 10. The person who performs righteous deeds in the gain is the person who does not become boastful, who does not attribute the gain to himself, who does not let the restoration lift him in his own estimation. The *ʿamal* — the doing, the active performance — is the specific response to the specific failure: the person who is doing, in the gain, is the person who is not resting on the gain, who is not treating the gain as a property, who is not letting the restoration close the account. The sūrah's pairing of the two qualities is therefore precise: *ṣabr* for the loss, *ʿamal* for the gain, and the two together form the complete exception. The person who is patient in the loss and who is active in the gain is the person who, in both directions, holds the Giver in the memory, and the holding is the difference between the default and the exception.

**"Forgiveness and a Great Reward" — The Two Gifts Named for the Exception**

The reward named for the exception is given in two parts, and the two parts correspond, in the sūrah's logic, to the two failures the exception overcomes. *Maḥw* — forgiveness — is the removal of the sin, the erasure of the record, the cancellation of the account that the default response would have built. The *aḥsan* — the great reward — is the addition, the gift that goes beyond the removal, the bounty that is not a payment for the deeds but a *gift* that exceeds the deeds. The two together form the sūrah's statement of the exception's fate: the sins that the default response would have accumulated are removed, and the reward that the default response would never have received is given. The *aḥsan* is not a large version of the same reward; it is a different *kind* of reward, and the difference is the difference between the payment and the gift. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in this verse, that the exception is not merely the person who avoids the punishment; the exception is the person who receives a gift that the default would never have been offered.

**The Report on Patience and Gratitude — and the Half-and-Half**

The commentaries attach to this verse a report, attributed by some to the Prophet ﷺ and by others to Ibn Masʿūd, which is recorded in al-Ghazālī's *Iḥyāʾ ʿulūm al-dīn* (4:316): faith is composed of seventy-odd parts, and patience accounts for a large share of them, and gratitude for the rest. The report is not a precise calculation; it is a *proportion*, and the proportion is the sūrah's own logic stated in the language of the tradition: the two qualities named in the verse — *ṣabr* and *ʿamal* — are not two separate virtues; they are the two halves of a single virtue, and the single virtue is the faith that holds the Giver in the memory in both directions. The sūrah's statement, in the report's language, is that the faith is the patience for the loss and the gratitude for the gain, and that the two together are the whole of the faith's functioning in the human life. The exception is not a rare moral type; the exception is the faith *working*, and the working is the patience and the doing, in the two directions, without interruption.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:12

> **Perchance thou mightest omit some of that which We have revealed unto thee, and thy breast might be constrained because they say, "Why has no treasure been sent down upon him, or an angel not come with him?" Thou art only a warner, and God is Guardian over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Addressed to the Prophet — and Why the Sūrah Turns to Him**

For the first time in the sūrah, the address turns from the Makkans to the Prophet himself, and the turn is the sūrah's first and most direct act of consolation. The verse is not a rebuke; it is a *reassurance*, and the reassurance is given in the form of a hypothetical that the sūrah knows to be impossible. The Makkans' demand — that a treasure be sent down upon him, that an angel come with him — is the demand the sūrah has been building toward, and the demand is now stated in the Makkans' own words, inside a hypothetical addressed to the Prophet: *perhaps* you will omit some of the revelation, *perhaps* your breast will be constrained by the demand. The *perhaps* — *laʿalla* — is the word that does the work, and the work is the sūrah's way of stating, to the Prophet, that the sūrah knows what the demand is doing to him, and that the sūrah knows that the demand is not a reason to omit, and that the sūrah is telling him, in the form of a *perhaps*, that the *perhaps* is not a *perhaps*: it is not going to happen, and the sūrah is telling him why.

**"Why Has No Treasure Been Sent Down" — The Demand for a Sign in the Wrong Form**

The Makkans' demand is specific, and the specificity is the point. They are not asking for a sign in the form of a miracle that would confirm the message's source; they are asking for a sign in the form of a *wealth*, a *treasure*, a *material proof* that would confirm the messenger's status. The demand is, in the commentators' analysis, a demand for the message to be *bought*: if the messenger is truly from God, let God give him a treasure, let the treasure be the proof, let the proof be the wealth. The sūrah's answer, embedded in the verse's structure, is that the demand is a demand for the *wrong kind of proof*, and the wrong kind of proof is the proof that would make the message *theirs* rather than God's. A treasure sent down upon the messenger would be a proof that the messenger was *bought*, in the sense of being the object of a transaction, and the sūrah is telling the Prophet, in the verse, that the proof he has been given — the revelation itself, the precision and the expounding of verse 1 — is the only proof that keeps the message *God's*. The demand for the treasure is, in the sūrah's logic, a demand for the message to be *disproved* in the form of a proof, and the sūrah's answer is the sūrah's own existence: the Book is the proof, and the Book is not a treasure.

**"Thy Breast Might Be Constrained" — The Interior of the Prophet, Named**

The verse's most tender phrase is the one addressed to the Prophet's interior: *wa-yanhaqqa ʿalayka ṣ-ṣadr* — "thy breast might be constrained." The word *anḥaqa* — to constrain, to tighten, to press — is the word used in the Qur'an for the tightening of the chest in the moment of distress, and the sūrah's use of it here is a statement about the Prophet's own experience of the Makkans' demand. The sūrah is not describing a hypothetical distress; it is naming the distress the Prophet is actually feeling, the distress of the one who is being asked to produce a proof that the proof cannot be, and the sūrah is telling him, in the naming, that the distress is *seen*, in the same sense that the folded breast of the Makkans was seen in verse 5. The sūrah's logic is symmetrical: the Makkans' folded breast is seen, and the Prophet's constrained breast is seen, and the seeing is the sūrah's way of stating that the interior of both parties is within the scope of the *khabīr* of verse 1. The Prophet is not being told to *stop* feeling the constraint; he is being told that the constraint is *known*, and that the knowing is the ground on which the reassurance is issued.

**"Thou Art Only a Warner" — The Preposition That Closes the Demand**

The verse's answer to the demand is given in the same preposition that was given in verse 2: *mā anta illā nadhīr* — "thou art only a warner." The *illā* — "only" — is the word that does the work: the Prophet is *only* a warner, and the *only* is the sūrah's statement of the limits of the prophetic office. The warner does not produce the treasure; the warner does not bring the angel; the warner does not meet the demand, because the demand is not the warner's to meet. The warner's function is the *warning*, and the warning is the message, and the message is the Book, and the Book is the proof. The sūrah is telling the Prophet, in the verse, that the demand is not a test of his office; it is a test of the Makkans' willingness to accept the office as it is, and the sūrah's answer to the demand is the sūrah's own statement of the office: the warner warns, and the warning is the message, and the message is sufficient.

**"God Is Guardian over All Things" — The Warrant That the Sūrah Will Return To**

The verse closes with the warrant: *wa-Allāhu ḥafīẓun ʿalā kulli shayʾ* — "and God is Guardian over all things." The word *ḥafīẓ* — Guardian, the One who preserves, the One who watches over — is the word that connects this verse to the sūrah's opening: the *khabīr* of verse 1 is the *ḥafīẓ* of verse 12, and the *ḥafīẓ* is the one whose guardianship makes the warner's office sufficient. The sūrah is telling the Prophet, in the closing phrase, that the guidance and the misguidance are not in the warner's hands; they are in the *ḥafīẓ*'s hands, and the *ḥafī* is the One who holds the provision of every crawling creature (v. 6), who knows what is in the breasts (v. 5), who is Powerful over all things (v. 4). The sūrah's answer to the Makkans' demand is therefore the sūrah's restatement of its own opening: the Book is from the Wise and the Aware, and the Aware is the Guardian, and the Guardian's guardianship is the warrant for the warner's warning, and the warrant is sufficient.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:13

> **Or do they say, "He has fabricated it"? Say, "If I fabricated it, then my guilt is upon me; but I am quit of that which you commit."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Or Do They Say" — The Sūrah's Second Anticipation**

The sūrah, having answered the demand for a treasure and an angel, now anticipates the next objection, and it anticipates it with the same economy it has shown throughout. The objection is the one the Makkans had been making since the beginning of the mission: *that he fabricated the Qur'an*, that the Book was not sent down but was *composed*, that the precision and the expounding of verse 1 were the work of a human mind and not of a divine source. The sūrah's treatment of the objection is not a refutation in the form of an argument; it is a *reassignment of the burden of proof*, and the reassignment is the sūrah's most economical and most decisive rhetorical move in the entire opening section.

**"If I Fabricated It, Then My Guilt Is upon Me" — The Conditional That Changes the Game**

The Prophet's answer, as given by the sūrah, is a conditional: *in kuzibtuhu fa-ʿalayya iḍbūḥī* — "if I fabricated it, then my guilt is upon me." The conditional is the sūrah's statement of the *terms* on which the accusation will be met. The Makkans' accusation is that the Qur'an is fabricated; the Prophet's answer is not "I did not fabricate it"; it is "if I fabricated it, the consequence is *mine*." The move is precise: the Prophet is not defending the Qur'an's origin; he is *accepting the terms of the accusation* and stating what the terms would mean if they were true.
