If the Qur'an is fabricated, then the fabricator is the one who bears the guilt of the fabrication, and the fabricator is the one who will be accountable for it. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in the conditional, that the accusation is not a neutral charge; it is a *charge with a defendant*, and the defendant is named, and the defendant is the one who will stand before the *ḥafīẓ* of verse 12 and the *khabīr* of verse 1 and give the account. The conditional is the sūrah's way of stating that the accusation is not a question about the Qur'an's origin; it is a question about the *accuser's* willingness to accept the consequence of the accusation if it is true.

**"But I Am Quit of That Which You Commit" — The Disassociation That Closes the Argument**

The second half of the answer is the *brāʾa* — the disassociation: *wa-anā min mā taʿmalūna muzāq* — "but I am quit of that which you commit." The word *muzāq* — quit, free, disassociated — is the word used in the Qur'an for the disassociation of the believer from the wrongdoing of the disbeliever, and the sūrah's use of it here is a statement about the *separation* between the messenger and the accuser. The Prophet is not saying "I did not fabricate it"; he is saying "I am *free* of what you are doing," and the *free* is the sūrah's statement of the *distance* between the messenger's account and the accuser's account. The Makkans' fabrication-accusation is not a shared enterprise; it is *their* enterprise, and the Prophet is *quit* of it, in the sense of being not a party to it, in the sense of being not liable for it, in the sense of being not the one who will stand before the *ḥafī* for it. The disassociation is the sūrah's way of stating that the accusation is not a debate; it is a *choice*, and the choice is the Makkans', and the Prophet is *free* of the choice, and the freedom is the sūrah's statement of the messenger's innocence in the form of a legal term.

**The Challenge That Was Already Issued — and the Sūrah's Restatement**

The sūrah's answer in this verse is not a new argument; it is a *restatement* of the challenge that was issued in verse 13's companion, the challenge to produce a sūrah like it, which appears in 2:23, 10:38, 17:88, and 26:181. The challenge is the sūrah's *proof* of the Qur'an's origin: if the Qur'an is fabricated, then it can be *replicated*, and the replication is the test, and the test is the challenge. The sūrah's answer in verse 13 is therefore the sūrah's restatement of the challenge in the form of a *legal position*: the Prophet is not the one who will be tested by the challenge; the Makkans are the ones who will be tested, and the test is the production, and the production is the proof. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in the verse, that the accusation is not a question they can ask; it is a *test they can take*, and the test is the production of the like, and the production is the only evidence that would support the accusation.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse is the sūrah's third and final answer to the Makkans' objections in the opening section: the demand for a treasure (answered in verse 12), the accusation of fabrication (answered in verse 13), and the rejection of the resurrection (answered in verse 7 and to be answered in the narrative middle). The three answers together form the sūrah's complete response to the Makkans' case, and the response is the sūrah's statement that the Makkans' case is not a case; it is a *set of demands* that the sūrah has met, one by one, with the sūrah's own terms. The sūrah is now free to move to the next section, and the next section is the narrative middle, where the Makkans' demands are met with the *stories*, and the stories are the sūrah's answer to the demand for a proof in the form of a *history*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:14

> **But if they answer thee not, then know that it has been sent down with God's Knowledge and that there is no god but He. So are you submitters?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"If They Answer Thee Not" — The Condition That the Sūrah Knows Will Be Met**

The sūrah, having issued the challenge, now states the condition under which the challenge will fail, and it states the condition with a knowledge that is not a prediction; it is a *certainty*. The Makkans will not answer the challenge, and the sūrah's statement of the fact is not a taunt; it is the sūrah's way of stating that the challenge has been *issued*, and that the issuing is the sūrah's complete answer, and that the Makkans' silence is not a failure of the sūrah; it is the sūrah's *proof*. The *idhā* — "if" — in the Arabic is the conditional of the certain, the conditional that states a fact in the form of a condition, and the sūrah's use of it here is the sūrah's way of telling the reader, and the Makkans, that the silence is not a possibility; it is the *expected* outcome, and the expected outcome is the sūrah's final argument.

**"It Has Been Sent Down with God's Knowledge" — The Warrant That Does Not Require the Answer**

The sūrah's answer to the silence is the warrant: *inna-hū naẓala bi-ʿilm Allāh* — "it has been sent down with God's Knowledge." The phrase is the sūrah's statement of the Qur'an's origin in the form of a *warrant*, and the warrant is the sūrah's way of stating that the Qur'an's origin does not depend on the Makkans' answer. The *ʿilm Allāh* — God's Knowledge — is the source, and the source is not a claim that requires the Makkans' verification; it is a *fact* that the sūrah states, and the fact is the sūrah's final position. The Makkans' silence does not weaken the warrant; it *confirms* it, in the sūrah's logic, because the silence is the proof that the challenge could not be met, and the proof that the challenge could not be met is the proof that the Qur'an is not fabricated, and the proof that the Qur'an is not fabricated is the proof that it was sent down with God's Knowledge. The sūrah's argument is circular in the sense that it is *complete*: the warrant does not require the answer, and the silence confirms the warrant, and the confirmation is the sūrah's final statement.

**"That There Is No God but He" — The Tawḥīd That Closes the Argument**

The second half of the warrant is the *tawḥīd*: *wa-inna huwa illā Allāh* — "and that there is no god but He." The tawḥīd is not a separate claim; it is the *source* of the warrant. The Qur'an was sent down with God's Knowledge *because* there is no god but He, and the tawḥīd is the sūrah's statement of the *ground* on which the warrant rests. The sūrah is telling the Makkans, in the phrase, that the Qur'an's origin is not a question that can be debated; it is a *consequence* of the tawḥīd, and the tawḥīd is the sūrah's first and most fundamental claim, restated here as the ground of the warrant. The Makkans, who were worshipping the idols, are being told, in the phrase, that the idols are not gods, and that the Qur'an's origin is not a product of the idols, and that the tawḥīd is the sūrah's final word on the question of the Qur'an's source.

**"So Are You Submits?" — The Question That Ends the Opening Section**

The verse closes with a question, and the question is the sūrah's final word to the Makkans in the opening section: *fa-amatattum muslimūn* — "so are you submits?" The word *muslimūn* — submitters, those who submit — is the word that names the *response* the sūrah is asking for, and the response is not a belief in the abstract; it is a *submission*, in the sense of a *turning*, in the sense of a *surrender* to the tawḥīd that the sūrah has just stated. The sūrah is asking the Makkans, in the question, whether they will *submit* to the warrant, to the tawḥīd, to the Book that was sent down with God's Knowledge. The question is not a request for an answer; it is a *statement of the choice*, and the choice is the sūrah's final word, and the sūrah will not repeat it. The Makkans' answer to the question is not given in the sūrah; it is given in the history that followed, and the history is the sūrah's own continuation, in the form of the events that came after the revelation. The sūrah's opening section ends with the question, and the question is the sūrah's way of stating that the argument is complete, and that the choice is the Makkans', and that the sūrah has done its part.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:15

> **Whosoever desires the life of this world and its adornment, We shall pay them in full for their deeds therein, and therein they will not be deprived.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Whosoever Desires" — The Universal That Includes the Believer**

The sūrah, having closed its address to the Makkans with the question of submission, now opens a new section, and the section begins with a universal that is wider than the Makkans. The Arabic *man yurid ẓ-ḥayāta-d-dunyā wa-biʿḍahā* — "whosoever desires the life of this world and its adornment" — is not addressed to the Makkans alone; it is addressed to *everyone* who desires the world, and the *everyone* includes, as the commentators note, the believers as well as the disbelievers. The distinction is not in the desire; the distinction is in the *only*. The person who desires the world *and nothing else*, who desires the world *as the whole of the account*, is the person the verse is describing, and the description applies to the believer who has made the world the whole of his hope as much as to the disbeliever who has made it the whole of his life. The sūrah's universal is therefore a statement about the *orientation*, not about the *identity*: the person who desires the world as the whole is the person the verse addresses, regardless of whether the person is a believer or a disbeliever.

**"Its Adornment" — The Word That Names the Temptation**

The *zīnah* — "adornment" — is the word that does the work in the verse, and the word is the sūrah's name for the *quality* of the world that makes the desire for it the whole of the account. The *zīnah* is not the world itself; it is the world's *presentation*, the world's *appearance*, the world's *surface* as it is shown to the person who desires it. The commentators note that the *zīnah* is the world's power to *attract*, to *hold*, to *make the person forget* that the world is not the whole. The *zīnah* is not a lie; it is a *partial truth*, the truth of the world's beauty presented as the whole of the truth, and the presentation is the temptation. The sūrah is not saying that the world is evil; it is saying that the world's *adornment* is the thing that makes the desire for it the whole of the account, and the adornment is the sūrah's name for the mechanism of the temptation.

**"We Shall Pay Them in Full" — The Justice That the Sūrah Does Not Withhold**

The sūrah's statement about the person who desires the world as the whole is not a punishment; it is a *payment*. *La-nujazīnna-hum ʿamā kānā yashnaʿūn* — "We shall pay them in full for their deeds therein" — is the sūrah's statement of the *justice* that is owed to the person who has chosen the world as the whole. The person who has chosen the world as the whole will be *paid* for the choice, and the payment is *full*, in the sense of being complete, in the sense of being without deficit, in the sense of being the *exact* equivalent of the deed. The sūrah is not withholding the payment; it is *giving* it, and the giving is the sūrah's statement of the *honesty* of the account. The person who has chosen the world as the whole will get *exactly* what he chose, and the exactly is the sūrah's way of stating that the account is not a punishment; it is a *settlement*, and the settlement is *full*, and the fullness is the sūrah's statement of the *justice* that is owed.

**"And Therein They Will Not Be Deprived" — The Completeness That Is the Trap**

The final clause is the one that makes the verse complete, and the clause is the sūrah's statement of the *completeness* of the payment: *wa-mā fīhā yuẓlamūn* — "and therein they will not be deprived." The *ẓulm* — deprivation, injustice — is the word that names the *absence* that the payment does not have: the person who has chosen the world as the whole will not be *deprived* of the world, in the sense of being given less than he chose, in the sense of being short-changed, in the sense of being given a *partial* version of the world. The completeness is the sūrah's way of stating that the payment is *exact*, and the exactness is the sūrah's way of stating that the person who has chosen the world as the whole will get *exactly* the world, and nothing else. The *trap* is in the exactness: the person who gets exactly what he chose is the person who gets *only* what he chose, and the only is the sūrah's statement of the *limit* of the payment. The person is not deprived; he is *limited*, and the limit is the sūrah's way of stating that the world is the whole of his account, and the whole is the sūrah's way of stating that the account is *closed*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:16

> **They are those for whom there shall be naught in the Hereafter but the Fire. Whatsoever they had wrought therein shall come to naught, and vain was that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Are Those" — The Identification That Closes the Previous Verse**

The sūrah now identifies the person described in verse 15 with a specificity the previous verse's universal did not have: *ʾulāʾika lladhīna lā lahum fī-d-dār al-ākhirati illā n-nār* — "they are those for whom there shall be naught in the Hereafter but the Fire." The identification is the sūrah's way of stating that the person who desires the world as the whole of the account is not an abstract type but a person with a fate, and the fate is named. Verse 15 described what such a person *receives in this world* — the full payment, the un-deprived share; verse 16 describes what is *left* after that payment, and the remainder is the Fire. The two verses together form a complete accounting: the full share here, the Fire there, and nothing in between.

**"Naught but the Fire" — The Negation That Is the Verse's Central Claim**

The central claim is the negation: *illā n-nār* — "but the Fire." The person who has chosen the world as the whole will have, in the Hereafter, *nothing* except the Fire. The *nothing* is the sūrah's statement of the completeness of the loss: the person is not given a partial version of the Hereafter, a reduced garden, a lesser reward; he is given the whole of the Hereafter as it is for him, and the whole is the Fire. The negation is not an added punishment; it is the *absence* of everything else, and the absence is the logical consequence of the choice stated in verse 15. A share that was fully paid for in this world cannot be paid for again in the next; the account was closed on this side of the veil, and the closure is what the negation names.

**"Whatsoever They Had Wrought Shall Come to Naught" — The Annulment of the Deed**

The second clause names the state of the deeds themselves. *Yabāṭil mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "whatsoever they had wrought shall come to naught." The *baṭl* — naught, voidness — is the word the Qur'an uses for the deeds rendered worthless by the wrong intention and the wrong goal, and the commentators connect it to the famous ḥadīth recorded by Muslim (no. 1907):

> *"Verily, actions are [judged] according to [their] intentions."*

A deed whose intention was the world alone, whose goal was the adornment described in verse 15, is a deed that was, in the sūrah's logic, already directed at the wrong target. The annulment is not a later punishment inflicted on the deed; it is the deed's own state, confirmed at the settlement. The person who built his life on the world built it on a ground that does not carry into the next account, and the *baṭl* is the sūrah's name for that: the deed stands, but it stands *void*, as a house built on sand stands when the tide comes — visible, shaped, and without foundation.

**"Vain Was That Which They Used to Do" — The Judgment on the Direction**

The final clause restates the annulment as a judgment on direction. *Wa-kaṣada mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "vain was that which they used to do." The word *kaṣada* conveys the sense of a thing that has been used up, consumed, spent — the way a lamp's oil is spent. The deeds were not merely void; they were *spent*, expended on a goal that does not remain. The sūrah is saying that the effort was real, the labor was real, and the direction was the failure: the oil burned, the lamp shone, and the light did not carry. This is the verse's deepest teaching, and it is the one the Makkans, for whom the labor of the world — the trade, the status, the accumulation — was the whole of life, were being asked to hear. The verse does not say their labor was evil; it says their labor was *spent*, and the spent is the sūrah's way of stating that the effort was real and the direction was not.

**The Pair as a Complete Warning**

Verses 15 and 16 together are the sūrah's complete warning to the person who has closed his account on this side of the veil: the full payment here (the justice that is owed and not withheld), the Fire there (the remainder that is the consequence), the annulment of the deed (the state of what was done), and the spent effort (the judgment on the direction). The warning is not a threat; it is a *description*, and the description is the sūrah's way of showing the Makkans the shape of the choice before they complete it. The next verse turns to the opposite case — the one who stands on the clear proof — and the contrast between the two is the sūrah's argument.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:17

> **So what of the one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord, and for whom it is recited by a witness from Him? And before it there was the Book of Moses, a guide and a mercy. It is they who believe in it. And whosoever disbelieves in it from among the parties, the Fire is their tryst. So be not in doubt concerning it. Verily it is the truth from thy Lord, but most of mankind believe not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"So What of the One" — The Rhetorical Question That Turns the Scale**

The sūrah, having completed its description of the person who chose the world as the whole of the account, now turns to the opposite case, and it turns with a rhetorical question that does the work of a scale: *fa-mā ẓāllu lladhī yaʿtī ʿalā-burhānin min rabbihi* — "so what of the one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord?" The *fa-mā* — "so what of" — is the sūrah's way of stating that the comparison has been made and the comparison is the argument. The person described in verses 15–16 was the one who spent his account on the world; the person described here is the one who stands on the proof, and the *mā* — "what of" — is the sūrah's question to the reader: what is the state of the one who stands on the proof, in comparison to the one who spent his account on the adornment? The question is not a request for an answer; it is the sūrah's way of stating that the answer is the *opposite* of what was described, and that the opposite is the sūrah's next subject.

**"A Clear Proof" — The Word That Recurs Through the Sūrah**

The *burhān* — "clear proof" — is the word that does the work in this verse, and the word is the sūrah's name for the *kind* of evidence that the standing person has. The *burhān* is not a *sign* in the sense of a miracle; it is the *proof* in the sense of the *evidence* that is *clear*, in the sense of being *visible*, in the sense of being *available to the one who looks*. The word recurs throughout the sūrah — in Noah's speech to his people (v. 28), in Ṣāliḥ's speech (v. 63), in Shuʿayb's speech (v. 88) — and the recurrence is the sūrah's way of stating that the *burhān* is the *constant* of the prophetic message, the one thing that does not change from prophet to prophet, from people to people, from century to century. The *burhān* is the sūrah's name for the *message* itself, in the sense of being the *evidence* that the message carries, and the evidence is the sūrah's way of stating that the message is not a *claim*; it is a *proof*, and the proof is the ground on which the standing stands.

**"Recited by a Witness from Him" — The Witness That Confirms the Proof**

The second clause names the *witness*: *wa-tunzalu ʿalayhi shāhidan minhu* — "and for whom it is recited by a witness from Him." The *shāhid* — witness — is the word that the commentators have discussed at length, and the discussion is worth following. The dominant reading, which al-Ṭabarī records, identifies the witness with the Qur'an itself, in the sense that the Qur'an is the *witness* to the proof, in the sense that the recitation of the Qur'an is the *witnessing* of the truth. The alternate reading identifies the witness with the angel, in the sense that the revelation is *delivered* by the angel, and the delivery is the *witnessing*. A third reading, recorded by some commentators, identifies the witness with a specific person, and the person named in the reports is ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, who is reported to have said that the verse was revealed in connection with him. The three readings are not contradictory; they are three aspects of the same claim: the proof is *witnessed*, and the witnessing is not a *later* addition; it is the *form* of the proof, in the sense that the proof is *given* in the form of a *witnessing*, and the witnessing is the sūrah's way of stating that the proof is not a *private* experience; it is a *public* event, witnessed, recorded, and available.

**"Before It There Was the Book of Moses" — The Continuity That the Verse Establishes**

The third clause establishes the *continuity*: *wa-min qablihi kitābu Mūsā* — "and before it there was the Book of Moses." The *min qablihi* — "before it" — is the sūrah's way of stating that the Qur'an is not a *new* revelation; it is a *continuation*, in the sense of being the *later* book in a *sequence*, in the sense of being the *final* book in a *line*. The Book of Moses — the Torah — is named as the *preceding* book, and the naming is the sūrah's way of stating that the message is not *discontinuous*; it is *continuous*, and the continuity is the sūrah's way of stating that the proof is not a *single* event; it is a *line* of events, and the line is the sūrah's way of stating that the message has a *history*, and the history is the sūrah's way of stating that the proof is not a *claim* without a *precedent*. The *hādiyy wa-raḥmah* — "a guide and a mercy" — is the sūrah's description of the Torah's function, and the description is the same description the Qur'an gives of itself in other verses, and the sameness is the sūrah's way of stating that the *function* is the same, even as the *book* is different.

**"The Fire Is Their Tryst" — The Verdict That Closes the Section**

The verse closes with the verdict on the one who disbelieves in the proof: *fa-alladhīna kafarū bihi min al-ʿashīri fā-n-nār mawʿiduhum* — "and whosoever disbelieves in it from among the parties, the Fire is their tryst." The *mawʿid* — tryst, appointment — is the word that names the *state* of the disbeliever's fate: the Fire is not a *possibility*; it is an *appointment*, in the sense of being a *fixed* event, in the sense of being a *set* time, in the sense of being a *known* destination. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the disbeliever's fate is not a *question*; it is an *appointment*, and the appointment is the sūrah's way of stating that the *trial* is not a *process*; it is a *destination*, and the destination is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is not *open*; it is *closed*.

**"So Be Not in Doubt" — The Command That the Sūrah Gives to the Prophet**

The final clause is the sūrah's command to the Prophet, and the command is the sūrah's way of stating that the *doubt* is not a *possibility* for the one who stands on the proof: *fa-lā takun fī mārā minhu* — "so be not in doubt concerning it." The *mārā* — doubt — is the word that names the *state* that the sūrah is forbidding, and the forbidding is the sūrah's way of stating that the *standing* on the proof is not a *state of uncertainty*; it is a *state of certainty*, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of stating that the *proof* is not a *hypothesis*; it is a *fact*, and the fact is the sūrah's way of stating that the *doubt* is not a *virtue*; it is a *failure*, and the failure is the sūrah's way of stating that the *one who stands on the proof* is the one who *does not doubt*. The *lākinnakthira an-nāsi lā yuʾminūn* — "but most of mankind believe not" — is the sūrah's final statement, and the statement is the sūrah's way of stating that the *fact* is not a *majority* opinion; it is a *fact* regardless of the *number* of those who accept it, and the *regardless* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *truth* is not a *vote*; it is a *witnessing*, and the witnessing is the sūrah's way of stating that the *proof* stands regardless of the *response*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:18

> **And who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God? It is they will be brought before their Lord, and the witnesses will say, "These are the ones who lied against their Lord." Behold! The curse of God is upon the wrongdoers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Who Does Greater Wrong" — The Superlative That Closes the Door on Comparison**

The sūrah, having described the one who stands on the proof and the one whose tryst is the Fire, now names the *greatest* wrong, and it names it in the form of a question that is not a question: *wa-man ẓalamun dzhallim min alladhī kafā bi-Allāhi kathibā* — "and who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God?" The superlative — *min* in the comparative construction, "greater than" — is the sūrah's way of stating that the wrong has a *maximum*, and that the maximum is named, and that the naming is the sūrah's way of closing the door on the comparison. The Makkans, who had been fabricating the lie in verse 13 — the lie that the Qur'an was composed — are being told, in the verse, that their lie is not a *small* wrong; it is the *greatest* wrong, and the greatest is the sūrah's way of stating that the *scale* is closed, and that the *top* of the scale is named, and that the name is *kafā bi-Allāhi kathibā* — "to fabricate a lie against God."

**"Fabricates a Lie" — The Word That Names the Specific Sin**

The *kafā* — to fabricate, to invent, to compose falsely — is the word that does the work, and the word is the sūrah's name for the *specific* act that constitutes the greatest wrong. The *kafā* is not a *lie* in the general sense; it is a *fabrication*, in the sense of being a *composition* that is *presented* as a *revelation*, in the sense of being a *human* product that is *attributed* to the *divine* source. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the greatest wrong is not a *lie* told *to* God; it is a *lie* told *about* God, in the sense of being a *false attribution* of a *human* work to the *divine* author, in the sense of being a *corruption* of the *relation* between the *source* and the *message*. The *kathib* — the lie — is the sūrah's name for the *product* of the *kafā*, and the product is the sūrah's way of stating that the *fabrication* is not a *private* act; it is a *public* claim, and the public claim is the sūrah's way of stating that the *greatest wrong* is a *social* sin, in the sense of being a sin that *corrupts* the *community's* relation to the *source* of the *message*.

**"Brought Before Their Lord" — The Standing That the Verse Describes**

The second clause describes the *standing*: *yuḥḍharūna ilā rabbi-him* — "they will be brought before their Lord." The *yuḥḍharūna* — to be brought, to be presented — is the word used in the Qur'an for the *presentation* of the *soul* before the *Judge*, and the sūrah's use of it here is a statement about the *form* of the *account*. The *wrongdoer* is not *punished* in the *abstract*; he is *brought*, in the sense of being *presented*, in the sense of being *made to stand*, in the sense of being *placed* before the *One* whose *witnessing* was stated in verse 5. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the *account* is not a *process* that the *wrongdoer* can *manage*; it is a *standing*, and the standing is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* is not the *agent* of the *account*; he is the *object*, and the object is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is not a *debate*; it is a *presentation*, and the presentation is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* will *hear* the *verdict* before the *One* whose *witnessing* is the *ground* of the *verdict*.

**"The Witnesses Will Say" — The Witnessing That the Verse Establishes**

The third clause names the *witnesses*: *wa-yaqūlu al-shuhadāʾ* — "and the witnesses will say." The *shuhadāʾ* — witnesses — is the word that the commentators identify, on the dominant reading, with the *angels* who *record* the *deeds* of the *human beings*, and the *recording* is the *witnessing*, and the *witnessing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is not a *private* event; it is a *witnessed* event, and the *witnessing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *verdict* is not a *solitary* judgment; it is a *corroborated* judgment, and the *corroboration* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* will not be able to *dispute* the *verdict*, because the *dispute* requires a *witness* who is *not* the *accuser*, and the *witness* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is *fair*, in the sense of being *witnessed* by *one* who is *not* a *party* to the *wrong*.

**"The Curse of God Is upon the Wrongdoers" — The Verdict That the Verse States**

The final clause is the *verdict*: *wa-laʿn Allāhi ʿalā-ẓ-ẓālimīn* — "and the curse of God is upon the wrongdoers." The *laʿn* — curse — is the word that the commentators define as *baʿd* — distance, exile — from the *Mercy*, and the definition is the sūrah's way of stating that the *curse* is not an *addition* of *harm*; it is a *removal* of *good*, in the sense of being a *separation* from the *source* of the *good*. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the *wrongdoer* is not *struck*; he is *separated*, and the separation is the sūrah's way of stating that the *consequence* of the *greatest wrong* is not a *punishment* in the *sense of an addition*; it is a *state* in the *sense of an absence*, and the absence is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* is *left* without the *Mercy*, and the leaving is the sūrah's way of stating that the *verdict* is not a *threat*; it is a *description*, and the description is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* has *chosen* the *state*, and the choosing is the sūrah's way of stating that the *verdict* is the *consequence* of the *choice*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:19

> **those who turn from the way of God and seek to make it crooked, and who disbelieve in the Hereafter.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appositive That Defines the Wrongdoers**

The verse is, in the sūrah's structure, an appositive: it does not begin a new thought but completes and defines the "wrongdoers" named in the curse of verse 18. The Arabic runs *alladhīna yavillūna ʿan sabīli Allāhi wa-yaḥbinūhā wa-hum bi-l-ākhirati hüm kāfirūn* — "those who turn from the way of God and seek to make it crooked, and who disbelievers in the Hereafter." The definition is given in three marks, and the three marks together form the sūrah's complete portrait of the one who incurs the curse. The portrait is not a description of a *type* of person in the abstract; it is a description of a *posture*, and the posture is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrong* is not a *single act*; it is a *stance* that is *held* over *time*, and the stance is the sūrah's way of stating that the *verdict* of verse 18 is not a *reaction* to a *moment*; it is a *consequence* of a *stance*.

**"Turn from the Way" — The First Mark: The Refusal**

The first mark is *yavillūna ʿan sabīli Allāh* — "they turn from the way of God." The *sabīl* — way, path — is the word the Qur'an uses for the *straight path* of the Fātiḥah (1:6), and the sūrah's use of it here is a deliberate echo: the *sabīl* is the *same* way, and the *turning* is the *same* turning that the Fātiḥah asks God to guard against. The *ʿan* — "from" — is the word that names the *direction* of the *turning*: the person does not *stand beside* the *way*; he *turns away* from it, in the sense of being *on* the *way* and *leaving* it, in the sense of having *known* the *way* and *refusing* it. The sūrah is stating, in the first mark, that the *wrong* is not *ignorance*; it is *refusal*, and the refusal is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* is not *unaware*; he is *aware* and *turning*, and the turning is the sūrah's way of stating that the *knowledge* of the *way* is not *sufficient*; the *following* is the *condition*, and the *refusal* is the *failure*.

**"Seek to Make It Crooked" — The Second Mark: The Distortion**

The second mark is the one that goes beyond the *refusal*: *wa-yaḥbinūhā* — "and they seek to make it crooked." The *ḥabṭ* — to make crooked, to distort — is the word that names the *active* attempt to *corrupt* the *way* for *others*, in the sense of *bending* the *straight* into the *curved*, in the sense of *presenting* the *distorted* as the *original*. The sūrah is stating, in the second mark, that the *wrongdoer* is not merely *absent* from the *way*; he is *working* on the *way*, in the sense of *altering* it, in the sense of *misrepresenting* it, in the sense of *leading* others *away* from it *through* the *misrepresentation*. The *crooked* is the sūrah's name for the *substitute* that the *wrongdoer* offers in place of the *straight*, and the *substitute* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrong* is not a *passive* state; it is an *active* enterprise, and the *enterprise* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* is not a *victim* of the *way*; he is an *agent* against it.

**"And Who Disbelieve in the Hereafter" — The Third Mark: The Denial of the Account**

The third mark is the *denial*: *wa-hum bi-l-ākhirati hüm kāfirūn* — "and who disbelieve in the Hereafter." The *ākhirah* — the Hereafter, the Last — is the *realm* of the *account*, and the *disbelief* in it is the sūrah's way of stating that the *wrongdoer* has *closed* the *account* on *his own* terms, in the sense of *denying* the *reality* of the *settlement*, in the sense of *living* as if the *term appointed* of verse 3 were *not* a *term*, in the sense of *treating* the *world* as the *whole* of the *account*. The three marks together form the *complete* posture: the *refusal* of the *way*, the *distortion* of the *way*, and the *denial* of the *account* that the *way* leads to. The sūrah is stating, in the verse, that the *curse* of verse 18 falls on the *posture* as a *whole*, and that the *posture* is the *stance* of the *one* who *knows* the *way*, *turns* from it, *bends* it, and *denies* the *end* to which it *leads*.

**The Verse's Function — Completing the Portrait Before the Stories Begin**

The verse is the last of the opening section's definitions, and its function is to *complete* the *portrait* of the *one* the sūrah has been *addressing* since verse 2. The *Makkans*, in the sūrah's *analysis*, are the *one* who *turns* from the *way*, *seeks* to *make* it *crooked* (in the *sense of offering* the *idols* as a *substitute* for the *straight*), and *disbelieves* in the *Hereafter* (in the *sense of* verse 7's *rejection* of the *resurrection*). The *sūrah* is *telling* them, in the *verse*, that the *portrait* is *complete*, and that the *verdict* of verse 18 *applies*, and that the *stories* that *follow* are the *demonstration* of the *verdict* in *history*. The *opening section* is *closed*; the *narrative middle* is *about to begin*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:20

> **Such as these cannot thwart [aught] on earth, and they have no protector apart from God. For them the punishment will be multiplied. They were not able to hear; neither did they see.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Cannot Thwart [Aught] on Earth"**

The verse opens with a statement of incapacity: *ulāʾika lam yakūnū muʿjizīna fī l-arḍ* — "such as these cannot thwart [aught] on earth." The commentators explain that this means the disbelievers will not be able to prevent God's punishing them in this life if He wishes to do so.

Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss is the operative one. The thwarting denied is not abstract resistance but the specific ability to escape punishment in this world. The commentators supply the parallels: 8:59, 9:2–3, 24:57, 29:22, 35:44, 42:31, 46:32, and 72:12.

The phrase *fī l-arḍ* — "on earth" — is what the commentators emphasize. The incapacity is located in this world, not in the hereafter. The disbelievers cannot flee, cannot hide, cannot arrange their circumstances so that the punishment misses them.

This connects to the sūrah's narratives. Every community that rejected its messenger was destroyed within the world, and the verse generalizes the pattern into a rule.

**"No Protector Apart from God"**

The verse continues: *wa-mā kāna lahum min dūni Llāhi min awliyāʾ* — "and they have no protector apart from God." The commentators note that God's being the only Protector for human beings is a common Qur'anic refrain.

The parallels are extensive: 2:107, 2:120, 4:123, 4:173, 9:74, 9:116, 29:22, 33:18, 33:65, 42:31, and 48:22. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing a fixed theological claim rather than a contextual remark.

The word *awlīyāʾ* is the plural of *walī* — protector, ally, one who stands near and acts on another's behalf. The commentators note that the negation is comprehensive: there are none besides God.

The connection to the preceding clause is close. Those who cannot thwart punishment on earth also have no one to interpose. The two statements describe the same isolation from different angles — no escape and no advocate.

The verse does not say the disbelievers have no protectors at all. It says they have none apart from God, which the commentators read as indicating that what they relied upon in place of God was never protection.

**"The Punishment Will Be Multiplied"**

The verse states a doubling: *yuḍāʿafu lahumu l-ʿadhāb* — "for them the punishment will be multiplied." The commentators explain the ground of the multiplication.

Al-Jawzī's gloss is specific: they will be punished for their own misguided ways and also for misguiding others. The commentators supply the supporting passages: 7:38–39c, 17:75, 29:12–13, and 33:66–68.

The principle is that leading others astray adds to the reckoning rather than distributing it. The commentators connect this to 29:12–13, where those who invite others to error bear their own burdens and burdens along with their own.

The verse's context matters. It addresses the notables — those with standing who shaped their community's response. Their influence, which they treated as an asset, is what multiplies the punishment.

The commentators note the contrast with the sūrah's treatment of the ordinary followers. In several narratives the leaders and the followers dispute in the Fire, and the leaders bear the greater share. The verse states the principle behind that pattern.

**"They Were Not Able to Hear; Neither Did They See"**

The verse closes with a diagnosis: *mā kānū yastaṭīʿūna s-samʿa wa-mā kānū yubṣirūn* — "they were not able to hear; neither did they see." The commentators note that the incapacity is stated as inability rather than refusal.

Al-Rāzī's gloss is central: these words refer to a "deafness of heart and blindness of soul." The commentators connect this to the Qur'an's consistent characterization of those insensible to the prophetic messages as blind and deaf.

The supporting passages are numerous: 2:171, 5:71, 6:104, 7:64, 10:43, 11:28, 13:19, 27:4, 27:66, 27:81, 30:53, 41:17, 41:44, and 43:36–40. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing that the terms are technical rather than physical.

The verb *yastaṭīʿūna* — "were able" — is worth noting. The commentators read the inability as acquired rather than innate. The disbelievers were not born deaf; they became unable to hear through the course of their refusal.

This connects to the multiplication of punishment. The notables' influence shaped their community's sensibility, and the verse describes the result as a shared incapacity. The deafness is both the cause of the rejection and its consequence.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 20 concludes the description of those who desire the life of this world, begun at v. 15. The commentators read the sequence as moving from the desire, to its reward in this world, to its consequence in the next.

The four clauses of the verse work together. No escape on earth; no protector; doubled punishment; and the incapacity that made the whole course possible. The commentators read the final clause as explaining the first three.

The verse also prepares the transition that follows. Having described those who desired this world, the sūrah turns at v. 17 to those who stand upon a clear sign from their Lord. The contrast is between those who cannot hear and those who have something to hear.

The pastoral edge is worth noting. The incapacity described is presented as a condition, and conditions can change. The commentators read the verse as a warning addressed to those who can still hear it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:21

> **They are those who have lost their souls, and that which they used to fabricate has forsaken them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Are Those Who Have Lost Their Souls" — The Loss That Is the Verse's Central Image**

The sūrah now names the *state* of the *wrongdoer* in a single *image* that is one of the *sharpest* in the *Qur'an*: *ʾulāʾika lladhīna ḍalla anfasuhum* — "they are those who have lost their souls." The *ḍalāl* — loss, being astray — is applied here not to a *person* in the *general* sense but to the *self*, and the *application* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *loss* is *not* a *loss of something outside the self*; it is a *loss of the self*, in the *sense of being* a *self* that has *misplaced itself*, in the *sense of being* a *self* that has *lost its bearing*, in the *sense of being* a *self* that has *gone astray in its own account*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *clause*, that the *wrongdoer* is *not* a *person* who has *lost a possession*; he is a *person* who has *lost himself*, and the *loss of self* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *posture* of the previous verses has *cost* the *wrongdoer* the *one thing* he *cannot* *recover*: the *self* that was *aligned* with the *way*.

**The Self That Is Lost — and What the Loss Means**

The *nafs* — soul, self — is the word the Qur'an uses for the *person* in the *sense of being* the *agent* of the *deed*, the *one* who *acts* and is *accountable* for the *act*, and the *sūrah's* use of the *word* here, in the *passive* of *ḍalla*, is a *statement* about the *self's* *state*: the *self* is *not* being *lost by an external agent*; it is *lost in the sense of being astray*, in the *sense of having taken the wrong path and arrived at the wrong place*. The *sūrah* is *stating* that the *loss* is the *consequence* of the *posture*: the *refusal* of the *way* (v. 19), the *distortion* of the *way* (v. 19), the *denial* of the *account* (v. 19) — the *three marks* together *produce* the *loss of self*, in the *sense of being* a *self* that has *oriented itself* against the *only orientation* that *preserves* it. The *image* is *economic* in its *logic*: the *wrongdoer* has *spent* the *self* on the *wrong* object, in the *same* way the *person* of verse 15 *spent* the *account* on the *world*, and the *spent* is the *sūrah's* word for the *state* of the *self* after the *spending*.

**"That Which They Used to Fabricate Has Forsaken Them" — The Abandonment of the False**

The second clause is the *sūrah's* most *economical* statement of the *fate* of the *false*: *wa-ṭaraka-humu mā kanū yaḍfabūn* — "and that which they used to fabricate has forsaken them." The *araka* — to forsake, to abandon, to leave behind — is the *word* that does the *work*, and the *word* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *false* does not *accompany* the *wrongdoer* into the *account*. The *idols*, the *fabricated* *gods*, the *invented* *justifications*, the *composed* *lies* — the *entire* *structure* of the *fabrication* — is *left behind*, in the *sense of being* *abandoned*, in the *sense of being* *unable to follow*, in the *sense of being* *without power* to *intervene*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *clause*, that the *fabrication* was *useful* only in the *world* of the *fabrication*, and that it *ceases to be* at the *threshold* of the *account*, and the *ceasing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wrongdoer* arrives at the *account* with *nothing* but the *self* that was *lost* in verse 21's first clause. The *two clauses together* form the *sūrah's* complete *portrait* of the *arrival*: the *self* is *lost*, and the *false* is *gone*, and the *arrival* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wrongdoer* is *alone* at the *threshold*, in the *solitude* named in verse 20.

**The Pattern — Fabrication, Then Forsaking**

The *sūrah* is *stating* a *pattern* that the *Qur'an* repeats in several places (6:24, 6:54; 10:30; 16:87; 28:75): whatever a *person* *fabricates* as a *protection* will *forsake* him at the *moment* of *need*. The *pattern* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *fabrication* is *not* a *protection*; it is a *delay*, in the *sense of being* a *structure* that *holds* in the *world* but *fails* at the *threshold*, in the *sense of being* a *house* that *stands* in the *season* but *does not stand* at the *flood*. The *Makkans*, who were *building* their *lives* on the *idols*, were *building* a *house* that the *sūrah* is *telling* them *will not stand* at the *flood*, and the *flood* is the *sūrah's* name for the *account*, and the *not standing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *building* was *never* a *protection*; it was a *performance* of *protection*, and the *performance* ends at the *threshold*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:22

> **There is no doubt that in the Hereafter they shall be the greatest losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"There Is No Doubt" — The Certainty With Which the Sūrah Closes the Argument**

The sūrah closes its argument on the wrongdoers with a verse of bare, unadorned certainty: *laya-qīnna la-hum fī-d-dār al-ākhirati khaṣṣa* — "there is no doubt that in the Hereafter they shall be the greatest losers." The *la-qayna* — "there is no doubt" — is the particle of certainty, and the sūrah's use of it here is not emphasis added to a claim; it is the sūrah's way of stating that the claim it has just made — the loss of the self (v. 21), the forsaking of the false (v. 21), the multiplied punishment (v. 20), the solitude at the threshold (v. 20) — is not a hypothesis, not a possibility, not a warning that might be averted by a change of posture that has not yet come. The certainty is the sūrah's seal on the argument: what has been described is what will be, and the *la-qayna* is the word that removes the last degree of conditional from the statement. The Makkans had been living in the conditionals — *if* the punishment comes, *if* the resurrection is real, *if* the warning is true — and the sūrah answers the conditionals with a *la-qayna*: there is no *if* left in the sentence.

**"The Greatest Losers" — The Superlative That Completes the Scale**

The verdict is given in the superlative: *khaṣṣa* — "losers," in the sense of the ones who have sustained the loss, and the sūrah's statement that they are *the greatest* of them is the completion of the scale the sūrah opened in verse 18, where the wrong was named as the greatest wrong. The scale now has both ends named: the greatest wrong at one end, and the greatest loss at the other, and the two ends are joined by the sūrah's argument. The loss is not a loss in the sense of a lesser share; it is the loss in the sense of the total expenditure without remainder, the loss that verse 16 described for the one who spent the account on the world, now applied to the one who spent the self on the fabrication. The *greatest* is the sūrah's way of stating that the loss is not proportional to the degree of the wrong; it is *complete*, because the object of the expenditure — the self, the whole of the person's account — was the whole of what the person had, and the whole, spent on the wrong object, leaves no remainder to carry into the settlement.

**The Loss That the Makkans Could Not See — and Why the Sūrah Names It**

The sūrah's deepest move in this verse is to name the loss *in the Hereafter*, and to name it as the *greatest* loss, while the Makkans were living in the present tense of their prosperity. The verse is the sūrah's way of stating that the loss is not yet visible, and that the not-yet-visible is the condition of the trial: the person who could see the loss in advance would not be making the expenditure, and the person who is making the expenditure is the person who cannot see it. The sūrah is not taunting; it is describing the structure of the trial as it actually is: the loss is real, it is certain, it is the greatest of losses, and it is *invisible while it is being incurred*. This is the condition that the sūrah has been building throughout the opening section — the folded breast that is seen by God while it is folded (v. 5), the mockery that will beset (v. 8), the boastfulness that prepares the despondency (v. 10) — and verse 22 is the sum: the greatest of losses, certain, in the Hereafter, and incurred by a people who are, at this moment, counting their goods.

**The Verse as the Threshold Between the Two Movements**

Structurally, verse 22 is the threshold. The opening section — the call, the warnings, the answers to the Makkans' objections, the portrait of the wrongdoers, the verdict of loss — ends here. The next verse (23) turns to the opposite group, the believers who are humble before their Lord, and the sūrah's great narrative middle begins two verses after that, with the sending of Noah. Verse 22 is the last word of the first movement, and it is a word of *certainty*, and the sūrah's logic is that the certainty must be stated *before* the stories, because the stories are the *demonstration* of the certainty, and a demonstration requires a claim to demonstrate. The claim is: the greatest of losers, in the Hereafter, without doubt. The demonstration begins with the next verse's opposite — the inhabitants of the Garden — and then, in verse 25, with the first of the seven stories.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:23

> **Truly those who believe and perform righteous deeds and are humble before their Lord, it is they who are the inhabitants of the Garden; they shall abide therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Immediate Reversal — and Why the Sūrah Gives It Without Delay**

The sūrah has just sealed its argument on the wrongdoers with the certainty of the greatest loss, and it does not pause, does not transition, does not soften: the very next verse turns to the opposite group and names their state. *Inna lladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti wa-khaṣaʿū li-rabbihim ʾulāʾika aḥlū-l-jannati halūna fīhā* — "truly those who believe and perform righteous deeds and are humble before their Lord, it is they who are the inhabitants of the Garden; they shall abide therein." The immediacy of the turn is the sūrah's way of stating that the two groups are not two separate topics; they are the two sides of one statement, and the statement is the sūrah's complete map of the human destination. The Makkans, who had just been told the verdict on their posture, are now being told, in the same breath, the verdict on the opposite posture, and the two verdicts are the sūrah's way of stating that the choice is not between *punishment* and *nothing*; it is between the *loss* and the *Garden*, and the *Garden* is named in the same certainty that the loss was named.

**Belief, Deed, and Humility — The Three Marks of the Inhabitants**

The inhabitants of the Garden are defined by three marks, and the three marks are the mirror image, mark for mark, of the three marks of the wrongdoers in verse 19. Where the wrongdoer *turned from* the way, the inhabitant *believes* — *āmanū*, from the root *ʾ-m-n*, the root of *aman* (security) and *iman* (faith): the belief is not an opinion adopted; it is a *settling into security*, a coming to rest in the truth in the way a traveler comes to rest in a safe house. Where the wrongdoer *distorted* the way, the inhabitant *performs righteous deeds* — *ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt*: the deed is the way walked, the straight path made real in the body's movements, the belief expressed in the hand's work. And where the wrongdoer *disbelieved in the Hereafter* in the posture of one who has nothing to answer for, the inhabitant is *humble before his Lord* — *khaḣaʿū li-rabbihim*.

**"Humble Before Their Lord" — The Third Mark, and the Word That Means It**

The third mark is the one that completes the definition, and it is the one the commentators have discussed most. *Khaḣa* — from the root *kh-ḣ-aʿ*, to lower oneself, to bend, to submit — is the word used in the Qur'an for the lowliness of the one who knows his place before the One who is above him, and the sūrah's use of it here, applied to the *inhabitants of the Garden*, is a statement about the *character* of the one who enters the Garden: he enters *humble*, in the sense of being *bent* before the Lord, in the sense of being *lowered* in his own estimation, in the sense of *knowing his place*. The commentators add the dimension that the *khaḣaʿ* also describes the state of *peace in the remembrance of God* — the heart that is at rest, the heart that has been *lowered* by the knowledge and has *come to rest* in the lowering, as in 13:28:

> *"Those who believe and whose hearts are at rest in the remembrance of God. Verily, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."*

The humility is therefore not a *preparation* for the Garden in the sense of a task to be completed before entry; it is the *state* of the inhabitant, the *posture* in which the Garden is *lived*, and the sūrah's naming of it as one of the three *marks* is its way of stating that the Garden is not a *reward for humility*; it is the *home of the humble*, and the *humble* are the ones for whom the home is *home*.

**"They Shall Abide in It" — The Permanence That Closes the Verse**

The verse closes with the permanence: *khālidīna fīhā* — "they shall abide therein." The *khalūd* — abiding, permanence — is the word that answers, in the sūrah's logic, the *ajal maʿdūd* — the appointed term — of verse 3. The goodly enjoyment of this world is for a term; the abiding in the Garden is for the *no-term*, the *beyond-term*, the *permanence* that the *term* was a *figure* for. The sūrah is stating, in the closing phrase, that the *difference* between the two destinations is not a difference of *degree*; it is a difference of *kind*: one is *term*, and one is *abiding*, and the *term* is the *shadow* of the *abiding*, and the *abiding* is the *substance*. The *Makkans*, who were measuring their goods by *permanence*, are being told, in the verse, that *permanence* is not a property of the world; it is a property of the *Garden*, and the *Garden* is for the *one* who *believes*, *does*, and is *humble*, and the *three marks* are the *sūrah's* complete answer to the *question* of verse 14: *so are you submits?*

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:24

> **The parable of these two groups is that of one blind and deaf, and one hearing and seeing. Are they equal when compared? Will you not remember?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Parable of These Two Groups" — The Comparison That the Sūrah Sets**

The sūrah closes the opening section with a parable, and the parable is the sūrah's way of *summarizing* the two portraits it has just given: the *wrongdoers* of verses 18–22, and the *inhabitants of the Garden* of verse 23. *Mathal hūlāʾi wa-ullāʾi* — "the parable of these and those" — is the sūrah's way of *setting* the *comparison* in the *form* of a *mathal*, a *parable*, in the *sense of being* a *concrete image* that *carries* the *abstract* *difference*. The *sūrah* is *stating* that the *difference* between the *two groups* is not a *subtle* *difference* that *requires* *long* *argument*; it is a *difference* that can be *carried* in a *single image*, and the *image* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *argument* of the *opening section* is *complete* in the *sense of being* *reducible* to a *parable*, and the *reducibility* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *difference* is *not* a *matter of degree*; it is a *matter of kind*.

**"One Blind and Deaf" — The Image of the First Group**

The first half of the parable names the state of the wrongdoers: *akhiʿhun wa-aṣmun* — "one blind and deaf." The *blindness* and the *deafness* are the *image* for the *spiritual* *condition* that verse 20 *described* in the *language* of *inability*: the *wrongdoer* is *not* *physically* *blind* and *not* *physically* *deaf*; he is *spiritually* *blind* and *spiritually* *deaf*, in the *sense of being* *unable to receive the signs* through the *eye of the understanding*, in the sense of being *unable to receive the word* through the *ear of the heart*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *image*, that the *wrongdoer's* *condition* is the *condition* of the *one who has closed the senses by choice*, and the *closedness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *world* to the *wrongdoer* is *not* *absent*; it is *present* but *unreceived*, in the *sense of being* a *light* that *falls* on the *closed eye*, in the *sense of being* a *voice* that *reaches* the *stopped ear*. The *parable* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wrongdoer* is *not* in *darkness* because the *light* is *absent*; he is in *darkness* because the *eye* is *closed*, and the *closedness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *darkness* is a *state*, not a *condition*, and the *state* is *chosen*, not *imposed*.

**"One Hearing and Seeing" — The Image of the Second Group**

The second half of the parable names the state of the inhabitants of the Garden: *samīʿun baṣīr* — "one hearing and seeing." The *hearing* and the *seeing* are the *image* for the *spiritual* *condition* of the *one* who *believes*, *does*, and is *humble*: the *believer* is *not* in a *state of receiving* that is *passive*; he is in a *state of receiving* that is *active*, in the *sense of being* a *receiving* that *opens*, in the *sense of being* a *receiving* that *responds*, in the *sense of being* a *receiving* that *transforms*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *image*, that the *believer's* *condition* is the *condition* of the *one* whose *senses* are *open*, and the *openness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *world* to the *believer* is the *same* *world* that is to the *wrongdoer*, but *received*, in the *sense of being* *perceived*, in the *sense of being* *answered*, in the *sense of being* *transformed* by the *receiving*. The *parable* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *difference* between the *two groups* is *not* a *difference of world*; it is a *difference of reception*, and the *reception* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *same world* is *lived* in *two ways*, and the *two ways* are the *sūrah's* complete *map* of the *human condition*.

**"Are They Equal When Compared?" — The Question That Is the Answer**

The sūrah poses the question: *afaya-stdīlūna* — "are they equal when compared?" The *question* is *rhetorical*, in the *sense of being* a *question* that *carries* its *own answer*, and the *answer* is *no*, in the *sense of being* *obvious*, in the *sense of being *self-evident*, in the *sense of being* the *kind* of *question* whose *answer* is *the point*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *question*, that the *comparison* is *not* a *question* that *requires* *argument*; it is a *question* that *requires* *seeing*, and the *seeing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *parable* is *complete* in the *sense of being* *visible*, and the *visibility* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *reader* who *cannot* *see* the *difference* in the *parable* is the *reader* who is *in* the *parable*, in the *sense of being* the *blind and deaf* one, and the *being in* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *parable* is *addressed* to the *Makkans* in the *form* of a *mirror*.

**"Will You Not Remember?" — The Final Address of the Opening Section**

The sūrah closes with the *address*: *afalā taʿqilūn* — "will you not remember?" The *taʿqil* — remembrance, mind, reflection — is the *word* that the *Qur'an* uses for the *act* of *bringing the mind to bear*, and the *sūrah's* use of it here is a *statement* about the *reader's* *capacity*: the *reader* *can* *remember*, in the *sense of being* *able* to *reflect*, in the *sense of being* *able* to *see the parable*, in the *sense of being* *able* to *answer the question*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing address*, that the *opening section* is *complete*, and that the *reader* has *been given* the *parable*, the *question*, and the *capacity to answer*, and that the *sūrah* has *done its part*. The *narrative middle* begins in the *next verse*, and the *stories* that *follow* are the *parable made history*, in the *sense of being* the *blind and deaf* and the *hearing and seeing* *played out* in *seven nations* across *seven centuries*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:25

> **Indeed, We sent Noah unto his people, "Truly I am unto you a clear warner,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Indeed, We Sent" — The Sūrah's Shift from Argument to History**

The sūrah's opening section has ended with the parable, and the sūrah now shifts, without transition, into its great narrative middle. The shift is marked by a single verb: *wa-laqad arsalnā* — "and indeed, We sent." The *la-qad* — the emphatic particle — is the sūrah's way of stating that the sending is not a *report* in the sense of a *hearsay*; it is a *fact* in the sense of being a *divine act*, and the *act* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *story* is not a *parable* made *history*; it is a *history* that is the *parable's* *proof*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *first word* of the *narrative middle*, that the *stories* are *real*, in the *sense of being* *sent*, in the *sense of being* *addressed*, in the *sense of being *happened*, and the *reality* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* who *reject* the *warning* are *rejecting* the *same* *warning* that *Noah's* *people* *rejected*, and the *sameness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *story* is *not* a *distance*; it is a *mirror*.

**Noah — The First of the Seven**

The sūrah's gallery of seven prophets opens with Noah, and the choice is deliberate. Noah is, in the Qur'an's own ordering, the first prophet sent to a people who had turned to idolatry after the original community of Adam (as 2:32 and the commentators' accounts state), and the sūrah's opening its narrative middle with him is its way of stating that the pattern it is about to demonstrate — the warning, the refusal, the punishment, the salvation of the few — is the *original* pattern, the pattern from which all the others are repetitions. Noah's story is the sūrah's foundation, and the foundation is the sūrah's way of stating that the Makkans' situation is not a *new* situation; it is the *oldest* situation, the situation that has been *played out* since the first turning, and the *playout* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *Makkans* are not *pioneers*; they are *rehearsers*, and the *rehearsal* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *script* is *known*.

**"A Clear Warner" — The Self-Description That the Sūrah Will Repeat**

Noah's first word to his people is the self-description: *innī lakum naẓīr bayyin* — "truly I am unto you a clear warner." The *bayyin* — clear, evident — is the word that the sūrah has already used in verse 17 for the *burhān* — the clear proof — and the recurrence is the sūrah's way of stating that the *warner's* *clarity* is the *same* as the *proof's* *clarity*: the *warning* is *clear*, in the *sense of being* *evident*, in the *sense of being* *available*, in the *sense of being* *without concealment*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *word*, that the *warning* is *not* *hidden*; it is *plain*, and the *plainness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *refusal* that *follows* is *not* a *refusal* of the *obscure*; it is a *refusal* of the *plain*, and the *refusal of the plain* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wrong* is *not* *ignorance*; it is *rejection*, and the *rejection* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *verdict* is *just*. The *same* *self-description* will be *given* to the *other prophets* in the *sūrah* — Ṣāliḥ (v. 63), Shuʿayb (v. 88) — and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *office* is *one*, and the *clarity* is *one*, and the *refusal* is *one*, across the *seven stories*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:26

> **that you might worship none but God. Truly I fear for you the punishment of a painful day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call — and Its Identity With the Sūrah's Own First Command**

Noah's message to his people, in the sūrah's telling, is a single command: *udʿū ilā Allāhi* — "worship none but God." The command is *identical* to the command with which the sūrah opened in verse 2, and the identity is the sūrah's way of stating that the *message* has *not* *changed* in *five* *thousand years*. The *Makkans* were *being* *addressed* by the *Prophet* with the *same* *command* that *Noah* *addressed* his *people* with, and the *sameness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *cultural* *product*; it is a *divine* *constant*, and the *constant* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *refusal* is *not* a *refusal* of a *foreign* *demand*; it is a *refusal* of the *same* *demand* that *has* *been* *made* to *every* *people* *since* the *first* *turning*. The *sūrah* is *placing* *Noah's* *people* and the *Makkans* in the *same* *room*, in the *same* *moment*, *hearing* the *same* *words*, and the *placement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *distance* between the *two* is *not* a *distance* of *time*; it is a *distance* of *response*.

**"Worship None but God" — The Command as the Whole of the Message**

The *command* is *given* in the *form* of a *negative* *before* the *positive*: *lā tudʿū* — "call upon none" — *min dūni Allāh* — "besides God." The *form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *primary* *task* is *removal*, in the *sense of being* the *removal* of the *false* *before* the *establishment* of the *true*, in the *sense of being* the *clearing* of the *altar* *before* the *offering*. The *commentators* note that the *command* is *addressed* to a *people* who *were not* *without* *religion*; they *had* *gods*, in the *sense of having* *objects* of *worship*, in the *sense of having* *structures* of *devotion*, in the *sense of having* a *religion* that *was* *not* the *religion*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *form* of the *command*, that the *task* is *not* the *addition* of a *new* *object*; it is the *removal* of the *old* *objects*, and the *removal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *conversion* is *not* an *accumulation*; it is a *subtraction*, and the *subtraction* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *task* — the *task* the *sūrah* *opened* with in verse 2 — is the *same* *subtraction*, and the *subtraction* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *idols* must *go* *before* anything *else* can *be*.

**"I Fear for You" — The Warner's Feeling, Named**

The second half of the verse names the warner's feeling: *innī aḫāfu ʿalaykum ʿadhāba yaumin alīm* — "truly I fear for you the punishment of a painful day." The *khawf* — fear — is the word that the sūrah uses for the *warner's* *relation* to his *people's* *fate*, and the *use* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warner* is *not* *indifferent*; he is *fearing*, in the *sense of being* *anxious*, in the *sense of being* *grieved*, in the *sense of being* *the one who loves the people and sees their fate*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *word*, that the *warning* is *not* a *professional* *act*; it is a *relational* *act*, and the *relation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warner* *bears* the *weight* of the *warning* in his *own* *person*, and the *bearing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *constrained breast* of verse 12 is the *same* *breast* that *Noah's* *breast* *was*, and the *sameness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *consolation* of the *narrative middle* is *real*: the *Prophet* is *not* *alone* in the *fearing*; he is *in the company of Noah*, and the *company* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *burden* is *shared* across *time*.

**"A Painful Day" — The Warning's Object, and the Day That Will Come**

The *object* of the *fear* is *ʿadhāb yaumin alīm* — "the punishment of a painful day." The *alīm* — painful, keen, smarting — is the word that names the *quality* of the *day's* *punishment*, and the *quality* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *punishment* is *not* a *general* *event*; it is a *specific* *quality* of *pain*, in the *sense of being* *keen*, in the *sense of being* *penetrating*, in the *sense of being* *the kind of pain that is felt in the whole person*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *word*, that the *warning* is *not* a *vague* *threat*; it is a *specific* *description*, and the *description* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warner* is *not* *hedging*; he is *naming*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *not* *being* *addressed* with a *formula*; they are *being* *addressed* with a *description*, and the *description* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warning* is *complete* in the *sense of being* *specific*, and the *specificity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *refusal* is *not* a *refusal* of the *vague*; it is a *refusal* of the *specific*, and the *refusal of the specific* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *not* *open*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:27

> **But the notables among his people who disbelieved said, "We see you as naught but a human being like us, and we see none who follow you, save the lowliest among us, as is clear to see. Nor do we see that you have any merit over us; nay, we think that you are liars."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Notables Among His People" — The Responders, Named**

The sūrah now gives the response to Noah's call, and it names the responders with a specificity that is the sūrah's way of stating that the refusal is not a random act; it is a *structured* act, performed by the *structured* part of society. *Allāhāʾ* — the notables, the elites, the leaders — are the ones who speak, and the naming is the sūrah's way of stating that the *refusal of the message* is, in the first instance, a *refusal by the power-holders*, and the *refusal by the power-holders* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *message* is a *threat to the structure*, and the *threat to the structure* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *conversion* that the sūrah describes in verse 26 — the subtraction of the idols — is a *conversion that threatens the order in which the notables hold their position*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *notables* are not refusing because the message is *false*; they are refusing because the message is *true*, and the *truth* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *notables'* *position* is a *position that the truth does not preserve*.

**"You Are Naught but a Human Being Like Us" — The First Objection, and Its Logic**

The first objection is the one that recurs in every one of the sūrah's stories, and the sūrah's repetition of it is its way of stating that the objection is *invariant*: *innāka ʾilla basharun miʾthānā* — "you are naught but a human being like us." The *logic* of the objection is worth stating clearly, because the sūrah does not let it pass. The notables are not saying "you are a human being" as a neutral fact; they are saying "you are a human being *like us*" as a *disqualification*. The *disqualification* works on the premise that the *message* requires a *source* above the *human*, and that a *human* *messenger* is *evidence against* the *message*, in the sense of being *proof* that the *message* is *human*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recurrence* of the *objection*, that the *objection* is *not* an *argument*; it is a *refusal to engage with the argument*, in the sense of being a *refusal to consider the possibility* that the *human* is the *channel*, in the sense of being a *refusal to accept* that the *source* can *speak through* the *human*. The *objection* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables'* *epistemology* is *closed*: they *cannot* *conceive* of a *divine* *message* *through* a *human* *messenger*, and the *inability* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *refusal* is *not* a *failure of evidence*; it is a *failure of conception*, and the *failure of conception* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *burhān* of verse 25 is *clear* to the *one* who *can conceive*, and *not* to the *one* who *cannot*.

**"None Follow You Save the Lowliest" — The Second Objection, and Its Social Logic**

The second objection is the one that reveals the *social* *logic* of the *refusal*: *wa-narā alladhī yaṭīʿuka illā al-ashaṭāʾ* — "we see none who follow you, save the lowliest among us." The *ashaṭāʾ* — the lowliest, the meanest — are the *poor*, the *traders* of *low* *status*, the *people* without *position* in the *structure* that the *notables* *hold*. The *objection* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables'* *test* for the *truth* of the *message* is *not* the *content* of the *message*; it is the *social composition of its followers*. The *notables* are *saying*, in effect: *if the message were true, the people of status would follow it first; the fact that only the lowliest follow it is proof that it is false*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording* of the *objection*, that the *notables'* *epistemology* is *social*, in the sense of being *based on the status of the followers rather than the content of the message*, and the *social epistemology* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables* are *not* *refusing* the *message*; they are *refusing the class of people who believe it*, and the *refusal of the class* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables'* *commitment* is *not* to the *truth*; it is to the *order*, and the *order* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *idols* are *not* *gods* to the *notables*; they are *institutions*, and the *institutions* are the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *conversion* threatens the *institution*, and the *threat to the institution* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables* will *refuse* whatever the *evidence*.

**"Nor Do We See That You Have Any Merit Over Us" — The Third Objection, and Its Complete Closure**

The third objection completes the closure: *wa-lā narā laka ʿalaynā faḍlan* — "nor do we see that you have any merit over us." The *faḍl* — merit, distinction — is the word that names the *quality* the *notables* *require* in a *messenger*, and the *requirement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables* *do not* *require* a *message*; they *require* a *person of higher status*, and the *requirement of the person* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables'* *search* is *not* for the *truth*; it is for the *superior*, and the *superior* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables* are *confusing* the *messenger* with the *message*, and the *confusion* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables* will *never* *find* what they *seek*, because what they *seek* is *not* the *thing* that *was* *sent*. The *final* *word* — *innā naʿtadīka la-min al-kādhibīn* — "nay, we think that you are liars" — is the *closure* of the *closure*: the *notables* *have* *decided*, and the *decision* is *not* a *conclusion*; it is a *position*, and the *position* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *dialogue* is *over*, and that the *next* *verse* will be *Noah's* *answer* to the *over*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:28

> **He said, "O my people! What think you: if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord, and He has granted me mercy from Himself, and you are blind to it. Shall we force it upon you, though you are unwilling?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"O My People" — The Address That Refuses the Notables' Frame**

Noah's answer begins with the address that the sūrah will give to every prophet in the narrative middle: *yā qawmī* — "O my people." The address is the sūrah's way of stating that the prophet *refuses* the *frame* the *notables* have *set*: they *spoke* to him as an *outsider*, as a *stranger* who *claims* *status*, and he *answers* as an *insider*, as a *member*, as a *brother* in the *same* *community*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *address*, that the *prophet* is *not* *outside* the *people*; he is *within* them, in the *sense of being* *of* them, in the *sense of being *concerned* with them, in the *sense of being* *the one who speaks to them as they are, not as he wishes them to be*. The *address* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warning* is *not* a *foreign* *imposition*; it is a *domestic* *appeal*, and the *appeal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* *bears* the *relation* to the *people* that the *notables* *refused* to *bear* to him.

**"If I Stand Upon a Clear Proof" — The Reiteration of the Burhān**

The first substance of the answer is the *reiteration* of the *burhān*: *in kuntu ʿalā-burhānin min rabbi* — "if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord." The *burhān* is the *same* *burhān* that verse 25 *named*, and the *reiteration* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *responding* to the *notables'* *objections* *point by point*; he is *restating* the *one* *thing* that *matters*: the *proof*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *reiteration*, that the *notables'* *three objections* — the *human* *like us*, the *lowliest* *followers*, the *lack of merit* — are *not* *three* *arguments*; they are *three* *refusals* of the *one* *argument*, and the *one* *argument* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *proof* is *not* *dispersible* into *three* *defenses*; it is *one*, and the *one* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *answer* is *not* a *debate*; it is a *presentation*, and the *presentation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *proof* stands *regardless* of the *response*.

**"He Has Granted Me Mercy from Himself" — The Word That Names the Source**

The second clause names the *source* of the *burhān*: *wa-wahaba li minhu raḥmah* — "and He has granted me mercy from Himself."
