## Sūrah Hūd 11:41

> **He said, "Embark upon it. In the Name of God be its coursing and its mooring. Truly my Lord is Forgiving, Merciful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Embark Upon It" — The Word That Begins the Crossing**

The sūrah now gives the prophet's own word at the moment of embarkation: *arqiʿū fīhā bismi Allāh* — "embark upon it, in the name of God." The *arqiʿ* — to embark, to mount, to take ship — is the word that names the *act of entering the vessel*, and the *sūrah's* use of it here is a *statement* about the *character* of the *crossing*: the *crossing* is *not* a *hiding*; it is an *entering*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who knows the vessel and trusts the water*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who has built the vessel and now submits to the sea*. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *prophet* is *not* *fleeing* the *flood*; he is *crossing* it, and the *crossing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *ark* is *not* a *refuge* in the *sense of being* *a place of hiding*; it is a *vessel* in the *sense of being* *the means of passage through the judgment*, and the *passage* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *salvation* is *not* an *escape*; it is a *crossing*, and the *crossing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *believers* are *not* *outside* the *judgment*; they are *within* it, *carried* through it, in the *way* *described* in *verse 40's* *command*.

**"In the Name of God Be Its Coursing and Its Mooring" — The Two Motions Given to the Name**

The *central* phrase of the *verse* is the one the *commentators* have *discussed* at *length*: *bismi Allāh majrāhā wa-mursaluhā* — "in the name of God be its coursing and its mooring." The *phrase* gives the *two* *motions* of the *ark* — the *coursing*, the *moving upon the water*, and the *mooring*, the *coming to rest* — to the *name* of *God*, and the *giving* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ark* is *not* *self-moving*; it is *moved by the name*, and that the *moved by the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *crossing* is *not* a *human* *act*; it is a *divine* *act* in the *sense of being* *the act that the name performs upon the vessel*. The sūrah is stating, in the phrase, that the *prophet* is *not* *captain* of the *ark*; he is *the one who speaks the name*, and that the *speaking the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *office* of the *prophet* in the *crossing* is *the office of the one who invokes*, and that the *invoking* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *believers* are *not* *saved by their own building*; they are *saved by the name that moves the vessel*, and that the *saved by the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *tawakkul* — the *trust* — is *not* a *passive* *waiting*; it is an *active* *invocation*, and that the *active invocation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *idle* in the *ark*; he is *speaking*, and the *speaking* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *crossing* is *not* a *silent* *act*; it is a *named* *act*, and the *named* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* who *mock the Prophet* are *not* *outside* the *name*; they are *within* it, in the *sense of being* *the ones whose account the name holds*, and that the *within the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *mockery* is *not* a *refuge*; it is a *vessel* that the *name* will *moor* at the *place it chooses*.

**"Truly My Lord Is Forgiving, Merciful" — The Closing That Names the Two Attributes**

The *verse* *closes* with the *naming* of the *two* *attributes*: *innī rabbi gafūr raḥīm* — "truly my Lord is forgiving, merciful." The *two* *attributes* are *the* *two* *attributes* that *govern* the *whole* *event*: the *forgiving* is *the attribute* that *explains* why the *few* are *saved*, and the *merciful* is *the attribute* that *explains* why the *preservation* of the *species* was *commanded* in *verse 40*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *crossing* is *not* a *mere* *rescue*; it is an *act of the forgiving and the merciful*, and that the *act of the forgiving* is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *salvation* is *not* a *merit* of the *believers*; it is a *pardon* in the *sense of being* *the act that goes beyond the account*, and that the *act of the merciful* is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *preservation* of the *pairs* is *not* a *calculation*; it is a *mercy* in the *sense of being* *the act that carries the creation through the judgment*. The sūrah is closing the *embarkation* with the *two* *attributes*, and the *closing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *crossing* is *not* a *neutral* *event*; it is a *named* *act of the forgiving and the merciful*, and that the *named act* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *name* *spoken* over the *ark*, and that the *hearing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *name* is *not* *Noah's* *name*; it is *God's* *name*, and that the *God's name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *crossing* is *not* a *human* *venture*; it is a *divine* *carrying*, and that the *divine carrying* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next* *verses* — the *sailing* and the *son* — are the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *carrying* is *not* *uniform*; it is *the carrying that includes the plea of verse 45*, and that the *including the plea* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ark* is *not* a *place of ease*; it is *the place where the prophet's love is tried against the command*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:42

> **And it sailed with them upon waves like mountains. And Noah called out to his son, who remained aloof, "O my son! Embark with us, and be not with the disbelievers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Waves Like Mountains" — The Image That the Sūrah Uses for the Flood**

The sūrah now describes the ark in motion, and it does so with the image that is the verse's central choice: *wa-taḥmil fīhā ʿalā mawājia ka-l-jibāli* — "and it sailed with them upon waves like mountains." The *tashbīh* — the simile — is the sūrah's way of stating that the *flood* is not a *rising* of water in the ordinary sense; it is a *mountain-range of water*, in the sense of being *the water that has the mass and the height of the mountains*, in the sense of being *the sea that is not a sea but a range of peaks moving upon the earth*. The sūrah is stating, in the image, that the *crossing* of verse 41 is not a *crossing upon a calm water*; it is a *crossing between peaks*, in the sense of being *the passage through a landscape of water that is itself the terrain*, and that the *landscape* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *ark* is *not* upon the water; it is *within* the water, in the sense of being *carried by the peaks and held between them*, and that the *carried by the peaks* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *salvation* is *not* a *stability*; it is a *carrying*, and the *carrying* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *tawakkul* of verse 41 — the trust given to the name — is *the condition of the crossing*, and that the *condition* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *believers* are *not* *secure* in the ark; they are *carried*, and the *carried* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *security* is *not their own*; it is the *name's*.

**"Who Remained Aloof" — The Word That Places the Son Outside**

The verse turns, in its second half, to the son, and it places him with a single phrase: *wa-nādā Nūḥun ibnahu wa-kanā fī muʿtariḍīn* — "and Noah called out to his son, who remained aloof." The *muʿtariḍ* — aloof, apart, standing aside — is the word that names the *son's* *position* at the *moment* of the *call*, and the *position* is *not* a *physical* *distance* in the first instance; it is a *relational* *distance*, in the sense of being *the standing aside from the ark*, in the sense of being *the apartness from the family that is embarked*, in the sense of being *the position of the one who is not with the believers*. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *son* is *not* *physically* *far* from the *ark*; he is *relationally* *apart*, in the sense of being *the one who has placed himself on the other side of the judgment*, and that the *placing himself* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *exception of verse 40* — the *exception* of *the one against whom the word has gone forth* — is *not* a *separation* that the *flood* *enforces*; it is a *separation* that the *son* *has made*, and that the *having made* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *call* of the *verse* is *the father's last word to the son who has already chosen*, and that the *last word* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father* is *not* *giving up*; he is *calling*, and the *calling* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *love* of verse 26 — the fear for the people — is *not exhausted* by the command of verse 40.

**"O My Son! Embark with Us" — The Call That Refuses the Distance**

The *call* itself is *two* *words* and a *command*: *yā bunayy arqiʿ maʿanā* — "O my son! embark with us." The *bunayy* — "my son" — is the *diminutive* form of the word for son, and the *use of the diminutive* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father* is *speaking to the son as the son*, in the sense of being *the one who addresses the child and not the disbeliever*, in the sense of being *the one who speaks the love before the warning*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *form* of the *call*, that the *father* is *not* *calling* the *disbeliever*; he is *calling the son*, and that the *calling the son* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *love* is *not conditional* on the *belief*, and that the *not conditional* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father's* *love* is *the love that calls to the person, not to the position*, in the way described in *verse 31's* *fourth* *denial*. The *command* — *arqiʿ maʿanā*, "embark with us" — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *father* is *offering* the *son* the *same* *vessel* that the *father* is *in*, and that the *offering the same vessel* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *salvation* is *not withheld* from the *son* by the *father*; it is *offered*, and the *offered* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *son's* *refusal* — which the next verse will state — is *not a refusal of an offered salvation*; it is a refusal of the *only salvation*, and that the *refusal of the only salvation* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *drowning* of verse 43 is *not an accident*; it is a *choice*.

**"And Be Not with the Disbelievers" — The Second Half of the Call**

The *second* *half* of the *call* is the *warning* within the *love*: *wa-lā takun maʿa-l-qawmi-l-kāfirīn* — "and be not with the disbelievers." The *warning* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *father* is *not* *only* *calling* the *son* to the *vessel*; he is *warning* the *son* against the *company*, and that the *warning against the company* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father* *knows* the *son's* *position* — the *position* of *muʿtariḍ*, the *standing aside* — and that the *knowing the position* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *call* is *not* a *general* *call*; it is a *specific* *call* to the *son who is with the disbelievers*, and that the *specific call* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father* is *distinguishing* between the *son* and the *company*, in the sense of *loving the son and warning him against the company*, and that the *distinguishing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *father* is *not* *confusing* the *two*, and that the *not confusing* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *love* is *clear*, and that the *clear* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *son's* *answer* in *verse 43* is *not* a *misunderstanding*; it is a *choice made with the call heard*, and that the *choice made with the call heard* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is *just*.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:43

> **He said, "I shall take refuge on a mountain; it will protect me from the water." He said, "There is no protector on this day from the Command of God, save for the one upon whom He has Mercy." And the waves came between them, and he was among the drowned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"I Shall Take Refuge on a Mountain" — The Son's Answer, and the Security He Assigns to the World**

The son's answer to his father's call is one of the most compressed statements in the Qur'an, and its compression is the sūrah's way of stating the entire error of the disbeliever in a single image: *qāla aʿūdhu ilā jibālin yaḥfayẓunī min al-māʾ* — "I shall take refuge on a mountain; it will protect me from the water." The *son* is *not* *refusing* the *ark* in the *form* of a *debate*; he is *proposing* an *alternative*, and the *alternative* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *disbeliever's* *response* to the *warning* is *not* a *rejection*; it is a *counter-plan*, in the *sense of being* *the plan of the one who has found his own security*, in the *sense of being* *the plan of the one who has located the protector*. The *mountain* is the *sūrah's* name for the *false security* that the *disbeliever* *assigns* to the *world*: the *high ground*, the *solid ground*, the *ground that does not move*, the *ground that the water cannot reach*. The sūrah is stating, in the image, that the *disbeliever* is *not* *without* a *plan*; he is *with* a *plan*, and that the *plan* is *not* a *bad* *plan* in the *sense of being* *foolish*; it is a *wrong* *plan* in the *sense of being *the plan that locates the protector in the created order rather than in the Creator*, and that the *locating in the created order* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *son* is *in* the *state* of *verse 20* — the *state* of *not seeing* — and that the *not seeing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *mountain* is *not* a *protector* because it is *not* the *one* who *commands* the *water*.

**"There Is No Protector on This Day from the Command of God" — The Father's Answer, and the Only Protector Named**

The father's answer is the sūrah's direct statement of the theology the son's plan denies: *qāla lā ḥāfiẓa lyawm min amri Allāh illā man raḥima* — "there is no protector on this day from the command of God, save for the one upon whom He has mercy." The *answer* names the *only* *protector* and *defines* the *only* *protected*: the *protector* is not the *mountain*; the *protector* is *the mercy of God*, and the *protected* are *not the high*; they are *the mercied*. The sūrah is stating, in the answer, that the *command* — *amr Allāh* — is the *act* that the *water* *executes*, and that the *act* is *not* *subject* to the *mountain*, in the sense of being *the act that the mountain cannot stay*, in the sense of being *the act that reaches the high ground as it reaches the low*, and that the *reaching the high ground* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *flood* is *not a* *flood* in the *ordinary* sense; it is a *command*, and the *command* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *son's* *mountain* is *not* a *refuge* because it is *not* the *one* who *issues* the *command*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *answer*, that the *father* is *not* *arguing* with the *son*; he is *stating* the *fact* that the *son* is *refusing*, and that the *stating the fact* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* of *verse 42* is *complete*, and that the *complete* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next* *clause* — the *waves between them* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *time for the call is over*, and that the *over* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, because the *son* *heard* the *answer*, and that the *heard* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *drowning* is *not* a *surprise*; it is a *consequence*.

**"The Waves Came Between Them" — The Separation That the Sūrah States**

The *next* *clause* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* the *separation*: *wa-ḥāla baynahumā al-mawju fa-kanā min al-ghariqīn* — "and the waves came between them, and he was among the drowned." The *ḥawl* — to interpose, to come between — is the *word* that the *Qur'an* *uses* for the *barrier* that *separates*, and the *use* here is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *separation* is *not* a *distance* that the *water* *creates*; it is a *barrier* that the *command* *places*, and that the *placing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *father* and the *son* are *not* *apart* because the *water* *rose*; they are *apart* because the *account* *separated* them, and that the *separation by the account* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *kinship* does not *cross* the *barrier*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 40's* *exception*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *clause*, that the *waves* are *not* the *cause* of the *separation*; they are the *form* of it, and that the *form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *separation* is *real* in the *sense of being* *the separation that the command effects*, and that the *effect* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *son* is *among the drowned* — *min al-ghariqīn* — and that the *among the drowned* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *fate* of *verse 40* is *fulfilled*, and that the *fulfilled* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *father* will now *plead* in *verse 45*, and that the *plea* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *love* is *not* *exhausted* by the *separation*, and that the *not exhausted* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next* *three* *verses* are the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *above* the *grief*, and that the *not above the grief* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *human* in the *way* *described* in *verse 31's* *third* *denial*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:44

> **And it was said, "O earth! Swallow your water! And O sky! Hold back!" And the water was made to recede, and the command was carried out, and it settled on [Mount] Jūdī. And it was said, "Away with the wrongdoing people!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Commands to the Earth and the Sky**

The verse opens with two addresses: *wa-qīla yā arḍu blaʿī māʾaki wa-yā samāʾu aqliʿī* — "and it was said, 'O earth! Swallow your water! And O sky! Hold back!'" The commentators explain that God's commands to the earth and sky denote the end of the flood.

The two commands are matched to the two sources of the water. The commentators note that the flood in the preceding verses came from the opened gates of the sky and the bursting springs of the earth, and that the reversal is addressed to each in turn.

The verbs are precise. *Iblaʿī* — swallow — is used of the earth taking back what it released; *aqliʿī* — hold back, cease — is used of the sky's rain stopping. The commentators read the pairing as indicating a complete cessation rather than a reduction.

The direct address to inanimate creation is characteristic of the Qur'an's flood narrative. The commentators note that the earth and sky are spoken to as addressees, which the tradition has read as indicating the immediacy of the command rather than a mediated process.

The verse's abruptness is worth noting. After the extended narrative of the flood's onset, its end is given in a single sentence of two imperatives.

**"The Water Was Made to Recede, and the Command Was Carried Out"**

The verse continues with two passive statements: *wa-ghīḍa l-māʾu wa-quḍiya l-amr* — "and the water was made to recede, and the command was carried out." The commentators note that both are stated as accomplished facts.

The verb *ghīḍa* denotes water diminishing or being absorbed. The commentators read it as describing the water's withdrawal rather than its evaporation or drainage — the earth has swallowed what it released.

The phrase *quḍiya l-amr* — "the command was carried out," or "the matter was decided" — is what the commentators emphasize. The commentators note that the same phrase appears elsewhere in the Qur'an for the settlement of a decisive matter.

The dual statement is significant. The water's recession is the physical event; the carrying out of the command is its meaning. The commentators read the pairing as indicating that the flood was not a natural catastrophe but an executed judgment.

The passive voice throughout removes any agent other than God. The commentators note that the verse does not describe the process by which the water receded, only that it did and that the command was fulfilled.

**"It Settled on [Mount] Jūdī"**

The verse names the resting place: *wa-stawat ʿalā l-jūdī* — "and it settled on [Mount] Jūdī." The commentators record the disagreement about the mountain's location and preserve it.

Al-Jawzī and others report that in some traditional sources Jūdī, where the Ark settled, is a mountain near Mosul. The commentators note that other sources associate it with Mount Ararat in the eastern region of present-day Turkey.

The disagreement is left open. The commentators do not resolve it, and the verse itself gives no geographical specification beyond the name.

The verb *istawat* — settled, became level — is worth noting. The commentators read it as indicating that the Ark came to rest rather than landed, and that the water's recession left it grounded.

The naming of a specific mountain is unusual in the Qur'an's flood narrative, which is otherwise sparing of geographical detail. The commentators have read this as anchoring the account in a place the hearers could locate.

**"Away With the Wrongdoing People"**

The verse closes with *wa-qīla buʿdan li-l-qawmi ẓ-ẓālimīn* — "and it was said, 'Away with the wrongdoing people!'" The commentators note the form of the statement: *buʿdan* is a noun of distance used as an imprecation.

Al-Rāzī records the belief that this was spoken by God. The commentators note the parallel at 23:41, where the identical phrase appears in the account of the destruction of Thamūd.

The placement is striking. The imprecation comes after the Ark has settled — after the deliverance is complete. The commentators read the sequence as significant: the judgment is pronounced once the saved are safe.

The word *ẓālimīn* — wrongdoers — is the sūrah's consistent term for the destroyed communities. The commentators note that it appears throughout the Noah narrative and that the verse uses it as the final word on the people.

The imprecation is not addressed to the people but about them. The commentators read this as indicating that the address has ended; there is no further exchange between God and the destroyed community.

**The Verse as the Narrative's Resolution**

Verse 44 concludes the flood narrative that begins at v. 36. The commentators read its compression as deliberate: after eight verses of warning, refusal, boarding, and rising water, the resolution is given in a single verse.

The verse contains the whole reversal. The waters that rose are commanded down; the Ark that floated is settled; the people who refused are dismissed. The commentators read the sequence as a complete undoing of the flood's conditions.

The connection to v. 43 is instructive. There the water rose and the Ark ran, and Noah's son refused to board. The verse here resolves both — the water recedes and the son is among those dismissed.

The commentators note that the verse sets the pattern for the sūrah's remaining narratives. In each case the destruction is stated briefly after the warning is refused, and in each case the saved are distinguished from the destroyed.

The final imprecation does pastoral work. The commentators read it as addressing the present audience: the distance pronounced on the wrongdoers is available to be avoided, and the sūrah's remaining narratives show how.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:45

> **And Noah called out to his Lord and said, "O my Lord! Truly my son is from my family. Thy Promise is indeed true, and Thou art the most just of judges."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Noah Called Out to His Lord" — The Turn That the Sūrah Records**

The sūrah now records the turn that is the verse's central event: *wa-nādā Nūḥun rabbahu* — "and Noah called out to his Lord." The *turn* is from the *son* to the *Lord*, and the *sūrah's* *recording* of it is *its* *way* of *stating* that the *father's* *grief* is *not* *silenced* by the *separation* of *verse 43*, and that the *not silenced* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *above* the *human* *response* to the *loss*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 31's third denial* — the *denial* that *kept* the *prophet* *human*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *prophet* *does not* *suppress* the *grief*; he *turns* with it, and that the *turning with it* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *grief* is *not* a *failure of faith*; it is a *form of address*, and that the *form of address* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *faith* is *not* a *composure*; it is a *relation*, and the *relation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* *speaks to God in the loss*, and that the *speaking in the loss* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *duʿāʾ* — the *supplication* — is *not* a *request for the reversal*; it is a *presentation of the claim*, and the *presentation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *demanding*; he is *stating his case*.

**"Truly My Son Is from My Family" — The Claim, Stated on the Basis of the Promise**

The *substance* of the *call* is the *claim*: *innā ibnī min ahlī* — "truly my son is from my family." The *claim* is *stated* not as a *petition* for *mercy* but as a *statement of fact* that the *prophet* *believes* the *divine* *promise* *requires*: the *promise* of *verse 40* was *the* *saving* of the *family*, and the *prophet* is *holding* the *promise* to its *terms*, in the *sense of being* *the one who presents the claim on the basis of the promise*. The sūrah is stating, in the claim, that the *prophet* is *not* *asking God to break the command*; he is *asking God to honor the promise*, and that the *asking to honor the promise* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* *trusts* the *promise* more than he *trusts* the *separation*, in the *sense of being* *the one who believes the family-promise covers the son*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not yet know the exception of verse 40*. The sūrah is stating, in the claim, that the *prophet* is *not* *wrong* in the *sense of being* *disobedient*; he is *mistaken* in the *sense of being* *uninformed of the exception*, and that the *being uninformed* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *claim* is *not* a *sin*; it is a *human* *reading* of the *promise*, and that the *human reading* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *human* in the *way* *described* in *verse 41's* *embarkation* — the *way* in which the *prophet* is *carried* and *speaks* and *loves* and *grieves*, all within the *name*.

**"Thy Promise Is Indeed True" — The Affirmation That the Sūrah Does Not Withhold**

The *second* *clause* of the *call* is the *affirmation*: *wa-ʿadu-dhakka ṣadqan* — "thy promise is indeed true." The *affirmation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *doubting* the *promise*; he is *affirming* it, in the *sense of being* *the one who holds the promise as true and therefore as covering the son*, in the *sense of being* *the one who reasons from the truth of the promise to the inclusion of the son*. The sūrah is stating, in the affirmation, that the *prophet's* *faith* is *not* *weakened* by the *grief*; it is *the ground of the grief*, and that the *ground of the grief* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *despairing*; he is *trusting*, and that the *trusting* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *grief* of the *prophet* is *not* the *despondency* of *verse 9*; it is the *trust that speaks in the loss*, and that the *trust that speaks* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ṣabr* of *verse 11* is *not* a *silence*; it is a *speech*, and that the *speech* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *patience* is *not* the *absence of the plea*; it is the *form of the plea*.

**"Thou Art the Most Just of Judges" — The Closing That Places the Claim Before the Judge**

The *call* *closes* with the *naming* of the *attribute* that *governs* the *claim*: *wa-anta aʿdamu-l-ḥukkāmi ḥukmā* — "and thou art the most just of judges." The *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *appealing* to *mercy* alone; he is *appealing* to *justice*, in the *sense of being* *the one who presents the claim to the judge on the basis of the judge's justice*, in the *sense of being* *the one who believes the just judge will honor the promise*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet* is *not* *begging*; he is *presenting a case*, and that the *presenting a case* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *relation* to *God* is *not* a *relation of fear*; it is a *relation of trust in the justice*, and that the *trust in the justice* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *above* the *correction* that *verse 46* will *give*, in the *sense of being* *the one who presents the claim knowing that the judge is above the claim*, in the *sense of being* *the one who trusts the judge's answer whatever it is*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet* is *the one who speaks to the most just of judges with the confidence of the one who knows the judge*, and that the *confidence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next* *verse* — the *answer* — is *not* a *rejection* of the *prophet*; it is a *correction* of the *claim*, and that the *correction* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *justice* is *not* the *prophet's* *reading* of the *promise*; it is the *promise itself*, in its *full* *form*, including the *exception*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:46

> **He said, "O Noah! Truly he is not from thy family; surely such conduct was not righteous. So question Me not concerning that whereof thou hast no knowledge; truly I exhort thee, lest thou be among the ignorant."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Truly He Is Not from Thy Family" — The Correction That the Sūrah States**

God's answer to the plea opens with the correction that is the verse's central word: *qāla yā Nūhu innahu lā min ahlīk* — "O Noah! truly he is not from thy family." The *correction* does not deny the *blood*; it *redefines* the *family*, and the *redefinition* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *family* in the *promise* of *verse 40* is *not* a *family of blood*; it is a *family of faith*, in the *sense of being* *the family that the promise covers*, in the *sense of being* *the family that the ark carries*. The sūrah is stating, in the correction, that the *son* is *of the blood* but *not of the family*, in the sense of being *the one who has placed himself outside the faith by the choice of verse 43*, and that the *placing himself outside* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *kinship does not carry the account*, in the way described in *verse 40's exception*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *correction*, that the *promise* was *true* in the *way* the *prophet* *affirmed* in *verse 45* — the *promise is indeed true* — and that the *truth of the promise* is *precisely* the *reason* the *son is not saved*, because the *promise* *covers the family of faith*, and the *son* is *not of it*, and the *not of it* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet's reading* was *not a doubt*; it was an *incomplete knowledge of the form of the promise*, and that the *incomplete knowledge* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *correction* is *not a rebuke of the faith*; it is a *completion of the knowledge*.

**"Such Conduct Was Not Righteous" — The Reason That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* gives the *reason*: *inna-hu ʿamalan ghayra ṣāliḥ* — "surely such conduct was not righteous." The *reason* is *not* a *list of sins*; it is a *single* *characterization* of the *son's* *conduct* as *not righteous*, in the *sense of being* *the conduct that does not meet the standard of the faith*, in the *sense of being* *the conduct of verse 43* — the *mountain*, the *refusal of the ark*, the *company of the disbelievers*. The sūrah is stating, in the reason, that the *exclusion* is *not* a *mystery*; it is *the conduct*, and that the *conduct* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *account* is *not* arbitrary; it is *the account of the deed*, in the way described in *verse 21* — the *loss of the self by the deed* — and that the *not righteous* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *son's* *choice* was *not a small* *choice*; it was *the choice that defined him*, and that the *defining* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *drowning* was *not* a *fate*; it was a *consequence*, and that the *consequence* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *justice* named in *verse 45* is *not* *violated* by the *drowning*; it is *fulfilled* by it, because the *just judge* *judges by the conduct*, and the *conduct* was *not righteous*.

**"Question Me Not Concerning That Whereof Thou Hast No Knowledge" — The Admonition Stated as Care**

The *third* *clause* is the *admonition*: *fa-lā tasʾilnī ʿammā lā ʿalayka fīhi ʿilm* — "so question me not concerning that whereof thou hast no knowledge." The *admonition* is *not* a *scolding*; it is a *care*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the One who protects the prophet from the state of asking beyond the knowledge*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the One who knows that the asking is born of love and not of presumption*. The sūrah is stating, in the admonition, that the *prophet* is *not* *forbidden* from *love*; he is *directed* to *knowledge*, in the sense of being *the one who is told that the love must be informed by the knowledge of the form of the promise*, and that the *directed to knowledge* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet's* *office* is *not* an *office of guessing*; it is an *office of receiving*, and that the *receiving* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet* is *not* to *reason beyond the revelation*; he is to *wait for the revelation*, and that the *waiting* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *correction* of *verse 46* is *the same* *form* as the *counsel* of *verse 36* — the *form* in which the *divine* *speaks to the prophet's interior* with the *knowledge of the interior*, in the way described in *verse 5*.

**"Lest Thou Be Among the Ignorant" — The Word That the Sūrah Renders with Care**

The *admonition* *closes* with the *word* that the *commentators* have *rendered* with *care*: *fa-innī naḥīka an takūna min al-jāhilīn* — "truly I exhort thee, lest thou be among the ignorant." The *jāhil* here is *not* *ignorance* in the *sense of being* *stupidity*; the *commentators* *note* that it refers *more specifically* to *sinfulness*, to the *state of the one who acts beyond the knowledge*, and the *sūrah's* *use* of it here is *its* *way* of *stating* that the *risk* the *prophet* is *protected from* is *not* a *risk of error*; it is a *risk of transgression*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the one who would overstep the knowledge in the plea*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the one who would make the love a ground for the demand*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet* is *not* *in* that *state*; he is *exhorted from it*, and that the *exhorting from it* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet* is *kept* in the *state of the one who receives*, and that the *kept* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *next verse* — the *prophet's response* — is *the sūrah's way of stating* that the *prophet* *does not* *argue back*; he *seeks refuge*, and that the *seeking refuge* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *correction* is *received* in the *form of the refuge*, and that the *received in the form of the refuge* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet* is *human* and *kept*, in the *way* described throughout the *narrative middle*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:47

> **He said, "My Lord! Truly I seek refuge in Thee from questioning Thee concerning that whereof I have no knowledge. If thou dost not forgive me and have Mercy upon me, I shall be among the losers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Truly I Seek Refuge in Thee" — The Response That the Sūrah Records**

The sūrah now records the prophet's response to the correction, and the response is one of the most studied in the Qur'an: *qāla rabbi innī aʿūdhu bayka an asʾala-ka mā laysa lī bihi ʿilm* — "my Lord! truly I seek refuge in thee from questioning thee concerning that whereof I have no knowledge." The *response* is *not* a *defense*; it is not an *excuse*; it is not a *re-presentation of the claim*. It is a *seeking of refuge*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who receives the correction and places himself under the protection of the One who gave it*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who does not argue with the correction*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *prophet* is *not* *the one who stands his ground* against the *correction*; he is *the one who turns from the claim to the refuge*, and that the *turning* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *character* is *not* a *character of defensiveness*; it is a *character of receptivity*, and that the *receptivity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *the one who receives the correction as the prophet receives the revelation*, in the same posture, in the same stillness, and that the *same posture* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *correction* is *not a lesser word*; it is a *word* of the *same kind as the revelation*, and the *same kind* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *prophet* *treats it so*.

**"From Questioning Thee Concerning That Whereof I Have No Knowledge" — The Specific From Which Refuge Is Sought**

The *response* *names* the *specific* *thing* from which the *refuge* is *sought*: not from the *love*, not from the *grief*, not from the *claim itself* — but from *questioning beyond the knowledge*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *naming*, that the *prophet* *does not* *seek refuge from the love of the son*; he *keeps* the *love*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not renounce the father's feeling*, and that the *not renouncing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *above* the *human*; he is *the one who has the grief and is corrected in the form of the grief*, and that the *corrected in the form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *correction* does not *remove* the *human*; it *informs* it. The *refuge* is *from the act of asking beyond the knowledge*, and the *naming of the specific act* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *precise* in his *self-knowledge*, in the sense of *naming the exact thing he will not do again*, and that the *precision* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *vaguely* *repentant*; he is *specifically* *refuged*, and that the *specifically refuged* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *tawba* of *verse 3* — the *turning* — is *not* a *general* *act*; it is a *named* *act*, and the *named act* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *repentance* is *real* in the *sense of being* *the turning from the specific*.

**"If Thou Dost Not Forgive Me and Have Mercy upon Me" — The Conditional That the Sūrah States**

The *second* *half* of the *response* is the *conditional*: *in lam taghfir lī wa-tarḥamnī lukunthu min al-khāsirīn* — "if thou dost not forgive me and have mercy upon me, I shall be among the losers." The *conditional* is *not* a *threat*; it is a *statement of the prophet's own account*, in the *sense of being* *the one who states what his state would be without the forgiveness*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not presume on the mercy but places himself within its condition*. The sūrah is stating, in the conditional, that the *prophet* is *not* *certain of his own acceptance* in the *sense of being* *the one who claims it for himself*; he is *the one who states the condition*, and that the *stating the condition* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *above* the *account* that *verse 22* *described* for the *losers*, in the *sense of being* *the one who knows the state of the loser and fears it for himself*, in the *sense of being* *the one who places himself on the right side of the state only by the forgiveness*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *conditional*, that the *prophet's* *humility* is *not* a *performance*; it is a *knowledge*, in the *sense of being* *the knowledge of his own account before the judge*, and that the *knowledge* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *the one who knows the loser and does not count himself among the safe without the mercy*, and that the *not counting himself* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *khaḍaʿ* of *verse 23* — the *humility before the Lord* — is *in operation* in the *prophet* in the *moment* of his *greatest* *grief*, and that the *in operation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *humility* is *not* a *state of ease*; it is a *state of the heart that holds in the loss*.

**"The Words Identical to Adam's" — and the Sūrah's Way of Stating the Pattern**

The *commentators* *note* that the *prophet's* *words* of *refuge* are *identical* in form to the *words* that *Adam* and his *wife* *uttered* in *7:23* after the *trickery* of *Satan* had *become clear* to them:

> *"Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves, and if Thou forgiveth not us and have not mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers."*

The *identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *moment* is *not unique*; it is *the moment of the father of mankind*, in the *sense of being* *the moment of the one who is corrected and turns to the refuge*, in the *sense of being* *the moment of the one who states the condition of his own account*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *identity*, that the *pattern* of *correction and refuge* is *the pattern of the human before God*, and that the *prophet* is *within* the *pattern*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 31* — the *way* in which the *prophet* is *human* and *kept* — and that the *within the pattern* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *consolation* of the *narrative middle* is *real* in its *deepest* *form*: the *Prophet* in *Makkah* is *not* *alone* in the *grief*; he is *in the company of Noah and of Adam*, and that the *company* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *burden* is *shared* across the *whole* of *human history*, and that the *shared* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — the *disembarkation with peace* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *grief* is *not the end of the story*; it is the *middle of it*, and that the *end* is the *peace of verse 48*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:48

> **It was said, "O Noah! Disembark with peace from Us, and blessings upon thee and upon the communities that [will arise] from those with thee, and communities for whom We shall grant enjoyment. Then a painful punishment from Us shall befall them."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Disembark With Peace From Us"**

The verse records the address to Noah once the Ark has settled: *ya Nūḥu hbiṭ bi-salāmin minnā* — "O Noah! Disembark with peace from Us." Al-Baqillānī and al-Ṭabarī explain that God tells Noah that he and those with him can come out of the Ark and live on land without fear of perishing.

The word *salām* — peace, safety, soundness — is qualified by *minnā*, "from Us." The commentators read the qualification as indicating that the safety is granted rather than situational. The water has receded, but the peace is not merely the absence of flood.

The verb *ihbiṭ* — descend, disembark — answers the command at v. 44 by which the earth was told to swallow its water. The commentators read the pairing as completing the reversal: the water that rose is gone, and those who were commanded aboard are now commanded off.

The address is to Noah by name. The commentators note that the sūrah names its prophets at the decisive moments of their narratives, and that this naming marks the transition from trial to settlement.

**The Elision in the Blessing — and What It Means**

The verse's second clause is compressed, and al-Zamakhsharī identifies the elision that governs it: *wa-barakātin ʿalayka wa-ʿalā umamin mimma maʿak* — "and blessings upon thee and upon the communities that [will arise] from those with thee."

The commentators explain that an elision in the phrase causes it to mean: blessings upon thee and upon the communities that will arise from those with thee; and from those with thee are communities for whom We shall grant enjoyment. The bracketed material supplies what the Arabic leaves implicit.

The elision matters because it makes both sets of communities descend from the same source. The commentators read this as significant: the blessed and the merely-provided-for share a common origin in those who were aboard.

Al-Zamakhsharī's reading turns on the repetition of *min*. The commentators note that the second *umam* is understood to take its reference from the same antecedent as the first, which is what makes the two groups branches of one family rather than unrelated peoples.

**Two Readings of the Blessing — Both Preserved**

The commentators record two readings of what the blessing amounts to, and both are preserved rather than one being preferred.

On one reading, the communities of believers that will arise from Noah's family will be blessed. On this reading the blessing is specific to the believing line, and the communities mentioned later are distinguished from them.

On the other reading, the communities of both disbelievers and believers that will arise from Noah's family will receive provision from God. On this reading the blessing is material and extends to all the descendants.

Al-Zamakhsharī's account holds the two together: provision is given to both, but the disbelieving communities receive it without the blessing attached. The commentators read this as distinguishing between sustenance and blessing rather than dividing the descendants into two unrelated groups.

It is worth recording that the verse does not settle which reading is intended. It names communities blessed and communities granted enjoyment, and leaves the relationship between them to be worked out. The tradition's two readings reflect a genuine ambiguity in the text.

**"Then a Painful Punishment Shall Befall Them"**

The verse closes with a turn: *wa-umamun sa-numattiʿuhum thumma yamassuhum minnā ʿadhābun alīm* — "and communities for whom We shall grant enjoyment. Then a painful punishment from Us shall befall them."

The verb *numattiʿ* — We shall grant enjoyment — is from the root of *matāʿ*, the provision of this world. The commentators connect it to the sūrah's earlier treatment of enjoyment at vv. 15–16, where those who desire the life of this world are paid in full in it.

The particle *thumma* — then — indicates sequence with a gap. The commentators read it as marking the interval between the enjoyment and the punishment, which is the span of the communities' life.

The adjective *alīm* — painful — qualifies the punishment. The commentators note that the verse pairs the two terms deliberately: enjoyment granted, then pain inflicted. The sequence is the verse's warning.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's central theme of respite. The enjoyment is real and it is given; the punishment follows and is not avoided. The interval between them is what the communities mistake for safety.

**The Verse as the Flood Narrative's Coda**

Verse 48 concludes the Noah narrative, and the commentators note that it does so by looking forward rather than back. The flood is over; the verse is about what comes after.

The two sets of communities are the point. The commentators read the verse as extending the narrative beyond Noah's lifetime to all the generations that descend from the Ark's passengers, which is to say all of humanity on the Qur'an's account.

The distinction between blessing and enjoyment is what gives the verse its moral weight. Not all who descend from the saved are blessed, and the verse says so plainly. The commentators read this as answering any assumption that descent from a saved family secures a standing.

This connects to v. 46, where Noah's son is refused entry to the Ark despite being of his family. The commentators read the two verses together: family did not save the son, and family will not bless every descendant.

The pastoral application the commentators draw is direct. The verse addresses any community that counts itself secure by lineage. The blessing and the enjoyment are distinguished, and the punishment follows the enjoyment unless something else intervenes.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:49

> **These are among the accounts of the Unseen that We reveal unto thee. Thou knewest not of them, neither thou nor thy people, beforehand. So be patient. Truly the end belongs to the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Accounts of the Unseen That We Reveal Unto Thee"**

The verse steps out of the narrative and addresses the Prophet directly: *tilka min anbāʾi l-ghaybi nūḥīhā ʿalayk* — "these are among the accounts of the Unseen that We reveal unto thee." The commentators note the demonstrative *tilka*, which points back to the Noah narrative just concluded.

The word *anbāʾ* is the plural of *nabaʾ*, a report of consequence. The commentators read it as indicating that what has been narrated is not entertainment but information of weight.

The phrase *mina l-ghayb* — "of the Unseen" — is what the commentators emphasize. The accounts belong to the category of what is not accessible by ordinary means, and their delivery is therefore evidence of the delivery's source.

The verb *nūḥīhā* — We reveal them — is in the present tense with the plural object. The commentators read this as indicating an ongoing process: the revelation of these accounts is part of the continuing revelation, not a completed act.

The verse's function is apologetic in the technical sense. It supplies the ground on which the Prophet can narrate what he did not witness, and it does so before the objection is raised.

**"Thou Knewest Not of Them, Neither Thou Nor Thy People"**

The verse states the premise: *mā kunta taʿlamuhā anta wa-lā qawmuka min qabli hādhā* — "thou knewest not of them, neither thou nor thy people, beforehand." Al-Kisāʾī and al-Zamakhsharī explain that this means the Prophet did not know such accounts as the story of Noah before the revelation came to him.

The commentators add that the accounts were not known to the people of Makkah at that time, since they were not "People of the Book." Al-Ṭūsī records this reading.

The commentators then qualify it, and the qualification is important. Given the presence of Christians and Jews in Makkah during the Prophet's lifetime, they note that it is more likely that this verse refers to a lack of knowledge about the details and spiritual meaning of such stories rather than a lack of general knowledge of them.

This qualification is a candid concession about the historical setting. The commentators are not claiming that no one in Makkah had heard of Noah; they are claiming that the detailed and interpreted account was not available.

The distinction matters for how the verse is read as evidence. The commentators' qualification narrows the claim from ignorance of the stories to ignorance of their content and significance, which is what the revelation supplies.

**"So Be Patient"**

The verse turns to a command: *fa-ṣbir* — "so be patient." The commentators note the particle *fa-*, which makes the command follow from what precedes.

The connection is what the commentators emphasize. The Prophet has been given accounts of earlier messengers, each of whom was rejected and each of whom endured. The command to patience is grounded in the narratives just delivered.

The commentators connect this to v. 115's command to patience and to v. 112's command to steadfastness, and read the three as forming a pattern in the sūrah's address to the Prophet.

The brevity of the command is notable. The verse does not elaborate on what patience requires or how long it must last. The commentators read the compression as indicating that the narratives have already supplied the content.

The placement between the revelation of the accounts and the promise of the outcome is significant. The commentators read the sequence as: here is what happened to those before you; be patient; the end belongs to the reverent.

**"The End Belongs to the Reverent"**

The verse closes with *inna l-ʿāqibata li-l-muttaqīn* — "truly the end belongs to the reverent." The commentators note the word *ʿāqiba*, which denotes the final issue or consequence of a matter.

The term *muttaqīn* — the reverent, those who guard themselves — is the Qur'an's standard designation for those who maintain awareness of God. The commentators connect it to the sūrah's use of the same root in Noah's address at v. 48.

The definite article in *al-ʿāqiba* is what the commentators emphasize. It is not an end among ends but the end — the final outcome that settles what preceded it.

The verse thus supplies what the command to patience requires: an assurance about how things end. The commentators read the two clauses as inseparable, since patience without the assurance would be mere endurance.

The connection to the narratives is direct. In each account the messenger was rejected and the community destroyed, and in each the reverent were saved. The verse generalizes the pattern into a promise.

**The Verse as the Narrative's Frame**

Verse 49 closes the Noah narrative that opens at v. 25, and the commentators note that it does so by reframing what has been told. The story is not presented as history but as revelation.

The reframing does specific work. The commentators observe that the sūrah's audience included people who knew some version of these accounts, and that the verse addresses their knowledge directly rather than ignoring it.

The qualification about the details and spiritual meaning is what allows the verse to function honestly. The commentators' candor about Jewish and Christian presence in Makkah prevents the claim from being overstated.

The verse also establishes the pattern for the narratives that follow. Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Abraham, Lot, Shuʿayb, and Moses are all introduced in similar terms, and the commentators read v. 49 as supplying the hermeneutic for all of them.

The command to patience is what connects the frame to the Prophet's situation. The commentators read the verse as telling him that the accounts are given for his endurance, not merely for his information.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:50

> **And unto ʿĀd, their brother Hūd. He said, "O my people! Worship God! You have no god other than Him. You are naught but fabricators."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And Unto ʿĀd, Their Brother Hūd" — The Second Story, and the Word That Opens It**

The sūrah opens the second story of its narrative middle with a single line that contains the people, the prophet, and the relation: *wa-ilā ʿĀdin akhāhum Hūd* — "and unto ʿĀd, their brother Hūd." The *akhu* — "brother" — is the word that the sūrah uses for every prophet in the narrative middle, and the *use* here is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* a *foreign* *missionary*; he is *one of them*, in the *sense of being* *of their blood and their language and their town*, in the *sense of being* *the one who speaks to them as one of them speaks to them*. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *message* is *not* a *foreign* *demand*; it is a *domestic* *appeal*, in the way described in *Noah's address of verse 28*, and that the *domestic appeal* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *Makkans'* *objection* — the *objection* that the *Prophet* is *not* one of *their standing* — is *not* a *new* *objection*; it is the *objection* that *every* *people* has *made* to *its* *brother*, and that the *not new* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *pattern* is *invariant*.

**ʿĀd — The People, and the Sūrah's Naming of Them**

The *commentators* *note* that *ʿĀd* were *descendants of Noah*, that they *inhabited* the *southern* *part of Arabia* in the *region* *between Hadramawt and Oman*, and that their *name* *derives* from a *man* *to whom they traced their common ancestry*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *people* are *not* an *abstract* *type*; they are *a people* with a *history* and a *land* and a *genealogy*, and that the *having a history* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *story* is *not a parable*; it is *a history*, in the way stated in *verse 49* — the *way* in which the *stories* are *the unseen made speech*. The *commentators* *also* *note* that there were *two peoples* named *ʿĀd* — *ʿĀd the Former* (known also as *Iram*, after 89:7–8) and *ʿĀd the Latter* — and that the *people to whom Hūd was sent* were *ʿĀd the Former*, and that the *distinction* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *naming* in the *Qur'an* is *precise*, and that the *precise* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* who *know* the *name* *ʿĀd* from their *own* *tradition* are *hearing* the *name* *used* in a *way* they *do not expect*, and that the *not expecting* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *revelation* carries *knowledge* that the *Makkans* *do not have*, in the way stated in *verse 49's clause*.

**"Worship God! You Have No God Other Than Him" — The Call, Repeated in Its Invariant Form**

Hūd's call to his people is *identical* in its core to the call with which the sūrah opened in verse 2, and to Noah's call in verse 26: *uʿbudū Allāh* — "worship God" — *mā lakum min ilāhin illā huwa* — "you have no god other than Him." The *identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *variable*; it is a *constant*, in the *sense of being* *the same words from the same source to the same kind of people*, in the *sense of being* *the call that does not change with the century or the country*. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* their *own call* *spoken* by a *prophet they have not met*, in a *country they have not seen*, in a *century they have not lived*, and that the *hearing their own call* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *distance* between the *Makkans* and *ʿĀd* is *not a distance of message*; it is a *distance of response*, in the way described in *verse 25's statement of the pattern*.

**"You Are Naught but Fabricators" — The Word That Closes the Opening of the Story**

The *story* *opens* with the *word* that *characterizes* the *people*: *innān kum muftarīn* — "you are naught but fabricators." The *iftirāʾ* — fabrication — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *used* in *verse 18* for the *greatest* *wrong*, and the *use* here is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not* merely *wrong*; they are *fabricators*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who ascribe to God what is not of God*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who create falsehood in the place of truth*. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *idolatry* of the *ʿĀd* is *not a* *mistake*; it is a *fabrication*, in the sense of being *the composition of a false divinity*, in the sense of being *the creation of a god that does not exist*, and that the *creation of the false god* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *ʿĀd* are *in* the state of *verse 18* — the state of *fabricating a lie against God* — and that the *in the state* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *Makkans* are *in the same state*, and that the *in the same state* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *mirror* is *complete* in the way it has been stated throughout the narrative middle, and that the *complete* is the sūrah's way of stating that the *next verses* — the *rejection* and the *drought* and the *wind* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *story* is *not over* by the *call*; it is *in the response*, in the way the *Noah story* was *in the response of the notables*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:51

> **O my people! I ask not of you any reward for it. My reward lies only with the One Who originated me. Do you not understand?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"I Ask Not of You Any Reward" — The Denial Repeated in Its Invariant Form**

Hūd's second word to his people is the *same* *denial* that *Noah* *made* in *verse 29*: *lā asʾalukum ʿalayhi ajran* — "I ask not of you any reward for it." The *identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *denial* is *not a* *prophet's* *individual* *choice*; it is *the form of the prophetic office itself*, in the *sense of being* *the form that every prophet takes when he speaks to his people*, in the *sense of being* *the form that separates the message from the economy*. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *notables of ʿĀd* are *hearing* the *same* *denial* that *the notables of Noah* *heard*, and that the *hearing the same denial* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *frame of the notables* — the *frame* in which *everything is a transaction* — is *the frame that the prophets do not enter*, and that the *not entering* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *frame* is *the same frame*, and that the *same frame* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern* is *invariant* across the *stories*.

**"My Reward Lies Only with the One Who Originated Me" — The Warrant, and the Word That Names the Source**

The *warrant* for the *denial* is stated in a *form* that *differs* slightly from *Noah's*: *innā ajra ʿalā-lladhī faṭarānī* — "my reward lies only with the One who originated me." The *fara* — to originate, to create, to bring into being — is the *word* that names the *relation* between the *prophet* and *God* as *the relation of the created to the Creator*, and the *use* of the *word* here, *rather than* the *simpler* "God," is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *reward* is *not* *placed* with *God* as a *general* *fact*; it is *placed* with *the One who made him*, in the *sense of being* *the One who has the specific claim on the prophet's life*, in the *sense of being* *the One to whom the prophet's existence itself is owed*. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *prophet's* *debt* is *not* a *debt to the people*; it is a *debt to the Creator*, and that the *debt to the Creator* is the *sūrah's way* of *stating* that the *people* have *no claim on the prophet's reward* because *the prophet's existence itself is not theirs to reward*, and that the *not theirs to reward* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables'* *frame of transaction* is *not the frame that can hold the prophet*, in the way described in *verse 29's statement of the replacement of the transaction*.

**"Do You Not Understand?" — The Question That Closes the Word**

The *verse* *closes* with the *question*: *afalā taʿqilūn* — "do you not understand?" The *question* is the *same* *question* that *closed* the *sūrah's* *opening section* in *verse 24*, and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *without* the *capacity* to *understand*; they *have* the *capacity*, and the *capacity* is *not engaged*, in the way described in *verse 20's statement of the spiritual deafness and blindness*. The sūrah is stating, in the question, that the *prophet* is *not* *despairing* of the *people's understanding*; he is *addressing* their *understanding* as the *one who knows it is there*, and that the *addressing as present* is the *sūrah's way* of *stating* that the *jāhil* of *verse 29* — the *ignorance* — is *not a* *lack of capacity*; it is a *suspension of the capacity*, and that the *suspension* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, because the *one who is not understanding* is the *one who is not engaging*, and that the *not engaging* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — the *call to seek forgiveness* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not finished* with the *people*; he is *turning to the practical form of the call*, in the way described in *verse 3's statement of the connection between forgiveness and repentance*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:52

> **O my people! Seek forgiveness from your Lord; then turn unto Him in repentance. He will send the sky upon you with abundant rain, and add strength to your strength. And turn not away, guilty."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Seek Forgiveness from Your Lord; Then Turn Unto Him in Repentance" — The Practical Form of the Call**

Hūd's third word to his people moves from the *denial* of the *transaction* to the *practical form* of the *call*: *istaghfirū rabbakum thumma ūbū ilayhi* — "seek forgiveness from your Lord; then turn unto him in repentance." The *form* is *identical* to the *form* of *verse 3* — the *form* with which the *sūrah* *opened* its *address* to the *Makkans* — and the *identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *series of abstract demands*; it is *a specific practice*, in the *sense of being* *the practice of seeking forgiveness and turning*, in the *sense of being* *the practice that the Makkans are being asked to perform now and that the ʿĀd were asked to perform then*. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* their *own* *call* *in its practical form* *spoken to another people*, and that the *hearing in the practical form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not a theory*; it is a *practice*, and that the *practice* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *conversion* of *verse 26* — the *subtraction of the idols* — has a *form*, and that the *form* is *the seeking and the turning*, in the way described in *verse 3's statement of the two verbs*.

**"He Will Send the Sky Upon You with Abundant Rain" — The Promise That Addresses the Drought**

The *second* *clause* is the *promise*: *yarsilān-samāʾa ʿalaykum ḍiddan* — "he will send the sky upon you with abundant rain." The *promise* is *addressed* to the *specific* *condition* of the *ʿĀd*: the *commentators* *note* that the *ʿĀd* were *experiencing* a *drought* — reported to have *lasted three years* — and that the *promise of rain* is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *call* is *not* a *general* *exhortation*; it is *a call addressed to the people's present need*, in the *sense of being* *the call that meets the people in their actual condition*. The sūrah is stating, in the promise, that the *rain* is *not* a *reward* in the *sense of being* *a bribe*; it is a *consequence* in the *sense of being* *the result of the turning*, and that the *consequence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *turning* is *not* a *spiritual* *act only*; it is a *cosmic* *act*, in the *sense of being* *the act that moves the sky*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 44's commands to the earth and the sky*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *promise*, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* being *asked* to *turn* for the *sake of the turning*; they are being *asked* to *turn* because the *turning* is the *condition of the rain*, and that the *condition of the rain* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *worldly good* and the *spiritual good* are *not separate* in the *sūrah's* *theology*; they are *one act seen from two sides*, in the way described in *verse 3's pairing of the goodly enjoyment and the bounty*.

**"And Add Strength to Your Strength" — The Promise of the Increase**

The *next* *clause* is the *second* *half* of the *promise*: *wa-yadiyadh ʿalā-quwwatikum quwwah* — "and add strength to your strength." The *zīyāda* — the *increase* — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *turning* is *not* a *subtraction only*; it is *an addition*, in the *sense of being* *the act that adds strength to the strength the people already have*, in the *sense of being* *the act that does not take the people's power and replace it but increases it*. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the *call* is *not* a *call to weakness*; it is a *call to strength*, and that the *call to strength* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* — a *people* known for their *physical* *power* (the *sūrah* will *describe* them in *7:69* as *the strongest of the nations*) — are *not* being *asked* to *surrender* their *strength*; they are being *asked* to *add* to it, and that the *not being asked to surrender* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *call to submission in the sense of diminishment*; it is a *call to submission in the sense of completion*, and that the *completion* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *taqwa* of the *sūrah* is *not* a *weakness*; it is a *strength*, in the way described in *verse 23's statement of the humble before their Lord*.

**"And Turn Not Away, Guilty" — The Warning That Closes the Word**

The *verse* *closes* with the *warning*: *wa-lā tufaqqarū jurrim* — "and turn not away, guilty." The *jurrīm* — guilty, culpable, the ones who ascribe partners — is the *word* that *returns* to the *characterization* of *verse 50* — the *characterization* of the *people* as *fabricators* — and the *return* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warning* is *not* a *general* *exhortation*; it is *a warning against the specific state*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the fabricators*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the ones who ascribe partners to God*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet* is *not* *finishing* his *word* on a *note of hope only*; he is *finishing* it on a *note of warning*, and that the *note of warning* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *one-sided* *address*; it is *the address that includes the consequence of the refusal*, in the way described in *verse 2's pairing of the warner and the bringer of glad tidings*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *rejection* of the *ʿĀd* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warning* is *not heeded*, and that the *not heeded* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern* of the *Noah story* is *repeating* in the *ʿĀd*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:53

> **They said, "O Hūd! You have not brought us a clear proof, and we shall not forsake our gods on your word, for we are not believers in you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"You Have Not Brought Us a Clear Proof" — The Objection That Denies the Burhān**

The ʿĀd's response to Hūd's call opens with the objection that denies the very existence of the proof: *qālū yā Hūdhum mā jīʾnāna bi-burhān* — "you have not brought us a clear proof." The *burhān* — the clear proof — is the word that the sūrah has used throughout for the prophet's evidence (vv. 17, 28, 63, 88), and the *ʿĀd's* *denial* of it is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables' frame* — the *frame* in which *evidence must be of a specific kind* — is *the frame that the burhān does not satisfy*. The *commentators* *note* that the *ʿĀd* were *not* *unable to see* the *clear proofs* brought by *Hūd*; they were *unable* *because of their obstinacy*, and the *unable because of obstinacy* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *denial* of the *burhān* is *not* a *denial of its absence*; it is a *denial of its sufficiency*, in the *sense of being* *the denial of the one who has set a standard that the burhān cannot meet*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *ʿĀd* are *in* the *state* of *verse 20* — the *state* of *not being able to hear and see* — and that the *in the state* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *notables of Noah* are *in the same state*, and that the *Makkans* are *in the same state*, and that the *three together* form the *sūrah's* complete *portrait* of the *refusal of the clear*.

**"We Shall Not Forsake Our Gods on Your Word" — The Attachment to the Idols, Named**

The *second* *clause* names the *specific* *attachment* that the *ʿĀd* *refuse* to *release*: *wa-lā nataruka ālihanā ʿalā qawlika* — "we shall not forsake our gods on your word." The *ʿalā qawlika* — "on your word" — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd's* *test* for the *releasing of the idols* is *the prophet's word*, and that the *test* is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *engaging with the content of the message*; they are *engaging with the authority of the messenger*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who require the messenger's word to be sufficient in itself to justify the releasing of the idols*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who set the standard of the messenger's word as the measure of the message's truth*. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *refusing the message*; they are *refusing the messenger's authority to justify the release*, and that the *refusing the authority* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *in* the *state* of *verse 12* — the *state* of *demanding a specific kind of proof* — and that the *in the state* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *demand of verse 12* is *the same demand*, in the *way* *described* throughout the *narrative middle*.

**"For We Are Not Believers in You" — The Closure of the Response**

The *verse* *closes* with the *closure*: *inna-nā bika lamunkirūn* — "for we are not believers in you." The *closure* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd's* *refusal* is *not* a *refusal of the message*; it is a *refusal of the messenger*, in the *sense of being* *the refusal that places the messenger outside the circle of belief*, in the *sense of being* *the refusal that makes the messenger's word insufficient by the fact of the refusal*. The sūrah is stating, in the closure, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *saying* "we do not believe the message"; they are *saying* "we do not believe in you," and that the *saying in you* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *confusing* the *messenger* with the *message*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 27's statement of the notables' confusion*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closure*, that the *ʿĀd's* *refusal* is *not* a *refusal that can be answered by a better argument*; it is a *refusal that has already decided*, and that the *already decided* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — the *claim of the evil* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not finished* with their *response*; they are *adding* to it, and that the *adding* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *refusal* is *not a* *single act*; it is a *course*, in the way described in *verse 32's statement of the notables' prolonging of the disputation*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:54

> **We say only that one of our gods has smitten you with evil." He said, "I call God as witness—and you be witness—that I am quit of that which you ascribe as partners**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"One of Our Gods Has Smitten You with Evil" — The Accusation That the Sūrah Records**

The ʿĀd add a second clause to their refusal, and the clause is the accusation that the sūrah records with particular care: *innā qālūnna ʾahadan min ālihanā ṣabahu ʿalayka sharrā* — "we say only that one of our gods has smitten you with evil." The *accusation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not merely refusing the message*; they are *explaining the prophet* by *attributing his condition to one of their idols*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who cast the prophet as a victim of their own gods*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who reduce the prophetic mission to a divine prank played by one of their deities*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *engaging* with the *prophet's claim*; they are *reclassifying* it, in the *sense of being* *the ones who turn the message into a medical or supernatural case*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who explain the prophet away by the operation of their own idols*. The *commentators* *note* that this *accusation* is *the same* *accusation* the *Makkans* *made* — the *accusation* of *madness* or *possession* (15:6; 23:70; 34:8; 37:36; 44:14; 68:51; 81:22) — and that the *sameness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd's* *accusation* is *not a* *unique* *invention*; it is *the standing accusation of the idolaters against their prophet*, and that the *standing accusation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in company with the ʿĀd* in the *way* *described* throughout the *narrative middle*.

**"I Call God as Witness" — The Prophet's Answer in the Form of the Oath**

Hūd's answer to the accusation is not a *defense* of his *mental state*; it is a *witnessing*: *qāla innī aḥudhu-llāh fa-hudūna maʿī* — "I call God as witness, and you be witnesses with me." The *ahud* — to take God as witness — is the *form* of the *oath* that the *prophet* *takes* when he is *accused of falsehood*, and the *form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *arguing* about his *condition*; he is *swearing* to his *sincerity*, in the *sense of being* *the one who places God as the witness to the truth of his mission*, in the *sense of being* *the one who invites the accusers to witness alongside him*. The sūrah is stating, in the form, that the *prophet* is *not* *defending himself against the accusation of evil*; he is *witnessing against the idols*, in the *sense of being* *the one who calls God to witness that he is free of the ascription*, and that the *witnessing against the idols* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *accusation* is *not met by a defense of the prophet*; it is *met by a disavowal of the idols*, and that the *disavowal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *position* is *not a* *personal* *position*; it is *the position of the one who is free of the ascription*, in the way described in *verse 31's fourth denial*.

**"That I Am Quit of That Which You Ascribe as Partners" — The Disavowal That the Sūrah States**

The *substance* of the *witnessing* is the *disavowal*: *innī barīʾun mimmā tuʿariku* — "that I am quit of that which you ascribe as partners." The *barāʾa* — the *disavowal* — is the *same* *word* the *sūrah* *used* in *verse 13* for the *Prophet's* *disassociation* from the *Makkans'* *fabrication*, and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *disavowal* is *not a* *prophet's* *individual* *act*; it is *the form of the prophetic disavowal itself*, in the *sense of being* *the form that every prophet takes when he is accused by the idolaters*, in the *sense of being* *the form that separates the prophet from the ascription*. The sūrah is stating, in the recurrence, that the *ʿĀd* are *hearing* the *same disavowal* that the *Makkans* will *hear* from the *Prophet*, and that the *hearing the same disavowal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern* is *invariant*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.
