The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *disavowal*, that the *prophet* is *not* *claiming* to be *above* the *idols*; he is *claiming* to be *free of them*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not ascribe them to God*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not participate in the ascription*, and that the *free of the ascription* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *mission* is *not a* *mission against the idols in the sense of attacking them*; it is *a mission of freedom from them*, and that the *freedom* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *conversion* of *verse 52* — the *turning to God* — is *not an* *addition of a new object*; it is a *freedom from the false objects*, in the way described in *verse 26's statement of the command as a subtraction*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:55

> **apart from Him. So scheme against me then, all together, and grant me no respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hūd's Challenge — Addressed to the People and Their Gods**

The verse records Hūd's defiance: *fa-kīdūnī jamīʿan thumma lā tunẓirūn* — "so scheme against me then, all together, and grant me no respite." The commentators note that the challenge is addressed both to his people and to their gods.

Al-Nuʿmānī records that the address includes the gods, and al-Kisāʾī explains that the challenge means: neither you nor your gods will be able to do anything. The commentators read the dual address as the point of the verse.

The imperative *kīdūnī* — scheme against me — is a direct invitation. The commentators note that Hūd does not merely claim safety but asks for the attempt, which is a stronger form of the claim.

The phrase *jamīʿan* — all together — is significant. The commentators read it as removing the excuse of insufficient numbers or coordination. The challenge is to the whole community acting as one.

The clause *thumma lā tunẓirūn* — and grant me no respite — completes the challenge. The commentators note the particle *thumma*, which indicates that the respite is withheld after the scheming, not instead of it. The challenge is to act without delay.

The commentators connect this to similar statements by other prophets about the beliefs of their peoples, at 6:19 and 6:78.

**The Ground of Hūd's Confidence**

The verse supplies the reason for the challenge, and al-Kisāʾī identifies it: Hūd provides them with the reason for his confidence in their inability to scheme against him, namely his firm and unshakeable reliance upon God.

The reliance is stated in the preceding clause, where Hūd declares his dissociation from what they associate with God. The commentators read the dissociation and the challenge as a single movement: having rejected their gods, he is not exposed to them.

The commentators note that the confidence is not in Hūd's own strength. The verse does not describe him as powerful or protected by any worldly means; it describes him as relying, and the reliance is what makes the challenge possible.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of trust throughout. The commentators read Hūd's challenge as the narrative enactment of the *tawakkul* that the sūrah commands elsewhere.

The verse's logic is worth setting out. Their gods have no power; God has all power; Hūd relies on God; therefore their scheming cannot reach him. The commentators read the challenge as inviting them to test the second premise.

**"There Is No Creature but That He Holds It by Its Forelock"**

The verse gives the ground of the reliance: *mā min dābbatin illā huwa ākhidhun bi-nāṣiyatihā* — "there is no creature that crawls, but that He holds it by its forelock." Al-Nuʿmānī explains that this is a metaphor to denote that all creatures are under God's Control and Authority.

The image is precise. The *nāṣiya* is the forelock, the hair at the front of the head, and grasping it is the gesture by which an animal is held and directed. The commentators read the metaphor as indicating control at the point of direction.

The word *dābba* denotes a crawling or moving creature, and the commentators note its comprehensiveness. The statement covers every living thing that moves, not merely human beings.

The commentators read the metaphor as answering the challenge's premise. If every creature is held by its forelock, then those who would scheme against Hūd are themselves held, and their scheming is within the control of the one Hūd relies upon.

The image also does theological work. The commentators note that it describes God's relation to creatures as immediate and particular rather than general — not sovereignty over a class but a hold on each.

**"Truly My Lord Is Upon a Straight Path"**

The verse closes with *inna rabbī ʿalā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm* — "truly my Lord is upon a straight path." Al-Ṭabarī explains that this means God is upon the path of justice, so that what He does is just.

The commentators note the unusual formulation. The straight path is ordinarily what creatures are commanded to follow; here it is attributed to God. The commentators read this as indicating that God's action conforms to rectitude rather than being its measure alone.

The connection to the preceding clause is close. If every creature is held by its forelock, the holding might seem arbitrary; the verse answers that the one who holds is upon a straight path. The commentators read the two clauses as completing each other.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss — the path of justice — is what gives the clause its force. The commentators read it as assuring that the control described is exercised justly, which is what makes reliance upon it reasonable rather than desperate.

The verse thus moves from challenge to ground to assurance. Hūd defies them; he relies on God; and the one he relies upon is just. The commentators read the sequence as the complete structure of prophetic confidence.

**The Verse in the Hūd Narrative**

Verse 55 sits at the climax of Hūd's exchange with his people, which runs from v. 50 to v. 57. The commentators read the sequence as moving from call, to rejection, to challenge, to final warning.

The challenge is the turning point. The commentators note that up to this verse Hūd has been arguing; from here he is defying. The shift marks the end of persuasion.

The verse's ground in reliance rather than strength is what distinguishes it from a mere boast. The commentators read it as showing that the prophet's safety is not his own achievement but the consequence of where he has placed his weight.

The connection to v. 56 is direct, since the forelock clause is the stated reason for the challenge. The commentators read the two verses as a single argument.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the conditions of speaking truth. Hūd's challenge is possible because of his reliance, and the verse is read as addressing those who must speak without worldly protection.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:56

> **Truly I trust in God, my Lord and your Lord. There is no creature that crawls, but that He holds it by its forelock. Truly my Lord is upon a straight path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Truly I Trust in God, My Lord and Your Lord" — The Statement That Completes the Invitation**

Hūd's answer to his own invitation closes with the statement that is the verse's foundation: *innī tawakkaltu ʿalā-llāhi rabbi wa-rabbikum* — "truly I trust in God, my Lord and your Lord." The *tawakkul* — the trust — is the word that names the prophet's *relation* to the *schemers' power*, and the *relation* is *not* a *relation of equality*; it is a *relation of submission to the One who is above the schemers*, in the *sense of being* *the relation of the one who has placed his weight on the One who holds all weights*. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *prophet* is *not* *meeting* the *schemers* *power* with *his own power*; he is *meeting* it with *the trust in the One who is their Lord as well*, and that the *meeting with the trust* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *alone* in the *confrontation*; he is *accompanied* by the *Lordship*, and that the *accompanied by the Lordship* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *schemers* are *not* *scheming* against the *prophet alone*; they are *scheming* against the *prophet and his Lord*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 30's statement of the prophet's question*.

**"My Lord and Your Lord" — The Phrase That Removes the Schemers from the Equation**

The *phrase* — *rabbi wa-rabbikum* — "my Lord and your Lord" — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* and the *schemers* are *not* in a *relation of two lords*; they are in a *relation of one Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the relation in which both are under the same Lordship*, in the *sense of being* *the relation in which the schemers' power is not their own but is held by the One who is also their Lord*. The sūrah is stating, in the phrase, that the *schemers* are *not* *above* the *prophet* by their *power*; they are *under* the *Lordship* that the *prophet* *trusts*, and that the *under the same Lordship* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *schemers'* *scheme* is *not a* *scheme against the prophet*; it is a *scheme under the Lordship*, and that the *under the Lordship* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *scheme* is *not* *free* in the *sense of being* *unconstrained*; it is *constrained* by the *Lordship*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 4's statement of the return to God*.

**"There Is No Creature That Crawls, but That He Holds It by Its Forelock" — The Image That the Sūrah Uses for the Lordship**

The *second* *clause* is the *image* that the *sūrah* uses for the *scope* of the *Lordship*: *mā min dābbatin illā ʿalayhi bi-nāḍīhā* — "there is no creature that crawls, but that He holds it by its forelock." The *nāḍī* — the forelock, the tuft of the forehead — is the *word* that names the *point* by which the *creature* is *held*, and the *image* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lordship* is *not a* *distant* *sovereignty*; it is *a holding* in the *sense of being* *the act that holds each creature by the specific point of its life*, in the *sense of being* *the act that knows each creature at the point where it can be held*. The sūrah is stating, in the image, that the *Lordship* is *not* a *general* *fact*; it is a *specific* *act* for *each creature*, and that the *specific act* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *schemers* are *not* *outside* the *holding*; they are *held* by it, in the *way* *described* in *verse 6's statement of the provision of every crawling creature*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *image*, that the *schemers'* *power* is *not their own*; it is *held* by the *One* who *holds* them *by their forelock*, and that the *held by the forelock* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *scheme* is *not* a *free* *act*; it is a *held* *act*, and that the *held act* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *schemers* are *not* *scheming*; they are *being held while they think they are scheming*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 5's statement of the knowledge of what lies within breasts*.

**"Truly My Lord Is Upon a Straight Path" — The Closing That Names the State of the Lord**

The *verse* *closes* with the *statement* about the *Lord's* *state*: *wa-inna rabbi ʿalā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm* — "truly my Lord is upon a straight path." The *ṣirāṭ mustaqīm* — the straight path — is the *word* that the *Fātiḥah* *uses* for the *path* that the *believer* *asks God to guide him to* (1:6), and the *use* here, *applied to the Lord*, is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not* *upon a* *crooked* *path*; he is *upon the straight*, in the *sense of being* *the One whose way is not crooked*, in the *sense of being* *the One whose justice does not deviate*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet's* *trust* is *not a* *trust in an uncertain Lord*; it is a *trust in the Lord whose path is straight*, and that the *trust in the straight* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *trusting blindly*; he is *trusting by knowledge of the Lord's state*, and that the *trusting by knowledge* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *tawakkul* is *not* a *suspension of the intellect*; it is a *placement of the intellect on the straight*, in the way described in *verse 23's statement of the humble before their Lord*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *schemers' response and the command* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *trust* is *not* *the end of the story*; it is the *ground* on which the *next* *words* are *spoken*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:57

> **But if you turn away, [know that] I have delivered unto you the message wherewith I have been sent by Him. My Lord will cause you to be succeeded by a people other than yourselves, and you harm Him not in the least. Truly my Lord is Watcher over all things."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"If You Turn Away" — the Message Already Delivered**

The verse opens with a conditional: *fa-in tawallaw fa-qad ablaghtukum mā ursiltu bihī ʿalaykum* — "but if you turn away, [know that] I have delivered unto you the message wherewith I have been sent by Him."

The commentators note the structure. The turning away is anticipated, and the response is not argument but a statement that the delivery has occurred. The commentators read this as marking the end of the messenger's responsibility.

The phrase *fa-qad ablaghtukum* — I have delivered unto you — uses the perfect tense. The commentators read it as indicating a completed act: the message has been given, and the turning away does not undo the giving.

The clause *mā ursiltu bihī ʿalaykum* — that wherewith I have been sent by Him — locates the message's source outside Hūd. The commentators read this as significant: what has been delivered is not his opinion, and its rejection is not a verdict on him.

The verse's function is to settle the account between the messenger and his people. The commentators read it as the prophet's final statement of what he owes and what he has paid.

**"My Lord Will Cause You to Be Succeeded by Another People"**

The verse continues: *wa-yastakhlifu rabbī qawman ghayrakum* — "my Lord will cause you to be succeeded by a people other than yourselves." Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī explain that this means God will destroy the ʿĀd and replace them with a people who believe in God's Oneness and worship Him alone.

The commentators supply the parallels: 7:69, 9:39, and 47:38. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing replacement as a standard consequence of rejection.

The verb *yastakhlif* denotes making one party succeed another, and the commentators note that it carries the sense of inheritance. The ʿĀd's place will be occupied by others.

The phrase *ghayrakum* — other than yourselves — is emphatic. The commentators read it as indicating that the replacement will not be a reformed version of the ʿĀd but a different people entirely.

The verse does not specify who the replacement will be. The commentators note that the sūrah's audience would have understood the reference to include themselves, and that the warning is thus addressed to the present as much as to the past.

**"You Harm Him Not in the Least" — Two Readings**

The verse states: *wa-lā taḍurrūnahu shayʾā* — "and you harm Him not in the least." Al-Ṭūsī records two readings, and both are preserved.

On the first reading, Hūd conveys the fact that they do not harm God in any fashion whatsoever by turning away from Him, nor does any harm come to Him if He destroys them, for He did not create them out of His need for them in the first place.

On the alternate reading, it can mean that they will not have the ability to cause God any harm if He causes another people to replace them. The commentators note that this reading focuses on incapacity rather than on needlessness.

The two readings differ in emphasis but not in substance. The first grounds the statement in God's independence from creation; the second in the creatures' inability to affect Him. The commentators preserve both.

Al-Ṭūsī supplies the supporting passages for the phrase: 3:144, 3:176, 9:39, and 47:32. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing the claim as a fixed theological premise.

The first reading's ground is worth stating plainly. God did not create them out of need, so their departure costs Him nothing. The commentators read this as answering any suggestion that worship benefits God or that its withdrawal injures Him.

**"My Lord Is Watcher Over All Things"**

The verse closes with *inna rabbī ʿalā kulli shayʾin ḥafīẓ* — "truly my Lord is Watcher over all things." The commentators gloss *ḥafīẓ* as one who watches, preserves, and keeps account.

The comprehensiveness of *kulli shayʾ* — all things — is what the commentators emphasize. The watching is not limited to the ʿĀd or to Hūd; it extends to everything.

The attribute functions as the ground of the preceding statements. If God is Watcher over all things, then the turning away is observed, the delivery is recorded, and the replacement is prepared. The commentators read the clause as securing the whole verse.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's opening statement of divine knowledge at v. 5. The commentators read the two as framing the sūrah's treatment of what is hidden and what is disclosed.

The pastoral edge is direct. The verse assures the messenger that the delivery has not gone unobserved, and warns the people that the turning away has not gone unrecorded. The commentators read the single attribute as doing both.

**The Verse as the End of Persuasion**

Verse 57 is Hūd's last word to his people, and the commentators note that it differs in kind from what precedes it. The earlier verses argue; this one reports.

The commentators read the shift as marking the end of the prophetic task. The message has been delivered; the response has been given; what remains is not persuasion but consequence.

The verse's three statements work together. The delivery is complete; the replacement is coming; and the harm is nil. The commentators read the sequence as removing every ground on which the ʿĀd might have thought their rejection mattered to God.

The connection to v. 58 is immediate. There the Command comes and Hūd and the believers are saved through a mercy, and the ʿĀd are destroyed. The commentators read the verse as the announcement of what v. 58 executes.

The verse's final clause is what the commentators draw out for the present audience. The Watcher over all things is watching now, and the delivery being made to the sūrah's hearers is being recorded as Hūd's was.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:58

> **And when Our Command came, We saved Hūd and those who believed with him through a Mercy from Us; and We saved them from a grave punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Command Came"**

The verse opens with the arrival of the decisive moment: *wa-lammā jāʾa amrunā* — "and when Our Command came." The commentators explain that the Command here refers to God's Punishment, which Hūd had promised would be visited upon the ʿĀd.

The commentators note that the same formulation appears at v. 66 with reference to the Thamūd, and read the recurrence as a fixed way of marking the transition from warning to execution in the sūrah's narratives.

The word *amr* — command, affair, decree — is what the commentators emphasize. The punishment is not described as a natural event but as something commanded, which locates its origin in the divine will rather than in circumstance.

The particle *lammā* — when — indicates that the arrival was expected and did in fact occur. The commentators read it as marking the fulfillment of what Hūd announced at v. 57, where he said his Lord would cause them to be succeeded by another people.

The verse's compression is notable. The ʿĀd's destruction is not narrated; only the Command's arrival and the salvation are given. The commentators read this as characteristic of the sūrah's later narratives, which become progressively terser.

**"We Saved Hūd and Those Who Believed With Him"**

The verse states the salvation: *najjaynā Hūdan wa-lladhīna āmanū maʿahū* — "We saved Hūd and those who believed with him." The commentators note the verb *najjā*, which denotes rescue or deliverance, and its attribution to God alone.

The phrase *alladhīna āmanū maʿahū* — those who believed with him — identifies the saved by belief rather than by kinship. The commentators connect this to the sūrah's repeated insistence, at vv. 46 and 48, that family does not secure standing.

The commentators note that the number of the saved is not given here. Other passages supply figures, and the commentators direct the reader to the parallel accounts. The verse itself is concerned with the fact of salvation rather than its scale.

The order of the names is worth noting. Hūd is named first, and the believers are described as being *with* him. The commentators read this as indicating that the community's identity derives from its relation to the messenger.

The verb's plurality — *najjaynā*, We saved — is the Qur'an's standard form for divine action. The commentators read it as indicating majesty rather than plurality, and note its recurrence throughout the sūrah's salvation statements.

**"Through a Mercy From Us" — the Ground of the Salvation**

The verse qualifies the salvation: *bi-raḥmatin minnā* — "through a Mercy from Us." The commentators note that the salvation is attributed to mercy rather than to the believers' merit.

Al-Qushayrī observes that, as was the case with Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and his followers are said to have been saved through a Mercy from God. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing that salvation in the sūrah's narratives is consistently described as mercy.

The preposition *bi-* indicates the means. The commentators read this as significant: the mercy is not merely the motive but the instrument. The salvation is accomplished by mercy rather than earned and then rewarded.

The phrase *minnā* — from Us — locates the mercy's source. The commentators read it as excluding any other origin, which is consistent with the sūrah's insistence that there is no protector apart from God.

The commentators connect this to v. 115's assurance that God neglects not the reward of the virtuous. The commentators read the two together: the reward is not neglected, but the salvation itself is mercy rather than payment.

**"From a Grave Punishment"**

The verse adds a second object of the saving: *wa-najjaynāhum min ʿadhābin ghalīẓ* — "and We saved them from a grave punishment." The commentators note that the verb is repeated, which emphasizes the deliverance.

The adjective *ghalīẓ* — grave, severe, heavy — is what the commentators emphasize. It is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for punishment of particular weight, and the commentators read its application here as indicating the severity of what the ʿĀd received.

The commentators note that the verse describes the believers as saved twice over — saved positively, and saved from something. The commentators read the doubling as indicating that the salvation was complete: they were not merely spared but delivered.

The connection to v. 60 is instructive. There the ʿĀd are pursued by a curse in this world and on the Day of Resurrection; here the believers are saved from a grave punishment. The commentators read the two verses as the paired outcomes.

The verse does not describe the punishment itself. The commentators note that the ʿĀd's destruction is given at 26:130 and elsewhere, and that this verse is concerned with the believers' deliverance from it rather than with its manner.

**The Verse as the Pattern for the Sūrah's Narratives**

Verse 58 establishes the pattern that the sūrah's remaining narratives follow. The commentators note that each subsequent account — Ṣāliḥ, Abraham, Lot, Shuʿayb — ends with a comparable statement of salvation.

The pattern has four elements: the Command arrives; the messenger and the believers are saved; the salvation is through mercy; and the disbelievers are destroyed. The commentators read the repetition as deliberate rather than formulaic.

The consistency is what gives the sūrah's argument its force. The commentators observe that the audience is shown the same outcome repeatedly, so that by the time the present case is reached, the pattern is unmistakable.

The verse's attribution of salvation to mercy rather than merit is what prevents the pattern from becoming a transaction. The commentators read this as addressing any assumption that the believers earned their deliverance.

The pastoral application the commentators draw concerns the audience's own position. The pattern is not history but a rule, and the rule applies to those hearing the sūrah as much as to those in it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:59

> **That was ʿĀd. They rejected the signs of their Lord, disobeyed the messengers, and followed the command of every stubborn tyrant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Was ʿĀd" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Completed Story**

The sūrah now steps out of the narrative to state, in its own voice, the *characterization* of the *completed story*: *dhālika ʿĀd* — "that was ʿĀd." The *dhālika* — "that" — is the *word* that *points* to the *story* as a *whole*, and the *pointing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *story* is *not a* *series of events*; it is *a case*, in the *sense of being* *the case that the sūrah has stated in full*, in the *sense of being* *the case that the characterization will summarize*. The sūrah is stating, in the pointing, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *a people in the distance*; they are *a case before the reader*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *pointing*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *pointing* *applied to the ʿĀd*, and that the *applied to the ʿĀd* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the same case*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.

**"They Rejected the Signs of Their Lord" — The First Mark of the Characterization**

The *first* *mark* of the *characterization* is the *rejection of the signs*: *kafarū bi-āyāti rabbi-him* — "they rejected the signs of their Lord." The *āyāt* — the *signs* — is the *word* that the *Qur'an* uses for the *evidence* of the *divine* *act*, and the *rejection* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *without* the *signs*; they are *with* the *signs* and *against* them, in the *sense of being* *the ones who see the signs and refuse them*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who have the evidence present and do not engage it*. The sūrah is stating, in the mark, that the *ʿĀd's* *refusal* is *not a* *refusal of the absent*; it is a *refusal of the present*, in the way described in *verse 28's statement of the blindness to the clear proof*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *mark*, that the *Makkans'* *refusal* is *the same refusal*, and that the *same refusal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *case* is *not over*; it is *in operation* in the *Makkans*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the folded breast*.

**"Disobeyed the Messengers" — The Second Mark, and the Word That Includes the Prophet**

The *second* *mark* is the *disobedience of the messengers*: *wa-ʿaaw al-rusul* — "they disobeyed the messengers." The *plural* — *al-rusul* — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *disobedience* is *not a* *disobedience of a single messenger*; it is a *disobedience of the office*, in the *sense of being* *the disobedience that targets the prophetic function rather than the individual prophet*, in the *sense of being* *the disobedience that the ʿĀd directed at Hūd as the representative of the office*. The sūrah is stating, in the plural, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *refusing Hūd as a person*; they are *refusing the office he carries*, and that the *refusing the office* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *refusal of the Prophet* is *a refusal of the office*, in the way described in *verse 12's statement of the prophet as only a warner*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *mark*, that the *disobedience* is *not a* *failure of the messenger*; it is *a choice of the disobeider*, and that the *choice* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part*.

**"Followed the Command of Every Stubborn Tyrant" — The Third Mark, and the Word That Names the Social Structure**

The *third* *mark* is the *following of the stubborn tyrant*: *wa-attabaʿū amra kulli jawvā ʿattīd* — "they followed the command of every stubborn tyrant." The *jawvā ʿattīd* — the *stubborn tyrant* — is the *word* that names the *social structure* that the *ʿĀd* *followed*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *ʿĀd's* *refusal* is *not a* *refusal of individuals*; it is a *refusal of the structure*, in the *sense of being* *the refusal that follows the tyrant rather than the truth*, in the *sense of being* *the refusal that makes the tyrant's command the measure of the right*. The sūrah is stating, in the mark, that the *ʿĀd* are *not* *refusing the message because it is false*; they are *refusing it because the tyrant refuses it*, and that the *refusing because the tyrant refuses* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *social structure* is *the form of the refusal*, in the way described in *verse 27's statement of the notables' social epistemology*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *mark*, that the *Makkans'* *notables* are *the stubborn tyrants* of the *Makkans'* *case*, and that the *being the tyrants* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *structure* is *not over*; it is *in operation* in the *Makkans*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*, and that the *in operation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — the *curse* and the *refrain* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *case* is *closed* by the *verdict*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:60

> **And they were pursued by a curse in this world, and [shall be] on the Day of Resurrection. Behold! Truly ʿĀd disbelieved in their Lord. Behold! Away with ʿĀd, the people of Hūd!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Pursued by a Curse in This World"**

The verse opens with the ʿĀd's condition: *wa-utbiʿū fī hādhihi d-dunyā laʿnatan* — "and they were pursued by a curse in this world." The commentators explain that the curse against the people who rejected Hūd refers to their being distanced from God's Mercy.

Al-Baghawī's gloss is the operative one. The commentators read *laʿna* not as a malediction uttered but as a state of removal from mercy, which is what the Qur'an's usage of the term consistently indicates.

The verb *utbiʿū* — they were followed, pursued — is what the commentators emphasize. The curse is not a single pronouncement but something that follows them, which the commentators read as indicating persistence.

The phrase *fī hādhihi d-dunyā* — in this world — locates the curse in the present life. The commentators read this as indicating that the consequence of rejection is not deferred entirely to the hereafter.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of the ʿĀd's destruction, which occurred within history and was visible to subsequent generations. The curse in this world is the destruction itself and its enduring report.

**"And [Shall Be] on the Day of Resurrection"**

The verse continues: *wa-yawma l-qiyāma* — "and [shall be] on the Day of Resurrection." The commentators note the elision: the verb is not repeated, and the clause depends on what precedes.

The elision is significant. The commentators read the omission as indicating continuity — the curse that pursued them in this world continues into the next without interruption or new pronouncement.

The two locations are what the commentators emphasize. The curse is not confined to this world or to the next; it spans both. The commentators read this as answering any assumption that death ends the consequence.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's eschatological unit at vv. 105–108, where the two abidings are described. The commentators read the verse as a particular instance of the general pattern stated there.

The verse's structure is worth noting. It states the curse's two locations without describing either. The commentators read the reticence as indicating that the locations are known and need no elaboration.

**"Behold! Truly ʿĀd Disbelieved in Their Lord"**

The verse turns to a declaration: *alā inna ʿĀdan kafarū rabbahum* — "behold! Truly ʿĀd disbelieved in their Lord." The commentators note the particle *alā*, which calls attention and marks what follows as a matter of record.

The declaration is summary. After the narrative of Hūd's call, the ʿĀd's refusal, and their destruction, the verse states the conclusion in a single clause. The commentators read the compression as indicating that the case is settled.

The phrase *kafarū rabbahum* — disbelieved in their Lord — uses the accusative without a preposition. The commentators read the construction as indicating ingratitude as well as disbelief, since the verb can carry both senses.

The naming of the Lord is significant. The commentators read it as indicating that the ʿĀd's disbelief was not ignorance of a deity but rejection of the one who created and provided for them.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's repeated emphasis on God's provision. The ʿĀd were given strength, buildings, and wealth; the verse states that they disbelieved in the one who gave them.

**"Away With ʿĀd, the People of Hūd"**

The verse closes with an imprecation: *alā buʿdan li-ʿĀdin qawmi Hūd* — "behold! Away with ʿĀd, the people of Hūd!" The commentators note that the phrase functions as a refrain throughout this sūrah.

The commentators trace the refrain's three occurrences: here in connection with the ʿĀd, then with the Thamūd at v. 68, and finally with the Midianites at v. 95. The commentators read the repetition as structuring the sūrah's narratives.

The word *buʿd* denotes distance, and the commentators read the imprecation as pronouncing removal rather than destruction. The commentators connect it to the gloss of *laʿna* as distance from mercy, and read the two as expressing the same reality.

The phrase *qawmi Hūd* — the people of Hūd — identifies them by their relation to the messenger. The commentators read this as significant: they are named not by their lineage or their city but by the prophet they rejected.

The parallel at v. 44 is instructive, where the identical phrase is used of Noah's people. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing the imprecation as the sūrah's standard closing for a destroyed community.

**The Verse as the ʿĀd Narrative's Verdict**

Verse 60 concludes the ʿĀd narrative that begins at v. 50. The commentators note that it does so by stating the verdict rather than narrating the destruction.

The verse's four clauses work together. The curse in this world and the next; the declaration of disbelief; and the imprecation. The commentators read the sequence as moving from consequence to cause to pronouncement.

The refrain's function is structural. The commentators observe that by the third occurrence the audience expects it, and that the expectation is part of the sūrah's argument: the pattern is fixed, and the present audience is inside it.

The connection to v. 59 is close, since there the ʿĀd are described as following the command of every stubborn tyrant. The commentators read the two verses together: the following was the cause, and the curse is the effect.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the refrain's availability. The imprecation is pronounced on a named people, and the sūrah's audience is left to consider whether the same pronouncement applies to them.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:61

> **And to Thamūd, their brother Ṣāliḥ. He said, "O my people! Worship God! You have no god other than Him. He brought you forth from the earth and settled you therein. So seek forgiveness from Him; then turn unto Him in repentance. Truly my Lord is Nigh, Responsive."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And to Thamūd, Their Brother Ṣāliḥ" — The Third Story, and the Word That Opens It**

The sūrah opens the third story of its narrative middle with the same form it used for ʿĀd: *wa-ilā Thamūdin akhāhum Ṣāliḥ* — "and to Thamūd, their brother Ṣāli." The *identity of form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *stories* are *not* *different kinds of events*; they are *the same kind* in the *sense of being* *the same pattern of the brother sent to the people*, in the *sense of being* *the same pattern of the call and the refusal and the command*. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *a different case* from the *ʿĀd*; they are *the same case* in the *sense of being* *the case of the brother refused*, in the way described in *verse 50's statement of the pattern*. The *commentators* *note* that *Ṣāliḥ* was *an Arabian prophet* not mentioned in the *Bible*, and that the *Thamūd* *inhabited* a *rocky plain* in *western Arabia* between the *Ḥijāz* and *Syria*, and that they were *descendants of Noah* in the *way* the *ʿĀd* were, and that the *descendants of Noah* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *nations* of the *narrative middle* are *not* *separate peoples*; they are *one people divided by time*, in the way described in *verse 48's statement of the communities that arise from those with Noah*.

**"He Brought You Forth from the Earth and Settled You Therein" — The Argument from the Origin**

Ṣāliḥ's call to his people includes an *argument* that the calls of *Noah* and *Hūd* did not state in the same form: *khalaqakum min al-arḍi wa-stakharakum fīhā* — "he brought you forth from the earth and settled you therein." The *argument* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not* a *series of commands*; it is *a call with a ground*, in the *sense of being* *the call that states the relation of the people to the Lord in the form of their origin*, in the *sense of being* *the call that reminds the people that they are not self-originated*. The sūrah is stating, in the argument, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *a people without an origin*; they are *a people brought forth from the earth*, and that the *brought forth* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd's* *claim to self-sufficiency* — the *claim* that the *ʿĀd* made in *their* *refusal* — is *not a* *claim that the earth supports*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 6's statement of the provision held by God*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *argument*, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *settled in the earth by their own power*; they are *settled by the Lord*, and that the *settled by the Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd's* *use of the earth* is *not their own right*; it is *a trust held by the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 52's statement of the strength that God adds*.

**"Seek Forgiveness from Him; Then Turn Unto Him in Repentance" — The Practical Form, Repeated**

The *second* *part* of the *call* is the *practical form* that the *sūrah* has *stated* in *verse 3* and *verse 52*: *istaghfirūhu thumma ūbū ilayhi* — "seek forgiveness from him; then turn unto him in repentance." The *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *practical form* is *not a* *variable*; it is *the constant* of the *call*, in the *sense of being* *the form that every prophet gives when he calls his people to the conversion*, in the *sense of being* *the form that the Makkans are being asked to perform now and that the Thamūd were asked to perform then*. The sūrah is stating, in the recurrence, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* their *own call in its practical form* *spoken to a third people*, and that the *hearing in the practical form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not a theory*; it is a *practice*, in the way described in *verse 52's statement of the practical form of the call*.

**"Truly My Lord Is Nigh, Responsive" — The Closing That Names the Two Attributes**

The *verse* *closes* with the *naming* of the *two* *attributes* that *govern* the *call*: *innī rabbi qarībun mujīb* — "truly my Lord is Nigh, Responsive." The *qurb* — the *nearness* — and the *ijābah* — the *response* — are the *two attributes* that the *sūrah* *names* for the *Lord* in this *verse*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not* *distant* from the *supplicant*; he is *nigh*, in the *sense of being* *the One who is close to the one who calls*, in the *sense of being* *the One who answers the call when it is made*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *calling into a distance*; they are *calling into a nearness*, and that the *calling into the nearness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *duʿāʾ* — the *supplication* — is *not a* *cry into the void*; it is *a cry into the nearness that answers*, in the way described in *2:186* — the *way* in which God states: "When My servants ask thee about Me, truly I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me." The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *Thamūd's* *response* and the *she-camel* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *nearness* is *not* *the end of the story*; it is *the ground* on which the *next* *words* are *spoken*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:62

> **They said, "O Ṣāli! You were a source of hope among us before this. Do you forbid us to worship that which our fathers worshipped, while we remain in grave doubt about that to which you call us?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"You Were a Source of Hope Among Us Before This" — The Acknowledgment That the Sūrah Records**

The Thamūd's response to Ṣāliḥ's call opens with an *acknowledgment* that the sūrah records with particular care: *qālū yā Ṣāliḥ qad kunta fi-nā ma-rjāan qabla hādha* — "you were a source of hope among us before this." The *marjāʾ* — the *source of hope*, the *one looked to* — is the *word* that names the *position* that *Ṣāliḥ* *held* in the *Thamūd's* *society* before the *call*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that *Ṣāliḥ* was *not* an *outsider* to the *Thamūd*; he was *one of their most noble and respected figures*, in the *sense of being* *the one they looked to for leadership*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose word carried weight in their assembly*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *refusing an unknown*; they are *refusing the one they had hoped in*, and that the *refusing the one they had hoped in* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *refusal* is *not a* *refusal of the foreign*; it is *a refusal of the domestic*, in the way described in *Noah's address of verse 28*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *acknowledgment*, that the *Thamūd's* *refusal* is *more* *grave* than the *refusal of an outsider*, because it is *the refusal of the one who was the hope*, and that the *more grave* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *case* is *not the case of the stranger*; it is *the case of the hope betrayed*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.

**"Do You Forbid Us to Worship That Which Our Fathers Worshipped" — The Appeal to the Fathers, Named**

The *second* *clause* of the *response* is the *appeal* that the *sūrah* will *hear* *repeated* in *several* *stories*: *a-taḥarramnā an naʿbuda mā ʿabada ābāʾunā* — "do you forbid us to worship that which our fathers worshipped?" The *appeal* to the *fathers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd's* *test* for the *legitimacy of the worship* is *not the content of the worship*; it is *the history of the worship*, in the *sense of being* *the test that measures the new by the old*, in the *sense of being* *the test that requires the new to be continuous with the old to be legitimate*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *engaging with the argument of verse 61* — the *argument from the origin* — but with the *authority of the fathers*, and that the *engaging with the authority of the fathers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd* are *in* the *state* of *verse 109* — the *state* of *worshipping only as the fathers worshipped* — and that the *in the state* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *appeal to the fathers* is *the same appeal*, in the way described in *2:170* — the *way* in which the *Qur'an* answers the *appeal*: "What! Even though their fathers understood nothing, and were not rightly guided?" The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *appeal to the fathers* is *not an argument*; it is a *refusal to argue*, in the sense of being *the refusal that places the fathers' worship above the question of its truth*, and that the *refusal to argue* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part*.

**"While We Remain in Grave Doubt About That to Which You Call Us" — The Doubt, Stated as a Position**

The *response* *closes* with the *statement of the doubt*: *wa-inna-nā fī mā taʿūduna ilayhi la-fī hakatun balīḥah* — "while we remain in grave doubt about that to which you call us." The *hakat* — the *doubt*, the *perplexity* — is the *word* that names the *Thamūd's* *state* toward the *call*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd* are *not* *without* a *response* to the *call*; they are *with* a *response*, and the *response* is *the doubt*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the one who is perplexed by the call*, in the *sense of being* *the state of the one who has not resolved the call into acceptance or refusal*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Thamūd's* *doubt* is *not a* *doubt that will be resolved by more evidence*; it is *a doubt that is the form of the refusal*, in the way described in *verse 20's statement of the spiritual deafness and blindness*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *open* to the *call*; they are *closed* in the *form of the doubt*, and that the *closed in the form of the doubt* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — *Ṣāliḥ's* *answer* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not finished* with the *people*; he is *turning to the clear proof*, in the way described in *verse 28's statement of the burhān*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:63

> **He said, "O my people! What think you: if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord, and He has granted me Mercy from Himself, then who can help me against God, were I to disobey Him? For you would increase me in naught but loss."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"What Think You: If I Stand Upon a Clear Proof" — The Rhetorical Question, Repeated in Its Invariant Form**

Ṣāliḥ's answer to the Thamūd's doubt is the *same* *rhetorical question* that *Noah* asked in *verse 28*: *yā qawmī afa-ḍālltumum in kuntu ʿalā-burhānin min rabbi* — "O my people! what think you: if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord." The *identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *question* is *not a* *prophet's* *individual* *formulation*; it is *the form of the prophetic presentation itself*, in the *sense of being* *the form that every prophet takes when he presents the burhān to his doubting people*, in the *sense of being* *the form that places the question in the people's hands*. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Thamūd* are *hearing* the *same question* that *Noah's people* *heard*, and that the *hearing the same question* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern* is *invariant*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Thamūd's* *doubt* of *verse 62* is *not met by a new argument*; it is *met by the same presentation*, and that the *met by the same presentation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *burhān* is *not a* *variable* *evidence*; it is *the constant* of the *prophetic office*, in the way described in *verse 17's statement of the clear proof*.

**"He Has Granted Me Mercy from Himself" — The Warrant, Repeated**

The *second* *clause* is the *warrant* that *Noah* *stated* in *verse 28*: *wa-wahaba lī minhu raḥmah* — "and He has granted me mercy from Himself." The *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warrant* is *not a* *prophet's* *individual* *claim*; it is *the standing warrant of the prophetic office*, in the *sense of being* *the warrant that every prophet states when he presents the burhān*, in the *sense of being* *the warrant that places the source of the burhān in the divine mercy*. The sūrah is stating, in the recurrence, that the *Ṣāliḥ's* *claim to prophethood* is *not a* *claim to personal merit*; it is a *claim to the mercy*, in the way described in *verse 51's statement of the reward that lies only with the One who originated the prophet*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *being asked* to *trust* in *Ṣāliḥ's* *merit*; they are *being asked* to *trust* in the *mercy that granted him the burhān*, and that the *being asked to trust in the mercy* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not a* *call to a person*; it is *a call to the mercy that sent the person*, in the way described in *verse 2's statement of the warner on God's behalf*.

**"Who Can Help Me Against God, Were I to Disobey Him?" — The Question That Inverts the Frame**

The *central* *clause* is the *question* that *Noah* *asked* in *verse 30*: *fa-man yanṣurunī min Allāh in ʿaṣaytuhū* — "who can help me against God, were I to disobey him?" The *question* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *afraid* of the *Thamūd's* *power*; he is *stating* that his *accountability* is *not to the people*; it is to the *Lord*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 30's statement of the question that inverts the frame*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Thamūd's* *doubt* is *not met by a defense of the prophet*; it is *met by the statement of the prophet's accountability to the Lord*, and that the *met by the statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *position* is *not a* *position that the Thamūd can threaten*, in the way described in *verse 56's statement of the trust in the Lord*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *in a position to demand* from *Ṣāliḥ* a *different form of the call*; they are *in a position to receive the call or refuse it*, and that the *in a position to receive or refuse* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part*.

**"For You Would Increase Me in Naught but Loss" — The Closing That Names the Consequence**

The *verse* *closes* with the *statement of the consequence*: *inna-kum lā tazīdūnī illā ḍalālā* — "for you would increase me in naught but loss." The *ḍalāl* — the *loss*, the *misguidance* — is the *word* that names the *state* that the *Thamūd's* *doubt* would *produce in the prophet* if he *yielded to it*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet* is *not* *immune* to the *effect of the doubt*; he is *the one who states that the doubt would increase him in loss*, in the *sense of being* *the one who knows that yielding to the doubt would be a misguidance for him*, in the *sense of being* *the one who states the consequence of the yielding*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *prophet* is *not* *refusing the Thamūd's doubt out of pride*; he is *refusing it out of knowledge of its consequence*, and that the *refusing out of knowledge* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *firmness* is *not a* *weakness of feeling*; it is *a strength of knowing*, in the way described in *verse 56's statement of the trust placed on the straight*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *she-camel* and the *hamstringing* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd* are *not* *persuaded* by the *question*; they are *demanding a sign*, in the way described in *verse 32's statement of the notables' challenge*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:64

> **O my people! This she-camel of God is a sign unto you. Leave her to graze freely on God's earth, and cause her no harm, lest you be seized by a punishment nigh."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This She-Camel of God Is a Sign Unto You"**

The verse records Ṣāliḥ's presentation of the sign: *yā qawmi hādhihī nāqatu Llāhi lakum āya* — "O my people! This she-camel of God is a sign unto you." The commentators direct the reader to 7:73c for the fuller account.

The commentators explain the sign's origin: Ṣāliḥ brought the pregnant she-camel forth from a large rock in accordance with the Thamūd's request to produce a miracle in order to prove his claim to prophethood. The commentators supply the parallel at 26:154.

The commentators note the three designations the Qur'an gives the camel, and read them as complementary. Here it is called a sign; at 7:73 it is called a clear proof; and at 54:27 it is referred to as a trial for them.

The three terms are worth distinguishing. A sign indicates; a clear proof establishes; a trial tests. The commentators read the progression as describing what the camel does for different audiences — for the seeker it indicates, for the objector it establishes, and for the rejecter it becomes the occasion of ruin.

The phrase *nāqatu Llāh* — the she-camel of God — is what the commentators emphasize. The genitive attribution is the camel's distinguishing feature; it is not merely an animal but one belonging to God, and its inviolability follows from that.

**"Leave Her to Graze Freely on God's Earth"**

The verse gives the first command: *fa-dharūhā taʾkul fī arḍi Llāh* — "leave her to graze freely on God's earth." The commentators note that this injunction, recounted here and at 7:73 and 26:155, makes up one part of the command.

The commentators identify the other part at 26:155: *She shall have drink and you shall drink on an appointed day*, with the parallel at 54:28. The commentators read the two parts together as a complete arrangement governing the camel's provision.

The phrase *fī arḍi Llāh* — on God's earth — is what the commentators emphasize. The earth is described as God's, which the commentators read as removing any claim of ownership that might exclude the camel.

The commentators note that the command is permissive rather than restrictive. Ṣāliḥ does not ask that the camel be fed or housed; he asks only that she be left to graze. The commentators read the modesty of the request as significant.

The sharing arrangement at 26:155 is what the commentators draw out. The water is divided by appointed day, which the commentators read as establishing a fair allocation rather than a preference for the camel.

**"Cause Her No Harm"**

The verse adds the prohibition: *wa-lā tamassūhā bi-sūʾ* — "and cause her no harm." The commentators note the phrase's literal sense of touching with evil, and read it as covering any injury.

The commentators connect the prohibition to the parallel at 7:73 and 26:155, and note that the Qur'an's accounts consistently present the command in these terms.

The consequence is stated immediately: *fa-yaʾkhudhakum ʿadhābun qarīb* — "lest you be seized by a punishment nigh." The commentators note the adjective *qarīb* — near, imminent — and read it as indicating that the punishment would not be delayed.

The nearness is significant given the sūrah's theme of delay. The commentators observe that elsewhere the punishment is postponed; here it is described as near, and read the difference as reflecting the severity of hamstringing a sign God had provided at request.

The commentators note that the Thamūd did hamstring her, as recorded at 26:155 and elsewhere, and that the punishment followed. The commentators read the verse as the warning whose fulfillment the narrative records.

**The Sign Requested and the Sign Given**

The commentators note an irony in the account that is worth setting out. The Thamūd requested a miracle to prove Ṣāliḥ's claim, and the camel was produced in response.

The request is recorded at 26:154 and in the parallel accounts. The commentators read the camel as precisely what was asked for — a sign sufficient to establish the prophetic claim.

The response to the sign was to hamstring it. The commentators read this as the sūrah's diagnosis of the Thamūd: the problem was not insufficient evidence but unwillingness to accept what the evidence required.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of sign-requests throughout. The commentators observe that in several narratives the demand for a sign is made and then rejected when granted, and read the pattern as indicating that the demand was never genuinely for evidence.

The designation of the camel as a trial at 54:27 is what the commentators draw out. The sign was not merely proof but test, and the Thamūd's treatment of it revealed what their request concealed.

**The Verse in the Ṣāliḥ Narrative**

Verse 64 sits within the Ṣāliḥ narrative that runs from v. 61 to v. 68. The commentators read the sequence as moving from call, to rejection, to the sign, to the hamstringing, to the destruction.

The verse's placement is significant. The commentators note that the sign comes after the call has been rejected, and read this as indicating that the camel was given in response to a demand rather than as an initial proof.

The command and its consequence are stated together. The commentators read the verse as supplying both the terms and the penalty, so that the subsequent hamstringing cannot be described as done in ignorance.

The connection to v. 65 is immediate, where the Thamūd hamstring her and Ṣāliḥ tells them to enjoy themselves in their homes for three days. The commentators read the two verses as warning and fulfillment in sequence.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns signs and their treatment. The verse is read as addressing those who ask for evidence and then refuse to be bound by what the evidence shows.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:65

> **But they hamstrung her. So he said, "Enjoy yourselves in your abode for three days—that is a promise that will not be belied."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"But They Hamstrung Her" — The Act That the Sūrah States in a Single Word**

The sūrah states the Thamūd's act against the she-camel in a single word: *fa-ʿaqarūhā* — "but they hamstrung her." The *ʿaqr* — the *hamstringing* — is the *word* that names the *specific act* of *cutting the camel's sinews* to *render her unable to stand or flee*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Thamūd's* *act* is *not a* *mistake*; it is *a deliberate act* in the *sense of being* *the act that requires the knowledge of the camel's anatomy*, in the *sense of being* *the act that requires the intention to disable*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *accidentally harming the camel*; they are *choosing to disable the sign*, and that the *choosing to disable* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *act* is *not a* *failure of care*; it is *a defiance of the command*, in the way described in *7:77* — the *way* in which the *Thamūd* are *described* as *insolently defying the command of their Lord*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *naming*, that the *Makkans* who *harm the sign* — the *Makkans* who *mock the Prophet and the believers* — are *in the same act*, in the *way* *described* throughout the *narrative middle*.

**"Enjoy Yourselves in Your Abode for Three Days" — The Word That the Sūrah Records**

Ṣāliḥ's answer to the hamstringing is the *word* that the sūrah records with particular solemnity: *qāla taʾattamū dārakum thalāthata ayyām* — "enjoy yourselves in your abode for three days." The *word* is *not a* *threat* in the *sense of being* *a demand for reparation*; it is *a statement of the term*, in the *sense of being* *the statement of the time that remains to the Thamūd before the command comes*, in the *sense of being* *the word that gives the Thamūd the knowledge of their remaining time*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Ṣāliḥ* is *not* *asking the Thamūd to repent*; he is *stating the term*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 3's statement of the appointed term*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *three days* are *not a* *gift of time*; they are *the form of the term that the command holds*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 8's statement of the muddat* — the *limited period of delay*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *word*, that the *Thamūd* are *not* *being given a chance to avoid the punishment by the three days*; they are *being given the knowledge of the punishment's arrival*, and that the *being given the knowledge* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *account* is *just*, because the *Thamūd* *know* the time, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part*.

**"That Is a Promise That Will Not Be Belied" — The Warrant That Closes the Word**

The *verse* *closes* with the *warrant*: *wa-huwa ʿaduwwun lā yukhaḍab* — "that is a promise that will not be belied." The *ʿadhw* — the *promise*, the *term* — is the *word* that names the *divine commitment* to the *time*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *three days* are *not a* *prediction*; they are *a promise* in the *sense of being* *the commitment that the Lord makes to the time*, in the *sense of being* *the term that the command holds and will execute*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *Ṣāliḥ* is *not* *stating a guess*; he is *stating the promise*, and that the *stating the promise* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's word* is *not a* *word of his own*; it is *the word of the promise*, in the way described in *verse 1's statement of the Book that is determined and expounded from the Presence of the Wise and Aware*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *coming of the command* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *promise* is *not belied*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 58's statement of the command that comes for the ʿĀd*, and that the *not belied* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *promise* *stated to the Thamūd*, and that the *stated to the Thamūd* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the same case*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:66

> **So when Our Command came, We saved Ṣāliḥ and those who believed with him, through a Mercy from Us, and from the disgrace of that day. Truly thy Lord, He is the Strong, the Mighty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Command Came" — the Same Formulation as v. 58**

The verse opens with the familiar formula: *fa-lammā jāʾa amrunā* — "so when Our Command came." The commentators explain that, as in v. 58, the Command here refers to God's Punishment.

Al-Qushayrī notes that the punishment is the one Ṣāliḥ promised would be visited upon the Thamūd at v. 65. The commentators read the verse as the fulfillment of that promise.

The recurrence of the formula is what the commentators emphasize. The same phrase marks the decisive moment in the ʿĀd narrative at v. 58 and here in the Ṣāliḥ narrative, and the commentators read the repetition as structuring the sūrah's accounts.

The particle *fa-* at the opening indicates sequence. The commentators read it as connecting the verse directly to what precedes — the three days' respite announced at v. 65.

The verse's compression is characteristic. The commentators note that the Thamūd's destruction is not narrated here; only the Command's arrival and the salvation are given.

**"We Saved Ṣāliḥ and Those Who Believed With Him"**

The verse states the salvation: *najjaynā Ṣāliḥan wa-lladhīna āmanū maʿahū* — "We saved Ṣāliḥ and those who believed with him." The commentators note the parallel with v. 58, where Hūd and the believers are saved in the same terms.

Al-Qushayrī observes that, as was the case with Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and his followers are said to have been saved through a Mercy from God. The commentators direct the reader to 7:75–76c for further detail regarding the followers.

The identification of the saved by belief rather than kinship is what the commentators emphasize. The commentators connect this to the sūrah's consistent treatment of family, at vv. 46 and 48.

The commentators note that the number of the believers is not given here, and direct the reader to the parallel accounts. The commentators read the verse as concerned with the fact of salvation rather than its scale.

The verb *najjā* is the same as at v. 58. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing the sūrah's standard vocabulary for divine deliverance.

**"Through a Mercy From Us" — and "From the Disgrace of That Day"**

The verse qualifies the salvation twice: *bi-raḥmatin minnā wa-min khizyi yawmiʾidhin* — "through a Mercy from Us, and from the disgrace of that day." The commentators note the double qualification.

The mercy is the means, as at v. 58. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing that salvation in the sūrah's narratives is consistently attributed to mercy rather than to merit.

The second qualification is distinctive to this verse. The commentators note that the ʿĀd at v. 58 were saved from a grave punishment, while the Thamūd's believers are saved from the disgrace of that day.

The word *khizy* denotes disgrace, abasement, being put to shame. The commentators read it as indicating that what the believers were spared was not merely pain but humiliation.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of the destroyed communities, several of whom are described as brought low. The commentators read the verse as indicating that the believers' deliverance included preservation of standing.

The phrase *yawmiʾidhin* — that day — refers to the day of the Thamūd's destruction. The commentators read it as the specific occasion rather than the Day of Resurrection, though the two are connected in the sūrah's argument.

**"Truly Thy Lord, He Is the Strong, the Mighty"**

The verse closes with a declaration: *inna rabbaka huwa l-qawiyyu l-ʿazīz* — "truly thy Lord, He is the Strong, the Mighty." The commentators note the two names and their pairing.

The name *qawiyy* denotes strength, and *ʿazīz* denotes might combined with invincibility. The commentators read the pairing as indicating power that cannot be opposed.

The address is to the Prophet in the singular — *rabbaka*, thy Lord. The commentators read the shift from the narrative to direct address as significant, and note that it occurs at several points in the sūrah's closing narratives.

The declaration functions as the ground of what precedes. The commentators read it as explaining how the salvation was accomplished: the one who saved is strong and mighty, and the destruction of the Thamūd did not require effort.

The commentators connect the two names to the sūrah's treatment of the destroyed communities. The commentators read the declaration as answering any suggestion that the communities were overcome by circumstance rather than by power.

**The Verse as the Ṣāliḥ Narrative's Resolution**

Verse 66 concludes the Ṣāliḥ narrative that begins at v. 61. The commentators note that its structure mirrors v. 58's conclusion of the ʿĀd narrative.

The mirroring is deliberate. The commentators observe that both verses follow the same pattern: the Command arrives, the messenger and believers are saved through mercy, and a declaration of divine power closes the account.

The variation is instructive. The commentators note that v. 58 speaks of salvation from a grave punishment while v. 66 speaks of salvation from the disgrace of that day, and read the difference as indicating that the two accounts emphasize different aspects of the deliverance.

The refrain at v. 68 — away with the Thamūd — follows shortly after, and the commentators read the sequence as salvation, destruction, imprecation.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the mercy attributed to the salvation. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who would count their standing as earned: the deliverance is mercy, and the declaration of strength removes any suggestion that it was owed.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:67

> **The Cry seized those who did wrong, and morning found them lying lifeless in their abode,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Cry Seized Those Who Did Wrong"**

The verse states the punishment in a single clause: *wa-akhadha lladhīna ẓalamū ṣ-ṣayḥa* — "and the Cry seized those who did wrong." The commentators explain that the punishment which befell the Thamūd is said to have been an earthquake, as recorded at 7:78, accompanied by a terrible, loud sound.

The commentators note that this is why the punishment is referred to here as the Cry. The commentators direct the reader to 7:77–78c for the fuller account.

The verb *akhadha* — seized, took — carries the weight here: on the commentators' reading the Cry was not merely heard but apprehended, and its effect was not sound but seizure.

The identification of those seized as *alladhīna ẓalamū* — those who did wrong — is significant. The commentators read the designation as indicating that the seizure was not indiscriminate but targeted at the wrongdoers, which is consistent with the salvation of Ṣāliḥ and the believers at v. 66.

The commentators note that the Qur'an describes the Thamūd's punishment variously — as the Cry here, as the earthquake at 7:78, and as the hamstringing's consequence elsewhere. The commentators read the accounts as describing a single event from different angles rather than as competing reports.

**"Morning Found Them Lying Lifeless in Their Abode"**

The verse continues: *fa-aṣbaḥū fī diyārihim jāthimīn* — "and morning found them lying lifeless in their abode." The commentators explain that the phrasing answers a question about the punishment's completeness.

What the commentators draw from *aṣbaḥū* — they became in the morning, morning found them — is that the destruction occurred within a single night, and that morning disclosed what had happened.

The word *jāthimīn* denotes lying prone, crouched, motionless. The commentators gloss it as describing bodies collapsed in place, and read it as indicating death without movement or struggle.

The phrase *fī diyārihim* — in their abode — is significant. The commentators read it as indicating that they were found where they lived, which is what makes the following clause possible.

The commentators connect this to the three days' respite announced at v. 65. The commentators read the sequence as warning, respite, and then the single night in which the respite ended.

The timing is worth noting. The commentators observe that the punishment came at night and was discovered at morning, and read the pattern as recurring in the sūrah's accounts of destruction.

**The Cry and the Earthquake — One Event, Two Descriptions**

The commentators address the relationship between the Cry described here and the earthquake recorded at 7:78, and their treatment is worth setting out.

The commentators do not treat the two as alternatives. They read the punishment as an earthquake accompanied by a terrible, loud sound, and understand the Cry as naming the auditory aspect of what 7:78 names as seismic.

This reading matters because it prevents the accounts from being set against each other. The commentators treat the Qur'an's varying descriptions as complementary rather than contradictory.

The commentators note that the same event is described elsewhere in terms of the hamstringing of the camel and its consequence. The commentators read all the descriptions as aspects of a single judgment.

It is worth recording that the verse itself names only the Cry. The earthquake comes from the parallel account, and the commentators' synthesis is their reading rather than this verse's statement.

**The Speed of the Punishment**

The verse's compression is itself significant. After the extended narrative of Ṣāliḥ's call, the Thamūd's refusal, the sign, and the hamstringing, the destruction is given in a single verse.

The commentators read the compression as reflecting the punishment's suddenness. The three days announced at v. 65 pass, and then the Cry seizes them; there is no narrated process.

The nearness of the punishment was announced at v. 64, where Ṣāliḥ warned that they would be seized by a punishment nigh. The commentators read the verse as the fulfillment of that specific warning.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's broader treatment of delay. Elsewhere the punishment is postponed; here it is described as near and arrives accordingly. The commentators read the difference as reflecting the severity of hamstringing a sign God had given at request.

The verse's reticence about the manner of destruction is notable. The commentators observe that the sūrah gives the fact and the timing but not the process, and read this as characteristic of its later narratives.

**The Verse as the Ṣāliḥ Narrative's Execution**

Verse 67 executes what the narrative has prepared. The commentators read its placement immediately after v. 66's statement of salvation as deliberate: the saved are named first, and then the destroyed.

The sequence is consistent with the sūrah's other accounts. The commentators note that at v. 58 the salvation of Hūd precedes the description of the ʿĀd's curse at vv. 59–60, and read the pattern as fixed.

The verse's two clauses correspond to the two aspects of the punishment. The commentators read the Cry as the means and the morning scene as the result.

The connection to v. 68 is immediate, where the Thamūd are described as though they had never dwelt there and the refrain pronounces their removal. The commentators read the two verses as description and verdict.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the respite. The three days were given and used; the verse records what followed. The commentators read the account as addressing those who treat a delay as a reprieve.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:68

> **as though they had never dwelt there. Behold! Truly Thamūd disbelieved in their Lord. Behold! Away with Thamūd!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"As Though They Had Never Dwelt There"**

The verse opens with the trace left behind: *alā inna Thamūda kafarū rabbahum* is preceded by *ka-an lam yaghnaw fīhā* — "as though they had never dwelt there." The commentators direct the reader to 7:77–78c, which explains the phrasing.

The commentators note that the identical wording appears at vv. 94–95 in connection with the Midianites, and at 7:78 in connection with the Thamūd. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing a fixed formula for the aftermath of destruction.

The phrase's force lies in its negation of habitation. The commentators read it as indicating not merely that the people were gone but that their dwelling left no mark of having been occupied.

This is a stronger claim than abandonment. The commentators observe that an abandoned settlement retains evidence of its occupants; the verse describes the Thamūd's homes as though no such evidence remained.

The commentators connect this to the morning scene at v. 67, where they were found lying lifeless in their abode. The commentators read the two verses together: the bodies were in the homes, and yet the homes were as though never inhabited.

**"Behold! Truly Thamūd Disbelieved in Their Lord"**

The verse turns to the declaration: *alā inna Thamūda kafarū rabbahum* — "behold! Truly Thamūd disbelieved in their Lord." The commentators note the parallel with v. 60, where the identical formula is used of the ʿĀd.

The particle *alā* calls attention, and the commentators read it as marking what follows as a matter of record rather than an argument. The commentators note that the declaration comes after the narrative, not before it.

The construction *kafarū rabbahum* — disbelieved in their Lord — uses the accusative without a preposition. The commentators read it as carrying the sense of ingratitude as well as disbelief.

The naming of the Lord is significant. The commentators read it as indicating that the Thamūd's disbelief was not ignorance of a deity but rejection of the one who had provided for them and given them the sign they requested.

The parallel with v. 60 is what the commentators emphasize. The identical formula is what establishes, for the commentators, the sūrah's standard verdict on a destroyed community.

**"Away With Thamūd" — the Refrain's Second Occurrence**

The verse closes with the imprecation: *alā buʿdan li-Thamūd* — "behold! Away with Thamūd!" The commentators note that this is the refrain's second occurrence in the sūrah.

The commentators trace the refrain across the sūrah: first for the ʿĀd at v. 60, then here for the Thamūd, and finally for the Midianites at v. 95. The commentators read the three occurrences as structuring the narratives.

The word *buʿd* denotes distance. The commentators read the imprecation as pronouncing removal, and connect it to the gloss of curse at v. 60 as distance from God's Mercy.

The naming is worth noting. The commentators observe that the ʿĀd were named at v. 60 as the people of Hūd, while here the Thamūd are named without reference to Ṣāliḥ. The commentators have read the difference variously.

The parallel at v. 44, where the identical phrase is used of Noah's people, establishes the refrain as the sūrah's standard closing for a destroyed community. The commentators read its recurrence as making the pattern unmistakable.

**The Refrain's Function in the Sūrah's Argument**

The commentators note that the refrain does structural work beyond marking the end of a narrative. By its third occurrence the audience expects it, and the expectation is part of the sūrah's method.

The refrain converts each narrative into an instance of a rule. The commentators read the repetition as indicating that what happened to the ʿĀd and the Thamūd is not particular history but a pattern.

The pattern's application to the present is what the commentators draw out. The commentators observe that the sūrah's audience is being shown the refrain repeatedly so that its application to them is not in doubt.

The refrain's brevity is significant. The commentators note that it gives no argument and no qualification; it pronounces. The commentators read this as indicating that the case has been made by the narrative and needs no further support.

The connection to the sūrah's closing section is worth noting. The commentators observe that the refrain ceases after v. 95, and that the sūrah's remaining verses turn to instruction and reflection.

**The Verse as the Ṣāliḥ Narrative's Verdict**

Verse 68 concludes the Ṣāliḥ narrative that begins at v. 61. The commentators note that its structure mirrors v. 60's conclusion of the ʿĀd narrative.
