The mirroring is close: the aftermath described, the declaration of disbelief, and the imprecation. The commentators read the parallel as deliberate rather than formulaic.

The variation is instructive. The commentators note that v. 60 speaks of a curse in this world and on the Day of Resurrection, while v. 68 speaks of the homes as though never inhabited. The commentators read the difference as indicating different emphases.

The verse's three clauses move from evidence to verdict to pronouncement. The commentators read the sequence as the sūrah's standard method of closing a destroyed community's account.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the homes. The commentators read the phrase about never having dwelt there as addressing those who count their settlement as permanent. The Thamūd built in rock, and the verse records what remained.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:69

> **And indeed Our messengers came to Abraham with glad tidings. They said, "Peace." "Peace," he said, and he hastened to bring them a roasted calf.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Our Messengers Came to Abraham with Glad Tidings" — The Story That the Sūrah Turns To**

The sūrah now turns from the stories of the punished peoples to the story of Abraham, and it turns with a form that is different from the forms it has used: *wa-laqad jāʾat rusulunā Ibrāhīma bi-bushrā* — "and indeed our messengers came to Abraham with glad tidings." The *bushrā* — the *glad tidings* — is the *word* that names the *purpose* of the *coming*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *coming* is *not a* *coming of judgment*; it is *a coming of the bushrā*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the messengers who bring the good news*, in the *sense of being* *the act that the sūrah will state in verses 71–73 as the announcement of the birth of Isaac and Jacob*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *story of Abraham* is *not a story of the punished*; it is *the story of the friend of God*, in the way described in *43:67* — the *way* in which the *Qur'an* names *Abraham* as *the friend of God* (*Khalīlat Allāh*). The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *turn*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the one who is the father of the prophets*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *idols* are *not* *in the line of Abraham*, in the way described in *verse 26's statement of the call as the whole of the message*.

**"They Said, 'Peace'" — and the Reply: "Peace"**

The *sūrah* *states* the *greeting* of the *messengers* and the *reply* of *Abraham*: *qālū salām* — "they said, peace" — *qāla salām* — "'peace,' he said." The *salām* — the *peace* — is the *word* that the *angels* *use* for the *greeting*, and the *reply* is the *word* that *Abraham* *uses* for the *return of the greeting*, and the *two together* form the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *meeting* is *not a* *meeting of strangers*; it is *a meeting of peace* in the *sense of being* *the meeting that begins with the peace and returns the peace*, in the *sense of being* *the meeting that the angels open and Abraham receives*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Abraham* is *not* *afraid* of the *messengers*; he is *the one who returns the peace*, in the *way* *described* in *verse 70's statement of the fear that Abraham will feel when he sees that their hands do not reach the food*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *peace* is *not a* *formality*; it is *the form of the divine meeting*, in the way described in *verse 48's statement of the disembarkation with peace*.

**"And He Hastened to Bring Them a Roasted Calf" — The Hospitality That the Sūrah Records**

The *verse* *closes* with the *act* of the *hospitality*: *fa-saraʿa ilayhim bi-ʿišyān maḥmār* — "and he hastened to bring them a roasted calf." The *saraʿ* — the *hasting*, the *running* — is the *word* that names the *quality* of *Abraham's* *service*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *hospitality* is *not a* *duty performed slowly*; it is *a service performed with haste*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who does not delay the guest*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who runs to the guest*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Abraham* is *not* *a host in the formal sense*; he is *the host in the full sense*, in the way described in *51:26* — the *way* in which the *calf* is *described* as *a fattened calf* in the *other telling of the story*. The *ʿišyān maḥmār* — the *roasted calf* — is the *word* that names the *specific act of the hospitality*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *serving the guests with a general food*; he is *serving them with the calf that he has prepared*, in the *sense of being* *the host who prepares the specific dish for the specific guests*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *next verses* — the *fear* and the *glad tidings of the wife* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *hospitality* is *not the end of the story*; it is *the ground* on which the *next words* are *spoken*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:70

> **Then when he saw that their hands reached not toward it, he conceived a fear of them. They said, "Fear not. Verily we have been sent unto the people of Lot."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When He Saw That Their Hands Reached Not Toward It" — The Sign That the Sūrah Records**

The sūrah now records the *sign* that *Abraham* *perceives* in the *guests*: *fa-lammā raʾā yadi-him lā tablughū* — "then when he saw that their hands reached not toward it." The *sign* is the *refusal of the guests to eat*, and the *recording* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *a host who does not observe his guests*; he is *the host who sees the hands that do not reach the food*, in the *sense of being* *the one who notices the specific act that is not the act of the eater*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Abraham's* *perception* is *not a* *perception of the general*; it is *a perception of the specific*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the knowledge of the folded breast*. The *commentators* *note* that the *guests* *did not eat* because they were *angels in the form of men*, and that the *angels do not consume physical nourishment*, and that the *not consuming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *sign* is *not a* *sign of hostility*; it is *the sign of the nature of the guests*, in the way described in *19:17* — the *way* in which the *angel appears in the form of a man* in the *story of Mary.

**"He Conceived a Fear of Them" — and the Reason That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* records the *Abraham's* *response* to the *sign*: *fa-waʿā bi-him wa-yaʾasū* — "he conceived a fear of them." The *waʿā* — the *fear*, the *dread* — is the *word* that names the *Abraham's* *state* when he *sees the hands that do not eat*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *a host who is indifferent to the behavior of his guests*; he is *the host who fears when the guests do not eat*, in the *sense of being* *the one who reads the refusal of the food as a sign of ill intent*, in the *sense of being* *the one who knows the custom of the hospitality and the custom of the hostility*. The *commentators* *note* that the *refusal to eat food brought by a host* would have been *a sign of hostility* in the *Arabian custom*, and that the *Abraham's* *fear* is *the fear of the one who reads the custom correctly*, in the way described in *51:27–28*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Abraham* is *not* *a prophet who is above the human response*; he is *the prophet who has the human response and is corrected by the divine word*, in the way described in *verse 47's statement of the prophet's response to the correction*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the prophet who fears*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *constrained breast of verse 12* is *not unique*; it is *the breast of Abraham*, in the way described in *verse 36's statement of the counsel not to be distressed*.

**"Fear Not" — and the Disclosure: "We Have Been Sent unto the People of Lot"**

The *angels'* *response* to the *Abraham's fear* is the *word* that the sūrah records with particular weight: *qālū lā taqhar* — "they said, fear not." The *lā taqhar* — the *fear not* — is the *word* that *removes* the *Abraham's fear*, and the *removal* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *angels* are *not* *guests who do not address the host's state*; they are *the messengers who know the host's state and remove it*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the knowledge of what lies within breasts*. The *second* *half* of the *response* is the *disclosure*: *inna-nā ursilnā ilā qawmi Lūṭ* — "verily we have been sent unto the people of Lot." The *disclosure* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *purpose* of the *coming* is *not a* *purpose that concerns the Abraham*; it is *the purpose of the destruction of the people of Lot*, in the sense of being *the purpose that the messengers carry from God to the people of Lot*, in the sense of being *the purpose that the Abraham is not part of except in the way of the witness and the plea that verse 74 will state*. The sūrah is stating, in the disclosure, that the *Abraham* is *not* *the target of the coming*; he is *the witness to the coming*, and that the *being the witness* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* — the *laughter of the wife* and the *glad tidings of Isaac* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not left* in the *fear*; he is *given the bushrā* in the way described in *verse 69's statement of the glad tidings*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:71

> **And his wife was standing there and she laughed. Then We gave her glad tidings of Isaac, and after Isaac, of Jacob.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And His Wife Was Standing There" — The Presence That the Sūrah Places in the Scene**

The sūrah now places the wife in the scene with a single phrase: *wa-imrāʾatuhu qāʾimahun fa-ḍiʿikat* — "and his wife was standing there and she laughed." The *qāʾimah* — the *standing* — is the *word* that names the *wife's* *position* in the *scene*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wife* is *not* *outside* the *meeting*; she is *in it*, in the *sense of being* *the one who stands at the place of the meeting*, in the *sense of being* *the one who hears what is spoken and sees what is done*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Abraham's household* is *not* *divided* in the *scene*; the *wife* is *present* in the *meeting with the messengers*, and that the *being present* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *bushrā* of *verse 69* is *not a* *bushrā for the Abraham alone*; it is *the bushrā for the household*, in the way described in *verse 73's statement of the blessings upon the people of the house*.

**"She Laughed" — and the Two Readings That the Sūrah Carries**

The *second* *word* of the *clause* is the *laughter*: *fa-ḍiʿikat* — "she laughed." The *ḍiʿk* — the *laughter* — is the *word* that names the *wife's* *act*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wife* is *not* *silent* in the *scene*; she is *the one who laughs*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose body responds to the meeting before her words do*. The *commentators* *offer two readings* of the *laughter*: the *first* is that she *laughed with joy* over the *fact that the guests did not pose a threat to Abraham*, in the *sense of being* *the laughter of the relief when the fear of verse 70 is removed*; the *second* is that she *laughed* in *wonder* at the *news* that will follow, in the sense of being *the laughter of the astonishment at the bushrā*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording of both readings, that the *laughter* is *not a* *single* *act*; it is *the act that carries the relief and the wonder together*, in the way described in *Genesis 17:17 and 18:12–15*, where the *Bible* records the *Abraham's and Sarah's laughter* at the *announcement of the son in old age*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *wife's* *laughter* is *not a* *sign of disrespect*; it is *the sign of the human response to the divine bushrā*, in the way described in *verse 72's statement of the wife's astonishment*.

**"Then We Gave Her Glad Tidings of Isaac" — The Bushrā, Stated for the Wife**

The *second* *clause* of the *verse* states the *bushrā* that the *sūrah* *had announced* in *verse 69*: *fa-waḥaynā lahā Ishaqā* — "then we gave her glad tidings of Isaac." The *bushrā* is *stated* for the *wife* in the *form of the divine act* — *waḥaynā* — "we gave," in the *sense of being* *the act that God performs upon the wife*, in the *sense of being* *the act that the angels announce but God gives*. The sūrah is stating, in the form, that the *bushrā* is *not a* *message that the angels own*; it is *the bushrā that God gives through the angels*, in the way described in *verse 2's statement of the warner on God's behalf*. The *name* *Ishaq* — *Isaac* — is the *name* of the *son* that the *bushrā* *announces*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *son* is *not a* *son in the abstract*; he is *the named son*, in the sense of being *the one whose name the sūrah will carry through the story of the binding in 37:102*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *name of the son of Abraham*, and that the *hearing the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *line* is *in the line of Abraham*, in the way described in *verse 69's statement of the father of the prophets*.

**"And After Isaac, of Jacob" — The Extension of the Bushrā Through the Grandson**

The *verse* *closes* with the *extension* of the *bushrā*: *wa-min warāʾ Ishaqa Yaʿqūb* — "and after Isaac, of Jacob." The *extension* to the *grandson* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *bushrā* is *not a* *bushrā of a single birth*; it is *the bushrā of a line*, in the *sense of being* *the announcement of the son and the grandson together*, in the *sense of being* *the announcement of the family that will carry the prophetic line*. The sūrah is stating, in the extension, that the *wife* is *not* *being given a child*; she is *being given a line*, in the way described in *verse 48's statement of the communities that arise from those with Noah*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *Makkans* are *in the line of Jacob*, and that the *being in the line* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *bushrā* is *not a* *past event*; it is *the event that the Makkans are standing in*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:72

> **She said, "Oh, woe unto me! Shall I bear a child when I am an old woman, and this husband of mine is an old man? That would surely be an astounding thing."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Oh, Woe Unto Me!" — Sarah's Exclamation**

The verse records the response of Abraham's wife: *qālat yā waylatā* — "she said, 'Oh, woe unto me!'" The commentators identify her as Sarah, and note that the exclamation is an idiom of astonishment rather than a literal invocation of woe.

Al-Jawzī and al-Rāzī record the identification. The commentators note that the Qur'an does not name her here, and that the identification comes from the exegetical tradition and the biblical parallels.

The exclamation's form is worth noting. The commentators read *yā waylatā* as a conventional expression of amazement, and observe that its use here indicates the extremity of the news rather than distress.

The verse's placement follows the announcement of the glad tidings at v. 71, and the commentators read the exclamation as the immediate human response to a promise that exceeds expectation.

The commentators note the parallel accounts at 51:27–28 and 15:53, and direct the reader also to 37:112. The commentators read the recurrence as indicating that the astonishment is a fixed element of the account.

**"Shall I Bear a Child When I Am an Old Woman?"**

The verse gives the ground of the astonishment: *a-alidu wa-anā ʿajūz* — "shall I bear a child when I am an old woman?" The commentators supply the ages recorded in the tradition.

Al-Jawzī records that Abraham was said to be 120 years old at this time and Sarah 99. The commentators direct the reader also to 51:29 for the parallel statement.

The commentators note that the ages are traditional rather than Qur'anic. The verse itself says only that she is an old woman and her husband an old man; the figures come from the exegetical tradition.

The question's form is rhetorical. The commentators read it as expressing not doubt about God's power but astonishment at the promise's content. The commentators connect this to the biblical parallels at Genesis 17:17 and 18:12–15.

The phrase *wa-hādhā baʿlī shaykhā* — "and this husband of mine is an old man" — completes the ground. The commentators read the dual statement as indicating that both natural conditions were against the promise.

The commentators note that Sarah's astonishment is on account of their old age, and connect it to the parallel statements at 15:54 and 51:29.

**"That Would Surely Be an Astounding Thing"**

The verse closes with *inna hādhā la-shayʾun ʿajīb* — "that would surely be an astounding thing." The commentators note the word *ʿajīb*, which denotes what provokes wonder.

The emphatic construction *inna ... la-* matters here: the astonishment, on this reading, is stated with conviction rather than tentatively.

The commentators note that the verse does not record any doubt about God's ability. The commentators read Sarah's statement as an assessment of the natural order rather than a challenge to the promise.

This distinction matters for how the verse is read. The commentators observe that the angels' response, which follows at v. 73, addresses the ground of the astonishment rather than rebuking it, and read this as indicating that the astonishment was not culpable.

The commentators connect the exchange to God's reply to Zachariah's prayer for a child at 19:9, and note the parallels at 15:55 and 51:30. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing a pattern: a promise that exceeds natural expectation, an astonished response, and a divine answer.

**The Laughter at v. 71 — Two Readings**

The commentators address Sarah's laughter at v. 71 and preserve two readings of its cause.

On one reading, al-Rāzī records that she laughed because of her joy over the fact that the guests did not pose a threat to Abraham. The commentators read this as relief following the fear described earlier in the narrative.

On the alternate reading, al-Jawzī and al-Rāzī record that her laughter denotes her happiness over the news of the impending destruction of the people of Lot. The commentators read this as a response to the announcement rather than to the guests' conduct.

The commentators preserve both without preferring either. The commentators note the biblical parallels at Genesis 17:17 and 18:12–15, where the laughter is associated with the promise of a child.

It is worth recording that the verse does not state the cause. The commentators' two readings reflect a genuine ambiguity, and the tradition has not resolved it.

**The Verse in the Abraham Narrative**

Verse 72 sits within the Abraham narrative that runs from v. 69 to v. 76. The commentators read the sequence as moving from the guests' arrival, to Abraham's fear, to the glad tidings, to Sarah's astonishment, to the angels' answer, and to Abraham's pleading.

The verse's function is to record the human response to a divine promise. The commentators read it as significant that the promise is met with astonishment rather than immediate acceptance.

The commentators note that the astonishment is what makes the angels' answer necessary. The commentators read the exchange as establishing that the promise is not subject to natural limitation.

The connection to v. 73 is immediate, where the angels ask whether she wonders at God's command. The commentators read the two verses as question and answer in sequence.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns promises that exceed expectation. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who receive a promise and find it incredible, and note that the astonishment is answered rather than rebuked.

The commentators also note the promise's content: a son, Isaac, and a grandson, Isaac's son Jacob. The commentators connect this to the parallels at 51:28 and 15:53, and read the double promise as extending the glad tidings beyond the immediate birth.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:73

> **They said, "Do you marvel at the Command of God? The Mercy of God and His Blessings be upon you, O People of the House! Truly He is Praised, Glorious."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Do You Marvel at the Command of God?" — The Answer That the Sūrah Records**

The angels' answer to the wife's astonishment is the question that is the verse's central word: *a-ʿajibtum min amri-llāh* — "do you marvel at the command of God?" The *question* is *not a* *question that seeks an answer*; it is *the form of the correction* in the *sense of being* *the question that states the error of the astonishment*, in the *sense of being* *the question that places the marvel in the wrong place*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *wife's* *marvel* is *not a* *marvel at the impossibility*; it is *a marvel at the command*, in the *sense of being* *the marvel that measures the command by the present state rather than by the power of the One who commands*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *angels* are *not* *correcting the wife by a proof*; they are *correcting her by a question*, and that the *correcting by a question* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *correction* is *not a* *lesson*; it is *a redirection of the marvel*, in the sense of being *the turning of the marvel from the command to the Commander*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans'* *marvel* at the *Qur'an* — the *marvel* that *states* the *gap* between the *revelation* and the *prophet's* *condition* — is *the same marvel*, and that the *same marvel* is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *next verse* of the *sūrah's argument* — the *command not to marvel* — is *the answer to the Makkans' marvel*, in the way described in *19:9* — the *way* in which God answers *Zachariah's* *marvel* at the *sign of the son*: "That is easy for Me."

**"The Mercy of God and His Blessings Be upon You" — The Blessing That Replaces the Marvel**

The *second* *clause* of the *answer* is the *blessing*: *raḥmatu-llāhi wa-barakātuhu ʿalaykum* — "the mercy of God and his blessings be upon you." The *barakah* — the *blessing* — and the *raḥmah* — the *mercy* — are the *two words* that the *angels* *give* in *place of the marvel*, and the *giving* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *marvel* is *not the end of the encounter*; it is *the state that the blessing replaces*, in the *sense of being* *the state that is covered by the mercy and the blessing*, in the *sense of being* *the state that the angels do not leave in the wife*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *angels* are *not* *leaving the wife in her astonishment*; they are *covering her with the blessing*, in the way described in *verse 41's statement of the forgiving and the merciful*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *blessing* *given to the people of the house of Abraham*, and that the *hearing the blessing* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the line of the house*, in the way described in *verse 71's statement of the line of Isaac and Jacob*.

**"O People of the House!" — The Address That the Sūrah Will Hear Again**

The *address* — *yā ahl al-bayt* — "O people of the house!" — is the *word* that the *angels* *use* for the *Abraham and his wife*, and the *use* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *blessing* is *not a* *blessing for the wife alone*; it is *the blessing for the house*, in the *sense of being* *the blessing that covers the Abraham and the wife together*, in the *sense of being* *the blessing that the sūrah will give in the form of the command to establish the prayer in 33:33*. The sūrah is stating, in the address, that the *house of Abraham* is *not a* *house of two persons*; it is *a house of a line*, in the way described in *verse 48's statement of the communities that arise from those with Noah*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *in the line of the house of Abraham*, and that the *being in the line* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *blessing* is *not a* *past blessing*; it is *the blessing that the Makkans are standing in*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*.

**"Truly He Is Praised, Glorious" — The Closing That Names the Two Attributes**

The *verse* *closes* with the *naming* of the *two* *attributes* that *govern* the *bushrā*: *inna-hu ḥamīdun ʿazīz* — "truly He is Praised, Glorious." The *ḥamīd* — the *Praised* — and the *ʿazīz* — the *Glorious*, the *Mighty* — are the *two attributes* that the *angels* *name* for the *Lord* in this *closing*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *bushrā* is *not a* *bushrā that the angels give by their own power*; it is *the bushrā of the Praised and the Glorious*, in the *sense of being* *the bushrā that the Praise governs and the Glory supports*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *naming of the attributes* *applied to their Lord*, and that the *applied to their Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *refusal* is *not a* *refusal that can withstand the Praised and the Glorious*, in the way described in *verse 66's statement of the Strong and the Mighty*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *Abraham's* *plea for the people of Lot* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *bushrā* is *not the end of the story*; it is *the ground* on which the *next words* are *spoken*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:74

> **So when the awe had left Abraham and glad tidings had come unto him, he pleaded with Us concerning the people of Lot.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When the Awe Had Left Abraham"**

The verse opens with a temporal clause: *fa-lammā dhahaba ʿan Ibrāhīma r-rawʿ* — "so when the awe had left Abraham." The commentators read the clause as marking the passage from fear to composure.

The word *rawʿ* denotes fright or awe. The commentators connect it to the fear described earlier in the narrative, when Abraham did not recognize his guests and was uneasy at their refusal of food.

The verb *dhahaba* — went away, departed — describes a state that passed rather than a permanent condition.

The commentators note that the verse's structure makes the departure of awe the precondition for what follows. The commentators read this as indicating that Abraham's pleading was undertaken in composure rather than in fear.

The particle *lammā* — when — indicates that the departure did in fact occur and was followed by the consequence. The commentators read the construction as establishing sequence.

**"And Glad Tidings Had Come Unto Him"**

The verse adds a second condition: *wa-jāʾathu l-bushrā* — "and glad tidings had come unto him." The commentators read the two conditions together as the circumstances of the pleading.

The glad tidings are the promise of a son and a grandson, announced at v. 71 and the subject of Sarah's astonishment at v. 72. The commentators read the verse as referring back to that promise.

The commentators note that both conditions are stated before the pleading. The commentators read this as significant: Abraham pleads not from fear but from a position of received blessing.

The connection between the glad tidings and the pleading is what the commentators draw out. The commentators read Abraham's receipt of the promise as informing his intercession for others.

The commentators note that the verse does not state a causal link between the two conditions and the pleading. The commentators read the sequence as temporal rather than strictly causal, though the connection is implied.

**"He Pleaded With Us Concerning the People of Lot"**

The verse states the action: *yujādilunā fī qawmi Lūṭ* — "he pleaded with Us concerning the people of Lot." Al-Kisāʾī and al-Ṭabarī explain that this is understood to mean that Abraham spoke to the angels and tried to dissuade them from bringing destruction down upon the people of Lot.

The commentators note that the pleading is described as being with Us, though its immediate addressees were the angels. The commentators read the formulation as indicating that the angels acted on God's command, so that pleading with them was pleading with God.

The verb *jādala* denotes arguing, disputing, contending in argument. The commentators read it as indicating sustained argument rather than a single request.

The commentators supply the parallel at 29:32, where Abraham protests, *Verily, Lot is in it*. The commentators note the biblical parallel at Genesis 18:23–33, where the pleading is extended across a series of exchanges.

The object of the pleading is *qawmi Lūṭ* — the people of Lot. The commentators note that the concern is for the community rather than for Lot himself, though Lot's presence in it is what 29:32 records as Abraham's ground.

**The Ground of the Pleading**

The commentators note that the following verse supplies the reason for Abraham's pleading, and that the reason is worth examining.

Verse 75 records that Abraham was forbearing, tender-hearted, and penitent. The commentators read these qualities as the ground of the intercession, and observe that the verse supplies a character explanation rather than a theological argument.

The commentators connect this to the parallel at 29:32, where Abraham's ground is that Lot is in the city. The commentators read the two accounts as complementary: the concern is for the community, and the immediate occasion is Lot's presence.

The commentators note that the pleading does not succeed. The commentators read this as significant: the intercession of a prophet, grounded in his character, did not avert the destruction.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of intercession elsewhere, and note that the destruction proceeds despite the pleading. The commentators read the account as establishing the limits of intercession.

**The Verse in the Abraham Narrative**

Verse 74 marks a turning point in the narrative. The commentators read the departure of awe and the arrival of glad tidings as establishing a new relation between Abraham and the angels.

The commentators note that up to this verse Abraham has been receiving; from here he is interceding. The commentators read the shift as significant for the narrative's structure.

The verse's two conditions are what make the shift intelligible. The commentators read the composure and the blessing as the circumstances in which intercession becomes possible.

The connection to vv. 75–76 is immediate. The commentators read the sequence as pleading, character explanation, and divine answer. The commentators note that the answer at v. 76 declines the intercession.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns intercession and its limits. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who would plead for a community under judgment, and note that the pleading is recorded with honor even though it does not succeed.

The commentators also note the contrast with the sūrah's other narratives. The commentators observe that elsewhere the destruction is announced and executed without recorded intercession, and read Abraham's pleading as distinctive.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:75

> **Truly Abraham was clement, tenderhearted, penitent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Truly Abraham Was" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Character**

The sūrah now steps out of the narrative to state, in its own voice, the *character* of the *Abraham*: *inna Ibrāhīma la-ḥalīman awwā dhāʾirā* — "truly Abraham was clement, tenderhearted, penitent." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *plea of verse 74* is *not an accident of the story*; it is *the act of a character*, in the *sense of being* *the act that the character produces*, in the *sense of being* *the act that the sūrah will now name*. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Abraham* is *not* *a prophet who pleads by chance*; he is *the prophet whose character is the plea*, in the way described in *verse 47's statement of the prophet who seeks refuge*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *character of the father of the prophets* *named by the sūrah itself*, and that the *hearing the character named* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *character* is *not a* *trait that the Makkans can claim without the acts*, in the way described in *verse 23's statement of the three marks of the inhabitants of the Garden*.

**"Clement" — The First Quality, and the Word That Names the Forbearance**

The *first* *quality* is the *ḥilm* — the *clemency*, the *forbearance* — from the root *ḥ-l-m*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *a prophet who is swift to anger*; he is *the one who is slow to anger* in the *sense of being* *the one who holds the tongue and the hand in the moment of provocation*, in the *sense of being* *the one who does not let the provocation set his actions*. The *commentators* *note* that the *ḥilm* is *the quality that the Qur'an names for the prophet in 3:159* — the *way* in which the *Qur'an* states: "And be gentle with them," in the *command to the Prophet ﷺ* — and that the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *clemency* is *not a* *private virtue*; it is *the quality of the prophetic office itself*, in the way described in *verse 2's statement of the warner and the bringer of glad tidings*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Abraham's* *plea for the people of Lot* is *an act of the clemency*, in the sense of being *the act of the one who does not rejoice in the destruction of the people*, in the sense of being *the one who pleads even for the people who are not his own.

**"Tenderhearted" — The Second Quality, and the Word That Names the Turning**

The *second* *quality* is the *aww* — the *tenderhearted*, the *one who returns again and again to God in remembrance and weeping* — and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *a prophet whose devotion is a duty performed once*; he is *the one who returns* in the *sense of being* *the one whose heart turns to God again and again*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose remembrance is not a single act but a course*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *aww* is *the quality that produces the plea of verse 74*, in the sense of being *the quality of the one whose heart is turned toward God and therefore turned toward the people whom God holds in the account*, in the sense of being *the quality that makes the joy of the bushrā open onto the plea for the people of Lot*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *naming of the quality that the Prophetic character carries*, and that the *hearing the naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *tenderness for the Makkans* — the *tenderness* that *makes him grieve at their refusal* — is *the same quality*, in the way described in *verse 12's statement of the constrained breast*.

**"Penitent" — The Third Quality, and the Word That Names the Return**

The *third* *quality* is the *dhāʾir* — the *penitent*, the *one who turns* — and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Abraham* is *not* *a prophet who stands still in his devotion*; he is *the one who turns* in the *sense of being* *the one whose whole person is oriented toward the return to God*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose state is the turning*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *three qualities together* form the *complete character* of the *Abraham*: the *clemency* that *holds the tongue and the hand*, the *tenderness* that *returns to God in remembrance*, and the *penitence* that *turns the whole person toward the Lord*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *statement*, that the *character* is *not a* *list of virtues*; it is *the form of the prophet*, in the sense of being *the form that produces the acts described in the narrative middle*, in the sense of being *the form that the Makkans are being shown as the model of the Prophetic character*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *divine word to the Abraham* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *character* is *not the end of the story*; it is *the ground* on which the *next words* are *spoken*, in the way described in *verse 76*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:76

> **"O Abraham! Turn away from this. Truly the Command of thy Lord has come, and surely a punishment that cannot be repelled comes upon them."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Turn Away From This" — the Answer to the Pleading**

The verse records the angels' response to Abraham's intercession: *yā Ibrāhīmu aʿriḍ ʿan hādhā* — "O Abraham! Turn away from this." Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī record that the words are said to have been uttered by the angels.

The commentators note the parallel expression at 12:29, in the context of the story of Joseph, and read the phrase as a conventional way of closing a matter that cannot be pursued.

The imperative *aʿriḍ* — turn away, desist — directs Abraham to abandon the argument rather than to modify it.

The demonstrative *hādhā* — this — refers to the pleading itself. The commentators read the object as the intercession rather than the punishment, which is what makes the command a refusal of the argument rather than a description of the outcome.

The address by name is worth noting. The commentators observe that the angels address Abraham directly, and read this as indicating that the refusal is personal rather than general.

**"The Command of Thy Lord Has Come"**

The verse gives the ground of the refusal: *innahu qad jāʾa amru rabbik* — "truly the Command of thy Lord has come." Al-Zamakhsharī explains that this means God's Decree and Judgment have been passed concerning the people of Lot.

The commentators note the perfect tense. The commentators read *qad jāʾa* as indicating that the Command has already arrived, not that it is forthcoming. The commentators read this as the decisive point: the matter is settled before the pleading begins.

The word *amr* — command, decree, affair — is the same term used at vv. 58 and 66 for the arrival of the punishment. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing a fixed formula for the moment of execution.

The phrase *rabbik* — thy Lord — addresses Abraham directly. The commentators read the possessive as significant: the Command comes from the Lord whom Abraham serves, which is what makes the pleading impossible to sustain.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of divine decree throughout. The commentators read the verse as establishing that once the decree has issued, no further appeal is available.

**"A Punishment That Cannot Be Repelled"**

The verse states the consequence: *wa-innahum ātīhim ʿadhābun ghayru mardūd* — "and surely a punishment that cannot be repelled comes upon them." The commentators note the adjective *ghayru mardūd* — not repelled, not turned back.

Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss is the operative one: the decree cannot be stopped by supplication or any other means. The commentators read the phrase as excluding every avenue of prevention, not merely Abraham's.

The comprehensiveness is what the commentators emphasize. The commentators note that the verse does not say the punishment cannot be repelled by Abraham but that it cannot be repelled at all.

This connects to the limits of intercession that v. 74's account established. The commentators read the two verses together: the pleading was undertaken, and the answer declares it unavailable.

The commentators note that the Qur'an elsewhere speaks of intercession as available by God's leave. The commentators read this verse as describing a case in which the decree has already issued, and observe that the two statements operate in different registers.

**Why the Pleading Was Refused**

The commentators address a question the verse raises: Abraham's character is described at v. 75 as forbearing, tender-hearted, and penitent, and the pleading proceeds from those qualities. Why then is it refused?

The commentators read the answer as lying in the timing rather than in the quality of the pleading. The commentators note that the verse's ground is that the Command has come, which is a statement about the state of the decree rather than about Abraham.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of respite. The commentators observe that where respite remains, warning and repentance are possible; where the decree has issued, they are not.

The commentators also note that the Qur'an records the pleading with honor. The commentators read the account as commending the intercession even while recording its refusal, and observe that Abraham's character is stated immediately before the refusal rather than after it.

It is worth recording that the verse does not rebuke Abraham. The commentators read the command to turn away as a direction rather than a reproof, and note that no fault is attributed to him.

**The Verse in the Lot Narrative**

Verse 76 closes the exchange between Abraham and the angels that begins at v. 74. The commentators read the sequence as pleading, character explanation, and refusal.

The commentators note that the narrative then turns immediately to the destruction at vv. 82–83, with no further exchange recorded. The commentators read the abruptness as reflecting the finality of the decree.

The verse's function is to establish the limit of prophetic intercession. The commentators read it as significant that the greatest of the sūrah's intercessors is refused, and that the ground is the decree's arrival rather than any deficiency in the plea.

The connection to the parallel at 29:32 is instructive. The commentators note that there Abraham protests that Lot is in the city, and that the answer is comparable. The commentators read the two accounts as making the same point.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns intercession and its conditions. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who plead for communities under judgment, and note that the pleading is honored but the decree is not reversed.

The commentators also note the contrast with the sūrah's other narratives. The commentators observe that in no other account is an intercession recorded at such length, and read the distinctiveness as indicating that the account is given precisely to establish its limit.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:77

> **When Our messengers came to Lot, he was distressed on their account, and felt himself powerless concerning them. And he said, "This is a terrible day!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Messengers Came to Lot" — The Scene That the Sūrah Moves To**

The sūrah now moves from the scene of Abraham to the scene of Lot: *fa-lammā jāʾat rusulunā Lūṭan* — "when our messengers came to Lot." The *commentators* *note* that the *angels* *went from Abraham's village to Lot's village*, which was *nearby*, and that the *movement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *two scenes* are *not separate stories*; they are *one scene* in the *sense of being* *the same messengers carrying the same command from one house to the next*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *bushrā* of *verse 69* and the *command of verse 76* are *the same journey*, in the sense of being *the journey that begins with the glad tidings for Abraham and ends with the destruction of the people of Lot*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *movement*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the messengers who move between the houses*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *message* moves *between the houses of the Makkans* in the *same way*, in the way described in *verse 2's statement of the warner on God's behalf.

**"He Was Distressed on Their Account" — and the Reason That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* records the *Lot's* *state* when he *sees the messengers*: *wa-ḥazina ʿalayhim wa-ʿajiza bi-him* — "he was distressed on their account, and felt himself powerless concerning them." The *ḥazn* — the *distress*, the *sorrow* — is the *word* that names the *Lot's* *state*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *joyful* at the *coming of the messengers*; he is *distressed*, in the *sense of being* *the one who fears for the guests*, in the *sense of being* *the one who knows his people and what they will do to the guests*. The *commentators* *note* that the *messengers arrived in the form of handsome male youths*, and that the *Lot did not know that they were angels*, and that the *distress* is *the fear of the one who knows that his people will attempt to have relations with the guests*, in the way described in *7:80–81* and *Genesis 19*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot* is *not* *a prophet who is above the fear*; he is *the prophet who has the fear and is met by the divine word in it*, in the way described in *verse 70's statement of the Abraham's fear.

**"And Felt Himself Powerless Concerning Them" — The Word That Names the Impotence**

The *third* *clause* records the *Lot's* *feeling of powerlessness*: *wa-ʿajiza bi-him* — "and felt himself powerless concerning them." The *ʿajz* — the *powerlessness* — is the *word* that names the *Lot's* *state* toward his *people*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *a prophet who can defend his guests by his own power*; he is *the one who feels the impotence of the single prophet against the people*, in the sense of being *the one who fears that he will be unable to defend the guests*, in the sense of being *the one who knows that his power is not the power that the people require. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Lot's* *impotence* is *not a* *failure of the prophetic office*; it is *the condition of the prophet in the midst of the people*, in the way described in *verse 12's statement of the prophet as only a warner*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the prophet who feels the impotence*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *feeling of the impotence in Makkah* is *the same feeling*, in the way described in *verse 36's statement of the counsel not to be distressed.

**"This Is a Terrible Day!" — The Word That the Sūrah Records**

The *verse* *closes* with the *Lot's* *word*: *qāla hādhā yawmun ʿaṣīb* — "he said, this is a terrible day!" The *ʿaīb* — the *terrible*, the *hard*, the *burdensome* — is the *word* that names the *day* in the *Lot's* *experience*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *naming the day for the people*; he is *naming the day for himself*, in the *sense of being* *the one who experiences the day as the terrible day*, in the *sense of being* *the one who knows that the day will bring the worst that his people can do. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot's* *word* is *not a* *prophet's word of command*; it is *the prophet's word of the moment*, in the sense of being *the word that the prophet speaks in the state of the distress and the impotence*, in the sense of being *the word that the sūrah will answer in the next verse with the word of the protection. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *coming of the people* and the *Lot's* *defense of the guests* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *terrible day* is *not over* by the *word*; it is *in the coming of the people*, in the way described in *verse 78.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:78

> **And his people came hurrying toward him, while earlier they had been committing evil deeds. He said, "O my people! These are my daughters; they are purer for you. So reverence God, and disgrace me not with regard to my guests. Is there not among you a man of sound judgment?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"His People Came Hurrying Toward Him" — and the Record of the Earlier Evil**

The sūrah now records the *coming of the people of Lot*: *wa-jāʾa qawmuhu ilayhi hasirīn* — "and his people came hurrying toward him." The *ḥasr* — the *hurrying*, the *rushing* — is the *word* that names the *manner* of the *coming*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people* are *not* *coming in the form of a visit*; they are *rushing* in the *sense of being* *the ones who come with the intent that the running states*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who seek the guests for the purpose that their history states*. The *sūrah* *adds* the *record* of the *earlier* *evil*: *wa-kānū min qablu yaʿmalūn al-sayyiʿāt* — "while earlier they had been committing evil deeds." The *record* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *coming* is *not a* *new* *act*; it is *the act of the people whose earlier deeds are the evil deeds*, in the *sense of being* *the people whose character is the commission of the evil*, in the *sense of being* *the people that the sūrah will characterize in 7:80–81 as the ones who come to their males and leave what God created for them*. The sūrah is stating, in the record, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *characterization of the people of Lot*, and that the *hearing the characterization* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *not being compared to the people of Lot in their specific evil*; they are *being compared to them in the pattern of the refusal of the prophet and the harm to the guests of the message*, in the way described throughout the *narrative middle.

**"These Are My Daughters; They Are Purer for You" — The Defense That the Sūrah Records**

Lot's *answer* to the *coming of his people* is the *defense* that the sūrah records with particular care: *qāla hādhihā banātī in kunnata faʿilīn* — "these are my daughters; they are purer for you." The *commentators* *note* that the *plain sense* of the *banāt* — the *daughters* — is *Lot's own daughters*, and that *others* *understand* the *daughters* as *the women among the people of Lot*, and that the *two readings* together form the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *defense* is *not a* *defense of the guests by force*; it is *the defense of the guests by the redirection*, in the *sense of being* *the act of the one who offers the women of the community as the pure alternative to the impure act that the people seek*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot* is *not* *surrendering his guests*; he is *turning his people from the guests to the women*, in the way described in *27:56* — the *way* in which the *people of Lot deride the morality of Lot and his followers by calling them those who keep themselves pure*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *defense* is *not a* *compromise of the guests*; it is *the act of the prophet who cannot defend by force and defends by the word*, in the way described in *verse 77's statement of the impotence.

**"So Reverence God, and Disgrace Me Not with Regard to My Guests" — The Command Within the Defense**

The *second* *part* of the *defense* is the *command* within the *defense*: *fattaqū-llāh wa-lā takhīyūnī min khayri dāri* — "so reverence God, and disgrace me not with regard to my guests." The *taqwā* — the *reverence* — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *has used* throughout for the *quality of the one who holds the awareness of God in the act*, and the *use* here is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *commanding his people by a general exhortation*; he is *commanding them in the specific moment of the specific act*, in the *sense of being* *the one who says to them: hold the awareness of God in this act, in this moment, before you do what you are about to do*. The *second* *command* — *the command not to disgrace him with regard to his guests* — is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *defending only the guests*; he is *defending his own standing as the host*, in the sense of being *the one who knows that the harm to the guests is the harm to the host*, in the sense of being *the one who invokes the custom of the hospitality that the people of the Arab world hold as the most sacred custom. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot* is *not* *a prophet who is separated from the customs of his people*; he is *the prophet who invokes the customs of his people in the defense of the guests of God*, in the way described in *verse 69's statement of the Abraham's haste to the roasted calf.

**"Is There Not Among You a Man of Sound Judgment?" — The Question That Closes the Defense**

The *verse* *closes* with the *question* that *addresses* the *people of Lot as a community*: *ā-lā takūnu rajulan ʿaqlah* — "is there not among you a man of sound judgment?" The *ʿaql* — the *sound judgment*, the *mind* — is the *word* that the *Qur'an* *uses* for the *capacity that the people have and do not exercise*, and the *use* here is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *despairing of the people's capacity*; he is *addressing the capacity as present*, in the way described in *verse 24's statement of the question that is the answer. The sūrah is stating, in the question, that the *people of Lot* are *not* *without the capacity for judgment*; they are *the ones who have the capacity and do not exercise it*, in the way described in *verse 20's statement of the spiritual deafness and blindness. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *people's answer* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *capacity is not exercised*, in the way described in *verse 79.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:79

> **They said, "Certainly you know that we have no right to your daughters, and surely you know that which we desire."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Certainly You Know That We Have No Right to Your Daughters" — The Rejection of the Redirection**

The people of Lot's answer to Lot's defense is the *rejection* of the *redirection*: *qālū la-ʿallaka ʿalamānnā ilā banātika min ḥājah* — "certainly you know that we have no right to your daughters." The *ḥājah* — the *need*, the *right*, the *claim* — is the *word* that names the *people's* *relation* to the *daughters*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people* are *not* *refusing the daughters out of a consideration of the daughters*; they are *refusing them out of the statement of their own need*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who state that they have no need for the daughters*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who place their own desire above the alternative that Lot offers*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *people* are *not* *engaging with the redirection*; they are *stating their own desire*, in the way described in *verse 78's statement of the defense by the redirection. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *people of Lot* are *not* *a people who can be turned by the word*; they are *the ones whose desire is the whole of their account*, in the way described in *verses 15–16's statement of the person who desires the world as the whole of the account.

**"And Surely You Know That Which We Desire" — The Statement That Closes the Answer**

The *second* *clause* is the *statement* that *closes* the *answer*: *wa-la-ʿalla mā nahnu bihi fāʿilūn* — "and surely you know that which we desire." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people* are *not* *ashamed of their desire*; they are *the ones who state it openly to the prophet*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who make the desire the object of the encounter*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who close the encounter with the statement of the desire*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *people of Lot* are *not* *a people who hide their evil*; they are *the ones who state it*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the concealment and the disclosure. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *statement of the desire* is *not a* *statement that the prophet can answer by a better argument*; it is *the statement that the sūrah will answer in the next verse with the word of the prophet's own impotence and the divine word of the protection*, in the way described in *verse 80. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *statement of the desire of the people of Lot*, and that the *hearing the statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *own desire* — the *desire* that *makes them harm the guests of the message* — is *the same desire*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen*.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:80

> **He said, "Would that I had the strength [to resist] you, or could seek refuge in some mighty support!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Would That I Had the Strength" — The Word That the Sūrah Records**

The sūrah now records Lot's *response* to the *people's* *statement of the desire*: *qāla law anna lī bikum quwwahaw aw ʿayadun ʿazīz* — "he said, would that I had the strength [to resist] you, or could seek refuge in some mighty support!" The *lāw annā* — the *would that* — is the *form* of the *wish* that the *sūrah* *uses* for the *prophet's* *statement of the impotence*, and the *form* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *cursing his people*; he is *wishing for the strength that he does not have*, in the *sense of being* *the one who states the absence of the strength in the form of the wish for it*, in the *sense of being* *the one who names the gap between the act he must do and the power he holds. The *commentators* *note* that the *quwwah* — the *strength* — is *an army*, and that the *Lot* is *wishing for the army that he does not have*, in the way described in *verse 77's statement of the impotence. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot* is *not* *a prophet who is content with the impotence*; he is *the one who wishes for the strength*, and that the *wishing for the strength* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *prophet's* *feeling of the impotence* is *not a* *failure of the faith*; it is *the feeling of the one who knows the gap and does not close it by the faith but by the wish*, in the way described in *verse 77's statement of the distress and the impotence.

**"Or Could Seek Refuge in Some Mighty Support" — The Second Half of the Wish**

The *second* *half* of the *wish* is the *refuge*: *aw ʿayadun ʿazīz* — "or could seek refuge in some mighty support." The *ʿayad* — the *support*, the *stronghold* — is the *word* that the *commentators* *understand* as *family or friends*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *wishing only for the army*; he is *wishing for the support of the family and the friends that he does not have*, in the *sense of being* *the one who is alone against the people*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose family is not the support that the act requires. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Lot* is *not* *a prophet who is surrounded by the support of his people*; he is *the one who is alone*, in the way described in *verse 40's statement of the few who believed. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *wish of the prophet who is alone*, and that the *hearing the wish* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet* in *Makkah* is *the one who is alone against the people* in the *same way*, in the way described in *verse 36's statement of the counsel not to be distressed. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *divine word of the protection* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *wish is not answered by the strength that the Lot wished for*; it is *answered by the protection that the Lot did not wish for*, in the way described in *verse 81.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:81

> **They said, "O Lot! We are the envoys of thy Lord. They shall not reach thee. So set out with thy family during the night, and let none of you turn around, save thy wife; surely that which befalls them shall befall her. Indeed, the morning shall be their tryst. Is not the morning nigh?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Are the Envoys of Thy Lord" — The Disclosure That the Sūrah Records**

The sūrah now records the *divine word* to the *Lot*, conveyed by the *messengers*: *qālū innā rusul rabbika lā yavlughūn* — "they said, we are the envoys of thy lord. they shall not reach thee." The *disclosure* of the *messengers' identity* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot's* *fear of verse 77* and the *impotence of verse 80* are *not* *left* in the *uncertainty*; they are *met by the disclosure*, in the *sense of being* *the state that the divine word addresses and removes*, in the *sense of being* *the state that the messengers' identity resolves. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Lot* is *not* *left* in the *fear of the guests*; he is *told* that the *guests are the envoys of his Lord*, in the way described in *verse 70's statement of the Abraham's fear met by the fear not. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the prophet to whom the envoys disclose their identity*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet's* *message* is *not a* *message of the human envoys*; it is *the message of the envoys of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 2's statement of the warner on God's behalf.

**"They Shall Not Reach Thee" — The Word of the Protection**

The *second* *clause* is the *word of the protection*: *lā yavlughūn* — "they shall not reach thee." The *protection* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people of Lot* are *not* *in a position to harm the Lot or his guests*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who are held by the divine word from the reaching*, in the *sense of being* *the ones whose power is not the power that the act requires. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *Lot's* *wish of verse 80* — the *wish for the strength and the support* — is *not* *answered by the strength that he wished for*; it is *answered by the protection that he did not wish for*, in the way described in *verse 41's statement of the name that moves the ark. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *protection* is *not a* *protection that the Lot can see*; it is *the protection that the divine word holds over him*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the knowledge of what lies within breasts.

**"So Set Out with Thy Family during the Night" — The Command That the Sūrah Gives**

The *third* *clause* is the *command*: *fa-amsir bi-ahlikhi ʿaṭāʾa-l-layl* — "so set out with thy family during the night." The *command* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lot* is *not* *left* in the *house of his people*; he is *commanded to leave*, in the *sense of being* *the one who is to depart with his family in the night*, in the *sense of being* *the one whose family is the few of verse 40 in the form of the household. The sūrah is stating, in the command, that the *salvation of the Lot* is *not a* *salvation that holds him in the house*; it is *the salvation that carries him out of the house in the night*, in the way described in *verse 44's statement of the disembarkation. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *command* is *not a* *command for the Lot alone*; it is *the command for the family*, in the way described in *verse 40's statement of the family in the ark.

**"Let None of You Turn Around, Save Thy Wife" — The Exception, Named**

The *fourth* *clause* states the *condition* and the *exception*: *wa-lā yualaykun minkum aḥadun muntaqil illā imrāʾataka* — "and let none of you turn around, save thy wife." The *exception of the wife* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *family* is *not a* *family of the same faith*, in the *sense of being* *the family that includes the one who is not of the faith*, in the *sense of being* *the family in which the wife is the one who lags behind*. The *commentators* *note* that the *wife* is *believed to have outwardly followed the religion of Lot but to have been secretly a disbeliever*, and that the *secret disbelief* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *salvation* is *not by the outward*; it is *by the inward*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the folded breast. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *exception*, that the *wife* is *not* *saved* by the *command to the family*; she is *held out of the salvation by her own state*, in the way described in *verse 40's statement of the exception in the family of Noah.

**"The Morning Shall Be Their Tryst. Is Not the Morning Nigh?" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *statement of the tryst*: *innahum mawʿiduhum ghadāʾun a-falā ghadāʾun qarīb* — "indeed, the morning shall be their tryst. is not the morning nigh?" The *mawʿid* — the *tryst*, the *appointed time* — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *used* in *verse 17* for the *Fire as the tryst of the disbelievers*, and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *morning* is *not a* *morning in the ordinary sense*; it is *the appointed time of the account*, in the *sense of being* *the time that the command holds and will execute*, in the *sense of being* *the time that the qarīb of verse 39 describes. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *statement of the tryst* *applied to the people of Lot*, and that the *applied to the people of Lot* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the same tryst*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *coming of the command and the overturning of the city* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *tryst* is *not over* by the *word*; it is *in the morning*, in the way described in *verses 82–83.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:82

> **So when Our Command came, We made its uppermost to be its lowermost, and We rained down upon them stones of baked clay, one upon another,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Command Came" — and the Cry**

The verse opens with the familiar formula: *fa-lammā jāʾa amrunā* — "so when Our Command came." The commentators note that God's Command is also referred to as the Cry that seized them at sunrise in 15:73.

The commentators direct the reader to the commentary on 11:66–68 for the connection between God's Command and the Cry with reference to the destruction of the Thamūd, and read the two accounts as using the same vocabulary for the same kind of event.

The recurrence of the formula across the sūrah is what the commentators emphasize. The commentators note that the same phrase marks the decisive moment at vv. 58, 66, and here, and read the repetition as structuring the narratives.

The commentators note that the timing is given in the parallel account as sunrise. The commentators read this as consistent with the sūrah's pattern of destruction occurring at night and being disclosed at morning.

The verse's compression is characteristic. The commentators observe that the Command's arrival is stated without description of its manner.

**"We Made Its Uppermost to Be Its Lowermost"**

The verse states the destruction: *jaʿalnā ʿāliyahā sāfilahā* — "We made its uppermost to be its lowermost." The commentators explain that this means God caused the city to become completely overturned.

The commentators supply the detail: the overturning made it the lowliest of the cities of the people of Lot after it had been the greatest of them. The commentators record this reading from Ālūsī.

The commentators direct the reader also to 15:75 for the parallel statement. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing the account.

The reversal is what the commentators emphasize. The commentators note that the city's physical inversion corresponds to its change in standing — the greatest made the lowliest — and read the correspondence as deliberate.

The attribution to God in the first person plural is significant. The commentators read *jaʿalnā* as indicating direct divine action rather than a natural catastrophe, which is consistent with the verse's framing as the execution of a Command.

**The Number of Cities — Two Readings**

The commentators record a disagreement about the scale of the destruction and preserve it.

Al-Qushayrī records that the people of Lot lived in five cities. Ālūsī records seven. The commentators note that both readings are preserved and that neither is preferred.

The commentators agree that Sodom was the greatest of the cities, recording this from Ālūsī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qushayrī.

The disagreement concerns the number rather than the substance. The commentators note that the verse itself does not specify a number, and that the figures come from the exegetical tradition.

It is worth recording that the verse speaks of the cities in the plural without enumeration. The commentators' figures are traditional rather than Qur'anic, and the tradition has not settled the count.

**"Stones of Baked Clay, One Upon Another"**

The verse describes the second element of the punishment: *wa-amṭarnā ʿalayhā ḥijāratan min sijjīlin manḍūd* — "and We rained down upon them stones of baked clay, one upon another."

The commentators note that the rain of stones is also referred to at 15:74, and that it is described as a rain in the parallel account at 7:84. The commentators read the recurrence as establishing the account.

The word *sijjīl* denotes baked clay, and the commentators note that the term is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for the stones sent against the people of the Elephant. The commentators read the shared term as indicating a particular kind of divine punishment.

*Manḍūd* — arranged, layered, one upon another — indicates that the stones fell in succession rather than singly.

The verb *amṭarnā* — We rained — is significant. The commentators note that the Qur'an ordinarily uses *anzala* for beneficial sending down and *amṭara* for destructive rain, and read the choice as marking the punishment.

The commentators note that the two elements — the overturning and the rain of stones — are stated together. The commentators read the pairing as describing a single punishment with two aspects rather than two separate punishments.

**The Verse as the Lot Narrative's Execution**

Verse 82 executes the destruction announced at v. 76. The commentators read its placement after the exchange with Abraham as deliberate: the refusal is given, and then the punishment follows.

The commentators note that no further warning or respite is recorded between the refusal and the execution. The commentators read the sequence as reflecting the finality of the decree stated at v. 76.

The verse's two elements correspond to the city and its people. The commentators read the overturning as directed at the settlement and the rain of stones as directed at the inhabitants.

The connection to v. 83 is immediate, where the stones are described as marked with thy Lord and not far from the wrongdoers. The commentators read the two verses as description and application.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the nearness stated at v. 83. The commentators read the account as addressing those who would treat the destruction as distant history, and note that the verse explicitly denies the distance.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:83

> **marked by thy Lord; and they are never far from the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Marked by Thy Lord" — and the Mark That the Sūrah Names**

The sūrah now completes the description of the stones with the word that is the verse's central note: *mawḍūḥatan min rabbik* — "marked by thy Lord." The *wadhʿ* — the *marking*, the *inscribing* — is the *word* that names the *quality* of the *stones*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *stones* are *not* *stones in the ordinary sense*; they are *marked* in the *sense of being* *the stones that bear the mark of the Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the stones that are not the products of the random. The *commentators* *note* that it is *believed that each stone had inscribed upon it the name of the person for whom it was intended*, and that the *inscription of the name* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *rain* is *not a* *rain that falls indiscriminately*; it is *the rain that falls upon the named*, in the *sense of being* *the stones that the Lord has appointed to the specific persons*, in the *sense of being* *the stones that the command releases in the order of the appointment. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *command* is *not a* *general act*; it is *the act that the Lord marks and appoints*, in the way described in *verse 40's statement of the exception in the family of Noah. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *statement of the marked stones* *applied to the people of Lot*, and that the *applied to the people of Lot* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the same appointment*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen.

**"And They Are Never Far from the Wrongdoers" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *final* *clause* of the *verse* is the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with particular weight: *wa-mā hiya min al-ẓālimīn bi-bāʿid* — "and they are never far from the wrongdoers." The *clause* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *stones* are *not* *a past event* that the *Makkans* can *observe from a distance*; they are *never far from the wrongdoers* in the *sense of being* *the stones that are within the reach of the wrongdoers of every age*, in the *sense of being* *the stones that the command can release upon the Makkans in the same form*. The *commentators* *note* that the *clause* is *understood to mean* that the *stones were not distant from the people of Lot*, and that *alternately* it can *mean* that the *stones could also be sent upon the Makkan idolaters*, thus *functioning as a threat*, and that the *two readings together* form the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *threat* is *not a* *threat of the past*; it is *the threat of the present*, in the way described in *verse 39's statement of the punishment that disgraces and the punishment that endures. The sūrah is stating, in the clause, that the *Makkans* are *not* *hearing the story of the stones as a history*; they are *hearing the stones as a threat*, in the sense of being *the ones who are the wrongdoers to whom the stones are never far. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — *the story of Shuʿayb* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *threat is not over* by the *stones*; it is *turning to the prophet of Midian*, in the way described in *verse 84.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:84

> **And unto Midian, their brother Shuʿayb. He said, "O my people! Worship God! You have no god other than Him. Diminish not the measure and the balance. Truly I see you faring well, but I fear for you the punishment of an all-encompassing Day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And Unto Midian, Their Brother Shuʿayb" — The Story That the Sūrah Turns To**

The sūrah now opens the story of Shuʿayb with the same form it has used throughout the narrative middle: *wa-ilā Madyana akhāhum Shuʿayb* — "and unto Midian, their brother Shuʿayb." The *commentators* *note* that *Shuʿayb* was *also an Arabian prophet* who is *identified by many Muslim authorities with the Biblical figure Jethro, the father-in-law of Moses*, and that he *was sent to the people of Midian*, who *resided in northwestern Arabia*. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not a* *prophet of a different kind* from the *prophets of the narrative middle*; he is *the brother of his people* in the *sense of being* *the one of them who speaks to them as one of them*, in the way described in *verse 50's statement of the brother of the ʿĀd. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *story of the prophet of Midian*, and that the *hearing the story* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *own prophet* is *the brother of the Makkans* in the *same way*, in the way described in *verse 27's statement of the notables' objection to the prophet as a human like them.

**"Diminish Not the Measure and the Balance" — The Command That Is Specific to Midian**

The *call of Shuʿayb* includes a *command* that the *calls of the other prophets* did not state in the *same form*: *lā tukhusisū-l-mīzāna wa-l-mīzān* — "diminish not the measure and the balance." The *command* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call* is *not a* *general exhortation*; it is *a call addressed to the specific evil of Midian*, in the *sense of being* *the evil of the fraud in the measure and the balance*, in the *sense of being* *the evil of the diminishment of the people's goods in the transaction. The *commentators* *note* that the *diminishment of the measure and the balance* was *also a problem among the Makkan idolaters during the time of the Prophet*, and that the *problem* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *not hearing a foreign command*; they are *hearing their own command*, in the way described in *Sūrah 83* — the *sūrah of the defrauders*. The sūrah is stating, in the command, that the *call of Shuʿayb* is *not a* *call that separates the worship from the transaction*; it is *the call that unites the two*, in the sense of being *the call that states that the worship of God and the honesty in the measure are one act*, in the sense of being *the call that the Makkans are being asked to hear in their own marketplaces.

**"Truly I See You Faring Well" — The Word That the Sūrah Records**

The *second* *part* of the *call* is the *word* that the *sūrah* records with particular care: *wa-innī arākum khayran* — "truly I see you faring well."
