The *khayr* — the *good*, the *faring well* — is the *word* that names the *state* of *Midian* in the *Shuʿayb's* *perception*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *calling his people from a state of deprivation*; he is *calling them from a state of prosperity*, in the *sense of being* *the prophet who speaks to the people who are faring well*, in the *sense of being* *the one who sees the good of the people and warns them of the punishment in the midst of the good. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *warning* is *not a* *warning of the desperate*; it is *the warning of the one who loves the people in their prosperity*, in the way described in *verse 26's statement of the warner who fears for his people. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *word of the prophet who sees them faring well*, and that the *hearing the word* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet* is *not* *warning the Makkans in their deprivation*; he is *warning them in their prosperity*, in the way described in *verse 12's statement of the constrained breast.

**"But I Fear for You the Punishment of an All-Encompassing Day" — The Warning That Closes the Call**

The *verse* *closes* with the *warning*: *wa-innī akhāfu ʿalaykum ʿadhāba yawmin ʿāmil* — "but I fear for you the punishment of an all-encompassing Day." The *ʿāmil* — the *all-encompassing* — is the *word* that names the *quality* of the *day*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *day* is *not a* *day that leaves anything outside*; it is *the day that encompasses all* in the *sense of being* *the day that the account includes*, in the *sense of being* *the day that the measure and the balance are weighed*. The sūrah is stating, in the closing, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *warning his people of a general evil*; he is *warning them of the specific day that encompasses the specific evil of the measure and the balance*, in the way described in *verse 39's pairing of the two punishments. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *Midianites' response* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *call is not heeded*, in the way described in *verse 87.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:85

> **O my people! Observe fully the measure and the balance with justice and diminish not people's goods, and behave not wickedly upon the earth, working corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shuʿayb and the People of Midian**

The verse records Shuʿayb's address to his people. The commentators supply the context: for the story of Shuʿayb, see also 7:85–93 and 26:176–89.

The commentators note that Shuʿayb was an Arabian prophet who is identified by many Muslim authorities with the Biblical figure Jethro, the father-in-law of Moses. The commentators record the identification without asserting it as certain.

The commentators explain that Shuʿayb was sent to the people of Midian, who resided in northwestern Arabia, and direct the reader to 7:85c for the fuller account.

The commentators note that, like other prophets, he called his people to worship the One God. The commentators read the call to worship as the foundation on which the commercial instructions rest.

It is worth recording that the identification with Jethro is traditional rather than Qur'anic. The commentators note that the Qur'an does not name him as Moses's father-in-law, and that the identification comes from the exegetical tradition.

**"Observe Fully the Measure and the Balance With Justice"**

The verse's first instruction is *awfū l-mikyāla wa-l-mīzāna bi-l-qisṭ* — "observe fully the measure and the balance with justice." The commentators direct the reader to the parallels at 7:85 and 26:181–82.

The commentators explain that the instruction means the Midianites should be honest in their business relations. The commentators read the command as addressing commercial practice specifically.

The verb *awfā* — to give in full, to complete — requires not merely accuracy but fullness, which excludes short measure.

The two instruments are named: *mikyāl*, the measure of dry goods, and *mīzān*, the balance or scale. The commentators read the pairing as covering both forms of commercial quantity.

The phrase *bi-l-qisṭ* — with justice, with equity — qualifies the manner. The commentators read it as indicating that the instruments are to be used justly, not merely that they exist.

The commentators note that the same instruction appears in the parallel accounts, and read the recurrence as indicating that commercial honesty was a central element of Shuʿayb's message.

**"Diminish Not People's Goods"**

The verse adds a prohibition: *wa-lā tabkhasū n-nāsa ashyāʾahum* — "and diminish not people's goods." The commentators direct the reader to the parallels at 7:85 and 26:183.

The commentators explain that this means they should give people the full measure of what they paid for. The commentators read the prohibition as the negative counterpart to the preceding command.

The verb *bakhasa* denotes to diminish, to undervalue, to short-change. The commentators read it as covering any reduction of what is due, whether by measure, by quality, or by valuation.

The commentators note that this was also a problem among the Midianites specifically, and read the instruction as addressed to an actual practice rather than a hypothetical one.

The object is *ashyāʾahum* — their things, their goods. The commentators read the possessive as significant: the goods belong to the people, and diminishing them is taking what is not one's own.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's broader treatment of wealth. The commentators note that several of the destroyed communities are characterized by their relation to provision, and read the Midianites' case as distinctive in that the wrongdoing is commercial.

**"Behave Not Wickedly Upon the Earth, Working Corruption"**

The verse closes with a general prohibition: *wa-lā taʿthaw fī l-arḍi mufsidīn* — "and behave not wickedly upon the earth, working corruption." The commentators note the two terms and their relationship.

The verb *ʿathā* denotes acting wickedly or destructively, and *mufsidīn* is the active participle of working corruption. The commentators read the pairing as describing both the act and its character.

The phrase *fī l-arḍ* — upon the earth — makes the corruption public and social rather than private.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's use of the same phrase at v. 116, where the question is asked why there were not those who would forbid corruption upon the earth. The commentators read the two as framing the same concern.

The commentators note that the commercial instructions are followed by a general prohibition, and read the sequence as indicating that dishonest trade is a form of corruption upon the earth rather than a private failing.

**The Verse in the Shuʿayb Narrative**

Verse 85 sits within the Shuʿayb narrative that runs from v. 84 to v. 95. The commentators read the sequence as moving from call, to commercial instruction, to rejection, to destruction.

The verse's distinctiveness within the sūrah is worth noting. The commentators observe that no other narrative in Hūd gives commercial instruction at this length, and read the Shuʿayb account as the sūrah's treatment of economic ethics.

The commentators connect the instructions to the parallel accounts at 7:85–93 and 26:176–89, and note that the commercial element is consistent across them.

The connection to v. 86 is immediate, where Shuʿayb states that the remnant from God is better. The commentators read the two verses as instruction and ground.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the relation between worship and commerce. The commentators read the verse as establishing that honesty in trade is part of the prophetic message rather than separable from it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:86

> **The remnant from God is better for you, if you are believers, and I am not a keeper over you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Remnant From God Is Better for You"**

The verse gives the ground of the preceding instructions: *baqiyyatu Llāhi khayrun lakum* — "the remnant from God is better for you." The commentators note that the phrase has been read in several ways.

The word *baqiyya* denotes what remains, a residue or remainder. The commentators read it here as referring to what remains of one's wealth after honest dealing, as distinct from what is gained by short measure.

The commentators connect the phrase to the sūrah's use of the same root at v. 116, where the question is asked why there were not those possessing merit — *ūlū baqiyya* — among the earlier generations. The commentators read the shared term as significant.

The comparative *khayr* — better — sets two gains against each other, and the honest gain is the better of them.

The commentators note that the verse does not promise that honest dealing will increase wealth. The commentators read the statement as a comparison of quality rather than a guarantee of quantity.

**"If You Are Believers"**

The verse qualifies the statement: *in kuntum muʾminīn* — "if you are believers." The commentators note the conditional and its function.

The commentators read the condition as indicating that the claim is apprehended only by believers. The commentators observe that the goodness of the remnant is not evident to one who does not believe, since it depends on the reward that belief discloses.

The conditional is not a doubt about their belief. The commentators read it as addressing the ground on which the statement can be accepted rather than questioning whether they believe.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of belief and perception. The commentators note that elsewhere the Qur'an describes the disbelievers as unable to hear or see, and read the condition here as consistent with that treatment.

The verse thus makes the commercial instruction depend on a prior disposition. The commentators read this as indicating that honesty in trade is not merely a social rule but a consequence of belief.

**"I Am Not a Keeper Over You"**

The verse closes with a disclaimer: *wa-mā anā ʿalaykum bi-ḥafīẓ* — "and I am not a keeper over you." The commentators note the phrase's function in the prophetic address.

The word *ḥafīẓ* denotes one who watches, guards, or keeps account. The commentators read the disclaimer as indicating that Shuʿayb cannot enforce the instruction or monitor its observance.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's use of the same term at v. 57, where Hūd says that his Lord is Watcher over all things. The commentators read the two as complementary: the prophet is not the keeper, but God is.

The disclaimer's function is to locate responsibility. The commentators read it as indicating that the instruction has been given and that its observance rests with the hearers, since the messenger cannot compel it.

The commentators note that the phrase recurs in the Qur'an's prophetic addresses, and read the recurrence as establishing a limit on the prophetic task. The commentators connect this to the prohibition of compulsion elsewhere in the Qur'an.

**The Three Clauses and Their Logic**

The verse's three statements work together, and the commentators read their sequence as deliberate.

The first clause supplies the ground: the remnant from God is better. The commentators read this as answering the motive for dishonesty, which is the expectation of greater gain.

The second clause supplies the condition: if you are believers. The commentators read this as identifying the disposition in which the ground is apprehended.

The third clause supplies the limit: I am not a keeper over you. The commentators read this as locating responsibility with the hearers.

The commentators note that the three together form a complete address: here is why you should do it, here is what makes the reason visible, and here is the fact that I cannot make you do it. The commentators read the structure as characteristic of the prophetic address.

**The Verse in the Shuʿayb Narrative**

Verse 86 completes the instructional section of the Shuʿayb narrative that begins at v. 84. The commentators read the sequence as call, instruction, ground, and disclaimer.

The commentators note that the narrative then turns to the people's response at v. 87, where they ask whether his prayer commands him to abandon what their fathers worshipped. The commentators read the shift as marking the rejection.

The verse's ground in the remnant from God is what the commentators draw out. The commentators read it as addressing the Midianites' specific failing, which was commercial, and note that the answer is framed in commercial terms.

The connection to the refrain at v. 95 is instructive. The commentators note that the narrative ends with the imprecation pronounced on the Midianites, and read the sequence as instruction, rejection, destruction, verdict.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the relation between honest dealing and belief. The commentators read the verse as establishing that the two are connected, and that the connection is apprehended only from within belief.

The commentators also note the disclaimer's relevance. The commentators read it as addressing any expectation that religious instruction carries enforcement, and observe that Shuʿayb explicitly disclaims the role.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:87

> **They said, "O Shuʿayb! Does your manner of praying require that we forsake that which our fathers worshipped, or that we should not do as we will with our wealth? Surely you are a man of forbearance and sound judgment."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Does Your Manner of Praying Require..." — the Question as Mockery**

The verse records the Midianites' answer to Shuʿayb: *yā Shuʿaybu a-ṣalātuka taʾmuruka an natruka mā yaʿbudu ābāʾunā* — "O Shuʿayb! Does your manner of praying require that we forsake that which our fathers worshipped?"

Al-Kisāʾī and al-Qushayrī note that the question is a form of mockery. The commentators read the interrogative as rhetorical, and observe that the Midianites are not seeking information but expressing contempt.

Attributing the command to his prayer reduces the message to a personal religious practice, and the framing dismisses it as such.

The object of the forsaking is *mā yaʿbudu ābāʾunā* — that which our fathers worshipped. The commentators connect this to the sūrah's recurring diagnosis at v. 109, where the idolaters worship as their fathers worshipped before.

The commentators note that the appeal to ancestral practice is the standard refusal in the sūrah's narratives, and read the Midianites' formulation as an instance of the pattern.

**"Or That We Should Not Do As We Will With Our Wealth"**

The verse gives the second half of the question: *aw an nafʿala fī amwālinā mā nashāʾ* — "or that we should not do as we will with our wealth?" The commentators note that this addresses the commercial instructions of v. 85.

*Mā nashāʾ* — what we will — is a claim of unrestricted discretion over one's own property.

The commentators connect this to the instructions Shuʿayb had given about full measure and not diminishing people's goods. The commentators read the question as rejecting the premise that commerce is subject to religious instruction.

The claim is significant for what it asserts. The commentators read the Midianites as maintaining that property is a private domain in which religious command has no standing, and observe that this is the substantive objection behind the mockery.

The commentators note that the two halves of the question correspond to the two parts of Shuʿayb's message — worship and commerce. The commentators read the question as rejecting both in a single formulation.

**"Surely You Are a Man of Forbearance and Sound Judgment"**

The verse closes with what appears to be praise: *innaka la-anta l-ḥalīmu r-rashīd* — "surely you are a man of forbearance and sound judgment." The commentators read the statement as irony.

Al-Qushayrī notes that even in their estimation of Shuʿayb as a man of forbearance and sound judgment, they intimate that he has these qualities only in his own opinion. The commentators read the apparent compliment as a dismissal.

The doubled emphasis in *innaka la-anta* marks the statement as exaggerated, and that is what signals the irony.

The two qualities named are worth noting. The commentators observe that *ḥalīm* and *rashīd* are genuine virtues, and that the Midianites' use of them as irony is what makes the mockery effective.

The commentators note that the sūrah describes Abraham at v. 75 with the same term *ḥalīm*. The commentators read the reuse as significant: the quality the Midianites mock in Shuʿayb is the quality the sūrah commends in Abraham.

**The Midianites' Standing Response**

The commentators supply the context of the Midianites' attitude, and it is worth setting out.

The commentators note that the Midianites were arrogant, as recorded at 7:88, and that they did not take seriously Shuʿayb's message. The commentators record that they referred to him as but one of the bewitched, at 26:185, and among the liars, at 26:186.

The commentators read the verse's mockery as consistent with these other designations. The commentators observe that the apparent praise here and the open insult there are two registers of the same dismissal.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of the notables throughout. The commentators note that in nearly every narrative the leaders respond to the messenger with charges against his person rather than answers to his message.

The pattern is what the commentators draw out. The commentators read the Midianites' response as an instance of a fixed reaction, and observe that the sūrah's audience would have recognized it.

**The Verse in the Shuʿayb Narrative**

Verse 87 records the rejection that follows the instructions at vv. 85–86. The commentators read the sequence as call, instruction, ground, disclaimer, and then refusal.

The commentators note that the refusal addresses both parts of the message. The commentators read this as indicating that the Midianites understood the scope of what was being asked and rejected it whole.

The verse's irony is what makes it distinctive within the sūrah. The commentators observe that no other community in Hūd responds with apparent praise, and read the distinctiveness as marking the Midianites' particular arrogance.

The connection to v. 88 is immediate, where Shuʿayb answers with the clear proof and the statement of his own consistency. The commentators read the two verses as mockery and answer.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the reception of instruction. The commentators read the verse as addressing the ways in which a message is dismissed without being answered, and note that the dismissal takes the form of compliment.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:88

> **He said, "O my people! What think you: if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord, and He has provided me with goodly provision from Himself? I desire not to do behind your backs that which I forbid you. I desire naught but to set matters aright so far as I am able; but my success lies with God alone. In Him do I trust and unto Him do I turn."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"If I Stand Upon a Clear Proof From My Lord"**

The verse records Shuʿayb's answer: *yā qawmi a-raʾaytum in kuntu ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbī* — "O my people! What think you: if I stand upon a clear proof from my Lord?" The commentators explain that the clear proof is said to be in the form of miracles.

The commentators note that miracles are given to all prophets by God to prove their veracity, and direct the reader to 7:85c for the fuller account.

The word *bayyina* denotes a clear proof, something manifest. The commentators read it as indicating evidence that is self-evident rather than requiring argument.

The phrase *min rabbī* — from my Lord — locates the proof's source. The commentators read this as significant: the proof is not Shuʿayb's own achievement but a provision from God.

The interrogative *a-raʾaytum* — what think you, have you considered — invites the Midianites to consider a hypothesis rather than asserting a fact.

**"He Has Provided Me With Goodly Provision From Himself"**

The verse continues: *wa-razaqanī minhu rizqan ḥasanā* — "and He has provided me with goodly provision from Himself." The commentators note that the provision is described alongside the proof.

The commentators read the pairing as significant. The commentators observe that Shuʿayb's standing rests on two provisions from God — the proof and the sustenance — and that neither is his own.

The word *rizq* denotes provision, sustenance. The commentators read its use here as connecting the commercial instructions to the source of all provision.

*Minhu* — from Himself — excludes any other source, which is consistent with the instructions about not diminishing people's goods.

The commentators connect this to v. 86's statement that the remnant from God is better. The commentators read the two as complementary: the provision is from God, and what remains of it after honest dealing is the better part.

**"I Desire Not to Do Behind Your Backs That Which I Forbid You"**

The verse states Shuʿayb's consistency: *wa-mā urīdu an ukhālifakum ilā mā anhākum ʿanhu* — "I desire not to do behind your backs that which I forbid you." The commentators note the phrase's force.

The commentators read the statement as declaring that Shuʿayb does not privately practise what he publicly prohibits. The commentators observe that this answers the Midianites' mockery by establishing his integrity.

*Ilā mā anhākum ʿanhu* — to that which I forbid you — applies the prohibition to himself first.

The commentators connect this to the commercial instructions. The commentators observe that Shuʿayb's message concerned honest dealing, and that his claim of consistency is therefore a claim about his own trade.

The commentators note that the verse does not merely deny hypocrisy but states a principle. The commentators read the statement as establishing that the messenger is subject to the message.

**"I Desire Naught but to Set Matters Aright"**

The verse continues: *in urīdu illā l-iṣlāḥa mā staṭaʿtu* — "I desire naught but to set matters aright so far as I am able." The commentators note the restrictive *illā* — nothing but.

The word *iṣlāḥ* denotes setting right, reforming. The commentators connect it to the root used at v. 117, where God is said not to destroy the towns while their people are reforming.

*Mā staṭaʿtu* — so far as I am able — limits the claim to Shuʿayb's capacity, and the limitation is stated rather than implied.

The commentators read the two clauses together: the desire is exclusively for reform, and its extent is bounded by ability. The commentators observe that this excludes both ulterior motive and exaggerated claim.

The commentators connect this to v. 86's disclaimer that he is not a keeper over them. The commentators read the two as complementary: he seeks reform but cannot compel it.

**"My Success Lies With God Alone"**

The verse states the ground: *wa-mā tawfīqī illā bi-Llāh* — "but my success lies with God alone." The commentators note that this is a well-known expression in everyday Muslim discourse employed in many diverse contexts.

The commentators explain that the phrase conveys the idea that no matter what one does, it is ultimately up to God whether it will be successful and carry a blessing in this world and in the next.

The commentators note the phrase's currency beyond the exegetical context. The commentators read this as indicating that the verse supplies a formula that entered ordinary religious speech.

The word *tawfīq* denotes being enabled to succeed, and the commentators read its attribution to God alone as excluding any claim of self-sufficiency.

The commentators connect this to the preceding clause about ability. The commentators read the two together: the effort is bounded by ability, and the outcome depends on God.

**"In Him Do I Trust and Unto Him Do I Turn"**

The verse closes: *ʿalayhi tawakkaltu wa-ilayhi unīb* — "in Him do I trust and unto Him do I turn." The commentators note that this statement is identical to the statement the Prophet is taught to make in 42:10.

The commentators direct the reader also to 7:88–89c for the parallel in the Shuʿayb account in Sūrat al-Aʿrāf.

The two verbs are worth distinguishing. The commentators note that *tawakkul* denotes reliance or entrusting, while *ināba* denotes turning back in repentance. The commentators read the pairing as covering both dependence and return.

The commentators observe that the verse ends on this note rather than on the argument. The commentators read the placement as indicating that the answer to mockery is not counter-mockery but a statement of reliance.

The commentators connect the verse's structure as a whole. The commentators read the sequence as proof, provision, consistency, purpose, dependence, and reliance — and observe that each clause removes a ground on which the message might be dismissed.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:89

> **O my people! Let not your dissension with me lead you into sin, such that there should befall you that which befell the people of Noah, or the people of Hūd, or the people of Ṣāliḥ; and the people of Lot are not far from you!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Let Not Your Dissension with Me Lead You into Sin" — The Warning That the Sūrah Gives**

Shuʿayb's next word to his people is the *warning* that the sūrah gives with particular weight: *yā qawmī lā yaʾtikum khaṣmukum an taʿmalū* — "O my people! let not your dissension with me lead you into sin." The *warning* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites'* *dissension* — the *mockery of verse 87*, the *reframe of the command*, the *claim of the wealth* — is *not a* *harmless* *act*; it is *the act that leads into the sin*, in the *sense of being* *the act that produces the fraud and the corruption of verse 85*, in the *sense of being* *the act that the warning addresses in its causal form. The sūrah is stating, in the warning, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *asking his people to stop the dissension out of respect for him*; he is *asking them to stop it because it leads into the sin*, in the way described in *5:2 and 5:8* — the *way* in which the *Qur'an* commands *not letting anger or resentment, even when justified, lead one to sin*. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans'* *dissension with the Prophet* is *the same dissension*, and that the *same dissension* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *mockery* is *not a* *harmless* *act*; it is *the act that leads into the sin of the fraud and the harm to the believers*, in the way described in *verse 38's statement of the mockery that the believers will laugh at.

**"That There Should Befall You That Which Befell the People of Noah" — and the Naming of the Four Peoples**

The *second* *part* of the *warning* is the *naming* of the *four peoples* that the *dissension will bring upon the Midianites if it continues*: *fa-yumsakākum mā amsaka qawma Nūḥin wa-qawma Hūdin wa-qawma Ṣāliḥ* — "such that there should befall you that which befell the people of Noah, or the people of Hūd, or the people of Ṣāli." The *naming of the three peoples* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *warning his people by a general threat*; he is *warning them by the specific names of the specific peoples that the sūrah has just narrated*, in the *sense of being* *the one who says to them: the flood that took the people of Noah, the wind that took the people of Hūd, the cry that took the people of Ṣāliḥ — these are the forms that your dissension will bring upon you*. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *inventing the threat*; he is *citing the history that the sūrah has just stated*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *naming of the four peoples* *applied to their own dissension*, and that the *applied to their own dissension* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *fate* is *not a* *new* *fate*; it is *the fate of the four peoples*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the share undiminished.

**"And the People of Lot Are Not Far from You" — The Word That the Sūrah Adds**

The *final* *clause* of the *warning* is the *word* that the *sūrah* adds with *particular* *weight*: *wa-qawmu Lūṭin lā bayʿadakum* — "and the people of Lot are not far from you!" The *word* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people of Lot* are *not* *a people in the distance* from the *Midianites*; they are *the most recent and the closest in time* of the *destroyed peoples* to the *Midianites*, in the *sense of being* *the people whose destruction is the nearest in the history that the Midianites can observe*, in the *sense of being* *the people whose stones are the nearest in the geography that the Midianites can see. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *warning his people by a distant history*; he is *warning them by the nearest history*, in the way described in *verse 83's statement of the stones that are never far from the wrongdoers. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *word of the nearness* *applied to their own history*, and that the *applied to their own history* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *nearest destroyed people* are *the peoples that the sūrah has just narrated*, in the way described in *verse 100's statement of the stories of the towns. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *call to seek forgiveness* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *warning is not the end of the word*, in the way described in *verse 90.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:90

> **And seek forgiveness from your Lord; then turn unto Him in repentance. Truly my Lord is Merciful, Loving."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Seek Forgiveness From Your Lord"**

The verse gives the instruction: *wa-staghfirū rabbakum* — "and seek forgiveness from your Lord." The commentators note that the same instruction is also uttered by the prophet Hūd, and read the recurrence as significant.

The verb *istaghfara* denotes seeking forgiveness, and the commentators read it as a request rather than a declaration. The commentators observe that the instruction is to ask, not merely to cease.

The possessive in *rabbakum* — your Lord — is significant: the Lord from whom forgiveness is sought is theirs, not a foreign deity.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of the destroyed communities. The commentators note that in each case the warning included an invitation to repentance, and read the recurrence as establishing the pattern.

The commentators note that the instruction is given before the threat rather than after it. The commentators read the sequence as indicating that the invitation precedes the warning.

**"Then Turn Unto Him in Repentance"**

The verse continues: *thumma tūbū ʿilayh* — "then turn unto Him in repentance." The commentators note the particle *thumma*, which indicates sequence.

The commentators read the two commands as distinct. The commentators observe that seeking forgiveness and turning in repentance are related but not identical, and that the verse gives both.

The verb *tāba* denotes turning, and the commentators read the phrase *ʿilayh* — unto Him — as indicating the direction of the turning. The commentators observe that repentance is defined by its orientation rather than by remorse alone.

The commentators connect this to v. 112's command that those who turn in repentance be steadfast along with the Prophet. The commentators read the two as addressing the same act from different angles.

The commentators note that the sequence is seeking and then turning. The commentators read the order as indicating that the request precedes the reorientation.

**"Truly My Lord Is Merciful, Loving"**

The verse closes with the ground: *inna rabbī raḥīmun wadūd* — "truly my Lord is Merciful, Loving." The commentators note the two names and their pairing.

The name *raḥīm* denotes one who is merciful, and the commentators connect it to the sūrah's repeated attribution of salvation to mercy at vv. 58, 66, and 94.

The name *wadūd* denotes one who loves, and the commentators note that it is distinctive here. The commentators observe that the sūrah's other declarations of divine attributes do not include this name.

The commentators read the pairing as significant. The commentators observe that mercy and love together describe a disposition toward the creatures rather than merely a capacity to forgive.

The commentators connect this to the preceding instruction. The commentators read the two names as the ground on which seeking forgiveness and turning are reasonable rather than futile.

The possessive *rabbī* — my Lord — is worth noting. The commentators observe that Shuʿayb speaks of his own Lord rather than addressing them as theirs in this clause, and have read the shift variously.

**The Verse in the Context of v. 89**

The commentators note that verse 89 precedes with a warning, and that the two verses work together.

Al-Kisāʾī explains that in a sense v. 89 says: let not your enmity toward me cause you to commit sin, so that you become afflicted with the kind of punishment that befell previous generations. The commentators read this as the verse's substance.

The commentators note the principle involved: not letting anger or resentment, even when justified, lead one to sin. The commentators direct the reader to 5:2 and 5:8 for the same principle.

The commentators supply the references for the destructions mentioned: the people of Noah at vv. 25–48, Hūd at vv. 50–60, Ṣāliḥ at vv. 61–68, and Lot at vv. 77–83. The commentators read the enumeration as indicating that the warning is grounded in the sūrah's own narratives.

The commentators note Shuʿayb's remark that the people of Lot are not far from them, and explain that he is pointing out that Lot's community was the most recent one, the closest to them in time, to have perished. The commentators read the proximity as the ground of the warning.

**The Verse as the Shuʿayb Narrative's Final Invitation**

Verse 90 is the last invitation in the Shuʿayb narrative before the exchange closes. The commentators read its placement as significant: the invitation comes after the warning rather than instead of it.

The commentators note that the sūrah's narratives consistently end their prophetic speeches with an invitation, and read the pattern as establishing that the destruction is not the message's purpose.

The verse's two commands and their ground form a complete invitation. The commentators read the sequence as: seek forgiveness, turn, and here is why it is worth doing.

The commentators connect the two names to the sūrah's broader treatment of mercy. The commentators observe that the salvation statements at vv. 58, 66, and 94 all attribute deliverance to mercy, and read the verse as consistent with them.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the availability of the invitation. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who have rejected a message and would assume the matter is closed, and note that the invitation is given after the rejection.

The commentators also note the connection to v. 89's warning about enmity. The commentators read the two verses together: the warning identifies the danger, and the invitation supplies the alternative.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:91

> **They said, "O Shuʿayb! We do not comprehend much of what you say, and verily we consider you to be weak among us. And were it not for your clan, we would surely stone you, for you have no power over us."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Do Not Comprehend Much of What You Say" — The Derision That the Sūrah Records**

The Midianites' final answer to Shuʿayb's call opens with the *derision* that the sūrah records with particular care: *qālū yā Shuʿaybu mā nafqah mimmā taqūlu* — "O Shuʿayb! we do not comprehend much of what you say." The *derision* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *not* *refusing the message because they have considered it and found it false*; they are *refusing it by the statement of their own incomprehension*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who state that they do not understand the prophet's words*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who make the incomprehension the form of the refusal. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Midianites* are *not* *a people who engage with the content*; they are *the ones who state their own incomprehension as the reason*, in the way described in *verse 20's statement of the spiritual deafness and blindness. The *commentators* *note* that the *statement of the incomprehension* is *a way of expressing derision for him*, and that the *derision* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *in* the *state of verse 38's mockery of Noah, in the way described throughout the narrative middle.

**"And Verily We Consider You to Be Weak Among Us" — and the Word That the Sūrah Records**

The *second* *clause* of the *answer* is the *statement* of the *Midianites'* *valuation* of the *Shuʿayb*: *wa-la-naʿtadūka fīnā ʿaẓafā* — "and verily we consider you to be weak among us." The *ʿaẓf* — the *weakness*, the *lowness* — is the *word* that names the *Midianites'* *valuation* of the *Shuʿayb*, and the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *not* *refusing the message because of its content*; they are *refusing it because of the prophet's status in their estimation*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who value the prophet by the social measure*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who state that the prophet is weak among them. The sūrah is stating, in the recording, that the *Midianites* are *in* the *state of verse 27's statement of the notables' social epistemology, in the way described throughout the narrative middle. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* who *despised the believers of low status* are *in the same valuation*, and that the *in the same valuation* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *valuation* is *not the measure that counts*, in the way described in *verse 31's statement of the valuation of God.

**"And Were It Not for Your Clan, We Would Surely Stone You" — The Threat That the Sūrah Records**

The *third* *clause* is the *threat*: *wa-lawlā ʿashīratuka larajamanāk* — "and were it not for your clan, we would surely stone you." The *threat* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *not* *refusing the message by a general statement*; they are *refusing it by the specific threat of the stoning*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who state the act that they would do to the prophet if they could*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who hold the prophet back by the clan. The *commentators* *note* that in *ancient Arabian tribal culture* people *were offered protection by their clan*, and that *the clan to which Shuʿayb belonged was a part of the religious community of the Midianites*, so that *they could not hurt him*, and that the *protection of the clan* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *safe by his own power*; he is *safe by the clan*, in the way described in *verse 80's statement of the Lot's wish for the mighty support. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *threat of the stoning* *applied to their prophet*, and that the *hearing the threat* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *threats against the Prophet and the believers* are *the same threats*, in the way described in *verse 38's statement of the mockery.

**"For You Have No Power over Us" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *statement* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-mā lakunā ʿalaynā min quwwah* — "for you have no power over us." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *not* *refusing the message by the threat only*; they are *refusing it by the statement of their own power*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who state that the prophet has no power over them*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who place their own power above the prophetic authority. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Midianites* are *in* the *state of verse 55's statement of the ʿĀd's scheme against Hūd, in the way described throughout the narrative middle. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *Shuʿayb's final answer* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *statement of the power is not the end of the story*, in the way described in *verse 92.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:92

> **He said, "O my people! Is my clan mightier over you than God? Yet, you would put Him behind you with disregard. Surely my Lord encompasses whatsoever you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Is My Clan Mightier over You than God?" — The Question That the Sūrah Records**

Shuʿayb's answer to the Midianites' statement of the clan is the *question* that the sūrah records with particular weight: *qāla yā qawmī a-ʿashīratī aʿaẓumu ʿalaykum min Allāh* — "O my people! is my clan mightier over you than God?" The *question* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *defending the clan*; he is *placing the clan and God in the same question*, in the *sense of being* *the one who asks the Midianites to compare the two*, in the *sense of being* *the one who states that the clan is not the measure of the might. The sūrah is stating, in the question, that the *Midianites'* *protection of the Shuʿayb by the clan* is *not a* *protection that the prophet claims*; it is *the protection that the Midianites hold*, and the *question* is *the turning of the protection against the holders*, in the way described in *verse 30's statement of the question that inverts the frame. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* who *hold the Prophet back by the clan* are *hearing the question addressed to their own holding*, and that the *hearing the question addressed to the holding* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *clan is not the measure of the might*, in the way described in *verse 56's statement of the trust in the Lord.

**"Yet, You Would Put Him Behind You with Disregard" — and the Word That the Sūrah Names**

The *second* *clause* is the *word* that the *sūrah* names with *particular* *care*: *wa-tatakhayhillūhu anduḥā* — "yet, you would put Him behind you with disregard." The *word* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites* are *not* *forgetting God in the sense of not knowing Him*; they are *putting Him behind them* in the *sense of being* *the ones who cast God behind their backs and are not fearful of Him*, in the *sense of being* *the ones who place God behind the clan and the wealth and the power. The *commentators* *note* that the *word* is *understood to mean* "You would cast God behind your backs and not be fearful of Him?", and that the *casting behind the backs* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites'* *refusal* is *not a* *refusal of the unknown*; it is *the refusal of the known who is placed behind*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the folded breast. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *naming*, that the *Makkans* who *put God behind the clan and the wealth* are *in the same casting*, and that the *in the same casting* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *refusal* is *not a* *refusal of the unknown*; it is *the refusal of the known who is placed behind*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the worship as the fathers worshipped.

**"Surely My Lord Encompasses Whatsoever You Do" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *statement* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-inna rabbi latāwī mā taʿmalūn* — "surely my Lord encompasses whatsoever you do." The *tawī* — the *encompassing* — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *used* in *verse 57* for the *Lord's watching over all things*, and the *recurrence* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Midianites'* *acts* — the *mockery*, the *threat*, the *statement of the power* — are *not* *outside* the *encompassing*, in the *sense of being* *the acts that the Lord holds in the encompassing*, in the *sense of being* *the acts that the account will reach. The sūrah is stating, in the recurrence, that the *Shuʿayb* is *not* *ending his answer with the question*; he is *ending it with the statement of the encompassing*, in the way described in *verse 5's statement of the knowledge of what lies within breasts. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *statement of the encompassing* *applied to their own acts*, and that the *hearing the statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *mockery and threats* are *not* *outside* the *encompassing*, in the way described in *verse 123's statement of the Lord who is not heedless of that which you do. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *word of the mutual watching* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *encompassing is not the end of the story*, in the way described in *verse 93.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:93

> **O my people! Act according to your position; I, too, am acting. Soon you shall know upon whom there comes a punishment that disgraces him, and who is a liar. So keep watch! I, too, am among you watching."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Act According to Your Position; I, Too, Am Acting"**

The verse records Shuʿayb's closing words: *wa-yā qawmiʾ-malū ʿalā makānatikum innī ʿāmil* — "O my people! Act according to your position; I, too, am acting." The commentators note the parallel formulations elsewhere in the Qur'an.

The commentators observe that these words are similar to what the Prophet is instructed to tell the Makkan idolaters, at 6:135 and 39:39, and that they serve as both a warning and a threat. The commentators direct the reader to 6:135c.

The word *makāna* denotes place, station, position. The commentators read the phrase as telling the people to act from where they stand, without pretense.

The commentators note that the statement is not a permission. The commentators read it as a challenge: proceed as you are, and the outcome will disclose which position was sound.

The parallel structure — you act, I act — sets the two courses side by side without argument.

**"Soon You Shall Know Upon Whom There Comes a Punishment"**

The verse continues: *sawfa taʿlamūna man yaʾtīhi ʿadhābun yukhzīh* — "soon you shall know upon whom there comes a punishment that disgraces him." The commentators note the word *yukhzī* and its connection to the sūrah's vocabulary.

The commentators connect this to v. 66, where the believers are saved from the disgrace of that day. The commentators read the two as paired: disgrace falls on one party and the other is saved from it.

The word *sawfa* — soon, in the future — indicates a definite future. The commentators read it as indicating that the knowledge will come, though not immediately.

The commentators note that the verse does not specify who will be disgraced. The commentators read the indirection as significant: the answer is left to the event.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's treatment of delay. The commentators observe that the Midianites, like the other communities, treated the absence of punishment as evidence against the warning.

**"And Who Is a Liar"**

The verse adds a second object of the knowledge: *wa-man huwa kādhib* — "and who is a liar." The commentators note that the question concerns the truthfulness of the messenger.

The commentators connect this to the Midianites' earlier designation of Shuʿayb as among the liars, recorded at 26:186. The commentators read the verse as answering that charge by deferring it to the outcome.

The commentators observe that the verse does not assert who the liar is. The commentators read this as significant: the claim is that the event will settle the question.

The commentators note that this is a recurring feature of the Qur'an's prophetic addresses. The commentators observe that the messengers consistently appeal to the outcome rather than arguing about their own veracity.

The commentators connect this to v. 88's statement of the clear proof. The commentators read the two as complementary: the proof has been given, and the outcome will confirm it.

**"So Keep Watch! I, Too, Am Among You Watching"**

The verse closes: *fa-ntaẓirū innī maʿakum min l-muntaẓirīn* — "so keep watch! I, too, am among you watching." The commentators note the parallel instructions elsewhere.

The commentators observe that the Prophet is told to speak similar words to those who do not believe in his message, at 9:52, 10:20, 10:102, and 32:30, and direct the reader also to 6:135c and 7:87c.

The commentators note that the same is the case with Shuʿayb's statement: they should keep watch, and he too is watching along with them. The commentators read the shared watching as significant.

The verb *intaẓara* denotes waiting, expecting, watching for. The commentators read it as indicating expectation of a known outcome rather than mere waiting.

*Maʿakum* — with you — places Shuʿayb not outside the situation but inside it with them.

The commentators note that the verse ends on this note rather than on a threat. The commentators read the placement as indicating that the final word is expectation rather than condemnation.

**The Verse as the End of the Shuʿayb Exchange**

Verse 93 concludes the exchange between Shuʿayb and his people that begins at v. 84. The commentators read the sequence as call, instruction, refusal, warning, invitation, and challenge.

The commentators note that the narrative then moves immediately to the destruction at v. 94, with no further exchange. The commentators read the abruptness as reflecting the finality of the rejection.

The verse's four statements work together. The commentators read the sequence as: act as you are, the punishment will come, the liar will be known, and we are all waiting.

The commentators connect the verse to the refrain at v. 95. The commentators observe that the challenge is followed by the destruction and then the imprecation, and read the sequence as the sūrah's standard closure.

The pastoral edge the commentators draw concerns the appeal to outcome. The commentators read the verse as addressing those who reject a message and demand proof, and note that the proof offered is the event rather than the argument.

The commentators also note the verse's restraint. The commentators observe that Shuʿayb does not pronounce the punishment himself but defers it to the outcome, and read this as consistent with v. 86's disclaimer that he is not a keeper over them.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:94

> **And when Our Command came, We saved Shuʿayb and those who believed with him, through a Mercy from Us. And the Cry seized those who did wrong, and morning found them lying lifeless in their abode,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Occurrence of One Formula**

This is the third time in the sūrah that a prophet's story ends in the same four movements. The command comes. The believers are saved through a mercy. The cry seizes the wrongdoers. Morning finds them lifeless in their homes. The commentators' note on these two verses says as much in terms: for identical wording in the story of Ṣāliḥ — the coming of the command, the role of mercy in the salvation of the believers, the cry seizing the wrongdoers, morning finding them lifeless as though they had never dwelt there — see 11:66–68. The formula had already appeared for Thamūd. It had appeared for the people of Lot. Now it closes Midian.

The repetition is the sūrah's method. Three peoples in three different places, separated by centuries, destroyed by three different means — an earthquake, an overturning, a cry — are nevertheless narrated in the same words. The reader is being told that the variation in the punishment is incidental and the pattern is not. Each community believed itself particular. The sūrah's prose refuses to let the reader believe it.

**Mercy as the Ground of Salvation**

The verse says the believers were saved through a mercy from Us. It does not say they were saved because they had been righteous, or because their belief was sound, or because they had outlasted the opposition. The ground of the salvation is named as mercy. The same formulation appeared at 11:66 for Ṣāliḥ's believers and at 11:58 for Hūd's.

This matters in a sūrah about punishment. The destruction of the wrongdoers is presented as the consequence of what they did; the salvation of the believers is presented as a gift. The two are not symmetrical, and the verse does not make them symmetrical. Shuʿayb had argued for eighty verses that reform was possible and that his people's ruin was not yet fixed. They refused the possibility. Those who accepted it were then saved by something other than their own merit, and the verse says so in the plainest available terms.

**The Cry and the Single Night**

The cry seized those who did wrong, and morning found them lying lifeless in their abode. The commentators read the verb *aṣbaḥū* — they became in the morning, morning found them — as indicating that the destruction occurred within a single night, and that morning disclosed what had happened rather than causing it. The same reading applied at 11:67 for Thamūd and at 11:94's parallel.

The pattern is exact. Shuʿayb warned them. They answered with contempt and drove the believers out of the city. The cry came. By morning the argument was over and the city was full of the dead. Nothing intervened — no siege, no famine, no gradual decline. The speed is part of the account's claim about the kind of event this was.

**The Pairing with Thamūd**

The next verse draws the comparison explicitly: Away with Midian, just as Thamūd was done away. The commentators note that the two peoples' fates were so similar that the sūrah states the equivalence outright, and they direct the reader to 7:91–92, where the same pairing appears. Both were destroyed by a cry. Both were found lifeless by morning. Both were described afterwards as though they had never dwelt in their homes.

The comparison is not incidental ornament. Thamūd had been the more famous of the two — a people with a recognised name, a known territory, monuments cut into rock. Midian had been a trading town on a route. To say that Midian went as Thamūd went is to say that fame and obscurity made no difference to the outcome, and that the sūrah's catalogue treats the great and the small by one standard.

The commentators direct the reader to 7:91–92 for the same pairing, where the earthquake takes Thamūd and the account of Shuʿayb closes with a comparable formula. That the two sūrahs make the same comparison independently is worth noting: it is not a local device of Hūd but a settled way of reading the two stories together. The sūrah is not inventing the link between Midian and Thamūd; it is stating a connection the tradition already recognised and giving it a place in the sequence.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not say how many of Shuʿayb's people believed, and does not name any of them. It does not describe the cry — its source, its sound, its duration — and does not say whether the believers heard it and were unharmed or whether they were spared by other means. It does not say where the survivors went, and does not record Shuʿayb's own end. The commentators note only that the mercy is named as the ground of the salvation.

The verse also does not say that the punishment was proportionate to the crime in any measurable sense. It presents the sequence — warning, refusal, destruction — and leaves the reader to draw the conclusion the sūrah has been building for eighty verses. Shuʿayb's grief at v93 is the last word from him in the narrative, and after it the account moves on without comment.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:95

> **as though they had never dwelt there. Behold! Away with Midian, just as Thamūd was done away!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"As Though They Had Never Dwelt There" — The Image, Repeated in Its Invariant Form**

The sūrah completes the description of Midian with the *image* that it *used* for the *Thamūd* in *verse 68*: *ka-ʾannihum lam yaʿsarū dhālika* — "as though they had never dwelt there." The *identity of the image* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *destruction of Midian* is *not a* *different kind of erasure* from the *destruction of the Thamūd*; it is *the same erasure* in the *sense of being* *the erasure that leaves the abode as if it had never been dwelt in*, in the *sense of being* *the erasure that the morning finds completed. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *image* *applied to Midian*, and that the *applied to Midian* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *abode* is *in the same erasure*, in the way described in *verse 100's statement of the stories of the towns. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *not* *hearing a foreign image*; they are *hearing the image of their own possible abode*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen.

**"Away with Midian" — The Fourth Strike of the Refrain**

The *sūrah* now *strikes* the *refrain* for the *fourth* *time*: *wailan li-Madyana ka-mā bāt Thamūd* — "away with Midian, just as Thamūd was done away!" The *strike* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that *Midian* is *not a* *people in the distance*; it is *a people done away*, in the *sense of being* *the people that the verdict has spoken over*, in the *sense of being* *the people that the refrain has marked. The sūrah is stating, in the strike, that the *Midian* are *named in the refrain with the reference to the Thamūd*, and that the *named with the reference* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *fates of the two peoples are so similar* that the *refrain names one by the other*, in the way described in *7:91–92. The *four strikes together* — *the people of Noah* in *verse 44*, the *ʿĀd* in *verse 60*, the *Thamūd* in *verse 68*, and *Midian* in *this verse* — form the *sūrah's* *complete verdict on the pattern of refusal*, and the *complete verdict* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern* is *not a* *series of separate events*; it is *one pattern* in the *sense of being* *the pattern that the four strikes mark.

**"Just as Thamūd Was Done Away" — and the Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *final* *clause* is the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *ka-mā bāt Thamūd* — "just as Thamūd was done away." The *word* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *fate of Midian* is *not a* *new fate*; it is *the fate of the Thamūd* in the *sense of being* *the fate that the refrain names by the reference*, in the *sense of being* *the fate that the sūrah has just described in verse 68. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *word of the done away* *applied to Midian by the Thamūd*, and that the *applied by the Thamūd* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *fate* is *not a* *new fate*; it is *the fate of the four peoples*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the share undiminished. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — *the story of Moses* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *pattern is not over* by the *fourth strike*; it is *turning to the prophet of Pharaoh*, in the way described in *verses 96–97.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:96

> **And indeed We sent Moses with Our signs and a manifest authority**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Prophet in the Series**

The sūrah has now carried three complete prophetic cycles — Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ — and has begun a fourth with Lot, whose story closed at v95 with the destruction of Midian. This verse opens the fifth. The commentators list the parallel accounts at length: 2:49–61, 7:103–55, 10:75–93, 20:9–97, 26:10–66, 28:3–46, and 79:15–25. Moses is the most narrated prophet in the Qur'an, and this sūrah's treatment of him is the shortest of the major accounts.

The placement is deliberate. The four preceding prophets were each sent to a single named community and each community was destroyed. Moses is sent to a ruling power. The sūrah is moving from the pattern of a prophet and his town to the pattern of a prophet and a state, and the difference will show in how the story ends.

The brevity of this account relative to the others is itself a feature. At 7:103–55 the story runs for fifty-three verses; at 20:9–97 for eighty-nine; at 26:10–66 for fifty-seven. Here it occupies a handful. The sūrah is not retelling the story but citing it — the reader is expected to know what happened, and the sūrah takes only what it needs for the argument it is making about leadership and its consequences. That argument reaches its conclusion two verses later, which is why the narrative is compressed to the point where the signs are named but never described.

**The Signs**

Moses is sent with Our signs. The commentators gloss the signs as the miracles he performed (Kl), and note that the same thing is elsewhere called clear proofs — at 7:105 and 29:39. At 10:75 the commentators add that the signs include Moses' hand and his staff (Mu).

This is the first prophet in the sūrah to be sent with demonstrable signs. Noah was sent with a message. Hūd was sent with a message. Ṣāliḥ was sent with a message and a she-camel, which was itself a sign but a standing one rather than a performed one. Lot was sent with a message. Moses arrives with things that can be shown, and the sūrah's account of what follows will turn on whether showing them persuades.

The apparatus at 10:75 identifies the signs more concretely as including Moses' hand and his staff (Mu), and the same verse notes that they are elsewhere called clear proofs (29:39). The vocabulary matters: a sign is something that points, and a clear proof is something that points unmistakably. The sūrah's insistence on the clarity is what makes the refusal that follows significant, since the objection cannot be that the evidence was obscure.

**The Manifest Authority**

Alongside the signs, Moses is sent with a manifest authority — *sulṭān mubīn*. The commentators record that some take this to refer to Moses' staff specifically (Q), and direct the reader to 7:106–8 for the account of the staff. Others read it more broadly as the warrant for his mission.

Both readings are preserved. On the narrower reading the authority is the same object as one of the signs, and the phrase singles it out as the sign that carried the commission. On the broader reading the authority is the commission itself — the standing behind the messenger — and the signs are its evidence. The verse does not decide between them, and the commentators do not decide for it.

The word *sulṭān* carries a sense of rightful power rather than mere force, which is why it suits a commission. Pharaoh holds power in the ordinary sense; Moses is sent with authority in the sense of warrant. The sūrah sets the two against each other in the verses that follow, and the outcome turns on which kind of power proves durable. The phrase also anticipates the next verse's verdict on Pharaoh's own command — that it was not sound — since an authority grounded in warrant and a command grounded in position are being measured against the same standard.

**A Different Kind of Opposition**

The verse names Moses' audience as Pharaoh and his notables. The commentators explain at 10:75 that the notables in Pharaoh's entourage were a group of dignitaries who held the highest social standing in Egyptian society (IṬ, Qm, Th), and at 7:103 that the opposition to each of the preceding prophets had likewise been led in part by the notables among the people to whom he was sent.

The continuity is worth marking. In every case so far the resistance came from those with most to lose. The sūrah has been consistent that the argument was never primarily about the truth of the message but about the standing of those who would have to accept it. Pharaoh's case will be the sharpest version of the pattern, because here the notables are not merely prominent citizens but the apparatus of a state.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not say which signs, and does not describe any of them. It does not say when Moses was sent or to which period of Pharaoh's reign, and it does not name Pharaoh. It does not say how long the mission lasted, and does not yet say what the outcome was.

The verse also does not say that the signs were sufficient to compel belief. It states that Moses came with them, and leaves the question of their effect to what follows. The sūrah's own answer, given at the next verse, is that they were not sufficient — and that the reason was not the weakness of the signs but the disposition of those who saw them.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:97

> **unto Pharaoh and his notables. But they followed the command of Pharaoh, and the command of Pharaoh was not sound.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Notables**

Moses is sent to Pharaoh and his notables. The commentators explain that the notables were dignitaries holding the highest social standing in Egyptian society (IṬ, Qm, Th), and note at 7:103 that the opposition to each preceding prophet in the sūrah had likewise been led in part by the notables. The same group is addressed at 7:103 and 10:75.

The word does real work in the sūrah's argument. It identifies who in each community actually decided. The mass of the people are rarely addressed directly in these accounts; the prophets speak to those with authority, and it is those with authority who refuse. The pattern is consistent enough across five prophetic cycles that the sūrah can rely on the reader noticing it without stating it.

At 7:103 the commentators add that the notables were guilty of treating God's signs wrongfully — by disbelieving in them (Ṭ, Z) or by turning others away from them (Z) — and of being workers of corruption. The second charge is the more serious of the two. Disbelief is a position; turning others away is an act with consequences beyond the actor. The notables are therefore not merely unconvinced but active, and the sūrah's account of them is an account of people who used their standing to determine what other people were permitted to believe.

**Following the Command**

But they followed the command of Pharaoh. The verse does not say they were compelled, and does not say they were deceived about what the command was. It says they followed it. At 10:75 the parallel account adds that they waxed arrogant, which the commentators gloss as meaning they were too proud to believe (Mu, My), and cross-reference 40:60: Truly those who are too arrogant to worship Me shall enter Hell debased.

Pride, on this reading, is not a feeling but a posture toward a claim. The notables had standing, and the standing depended on the existing order. To accept Moses would have been to accept that the order rested on something other than their position in it. The commentators' gloss makes the mechanism explicit: the refusal was not a failure to see the evidence but a refusal to occupy the position the evidence would have required.

The cross-reference at 40:60 supplies the consequence: Truly those who are too arrogant to worship Me shall enter Hell debased. The pairing of arrogance with debasement is deliberate, and the following verse in this sūrah enacts it — the one who stood in front of his people will still be in front of them, but the direction will have reversed. The commentators treat the two accounts as describing the same posture from different ends.

**The Command Was Not Sound**

And the command of Pharaoh was not sound. The verse states this as a fact about the command rather than as a judgement on those who followed it. The apparatus offers no gloss on the phrase beyond the cross-references, so the claim stands on the verse's own wording.

The unsoundness is what the sūrah has been demonstrating for ninety-six verses. A command is sound if it can be followed to a good end. Pharaoh's command led to the Fire, and the next verse says so directly. The sūrah is making a structural point about authority: the test of a command is where it leads, and this one led somewhere that the verse then names.

**The Repetition of the Name**

The verse says the command of Pharaoh twice — they followed the command of Pharaoh, and the command of Pharaoh was not sound. The repetition is not incidental. It puts the name in the position where an abstract noun would normally sit, so that the reader is left with the man rather than the office.

The commentators do not gloss the repetition, and the verse does not explain it. What it achieves is that the following verse can then say he shall go before his people and lead them to the Fire. The antecedent of that he is unambiguous, and the verse before it has made sure the reader is thinking of a particular person rather than of authority in general.

At 7:103 the parallel account closes with the command so behold how the workers of corruption fared in the end, which the commentators note is addressed to the Prophet — indicating that these prophetic narratives were meant to give him confidence that God aids His messengers against their enemies. The same function operates here. The sūrah is not narrating Pharaoh for its own sake but placing him in a sequence that the reader is meant to apply to the present case: those who refuse a messenger have refused before, and the outcome is on record.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not say what Pharaoh's command was. It does not record his words, and does not summarise his policy. It does not say how many followed him or whether any refused. It does not say whether the notables agreed with the command or merely complied with it.

The verse also does not say that following an unsound command excuses those who follow it. The next verse holds Pharaoh responsible for leading and his people responsible for being led, and the sūrah nowhere suggests that the second group's culpability is reduced by the first group's initiative.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:98

> **He shall go before his people on the Day of Resurrection and lead them to the Fire. Evil indeed is the watering place to which they are led!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Leader Goes First**

He shall go before his people on the Day of Resurrection and lead them to the Fire. The verse takes up the leadership established in the previous verse and carries it past death. Pharaoh commanded, and his people followed; on the Day of Resurrection he will still be in front, and they will still be behind him, and the destination will have changed.

The commentators connect this to 17:71: On the Day We shall call every people by their imam. The word *imām* carries both senses at once — the one who stands in front, and the one whose example is followed. The verse uses the fact that leadership is positional as well as normative: whoever is in front determines where the group goes, and the group's arrival is not independent of the leader's direction.

The verse is also a deliberate reversal of the leadership the preceding prophets exercised. Noah led the believers into the ark. Hūd's and Ṣāliḥ's and Shuʿayb's believers were saved through a mercy, and their prophets stood with them. In each case the leader's direction was toward safety, and the followers arrived where the leader led. Pharaoh is the counter-case: the same structure, the same willingness to follow, and the opposite destination. The sūrah has spent ninety-seven verses establishing that following a prophet is safe, and now shows what following anyone else costs.

**The Watering Place**

Evil indeed is the watering place to which they are led. The commentators explain that watering place renders *wird*, a word usually associated with the quenching of thirst, and that the choice of it emphasises the severity of the punishment (Z). The thing the led are brought to is the opposite of what the word normally means.

The irony is exact and does not need elaboration. A *wird* is where a traveller arrives after a journey and is relieved. The verse uses the word for arrival-and-relief to name an arrival that is the beginning of punishment. The commentators note the effect rather than explaining the mechanism: the word's ordinary sense is what makes its use here severe.

**The Inversion of the Shepherd**

The image of a leader conducting his people to a watering place is a familiar one, and it is ordinarily benevolent. The shepherd brings the flock to water. The guide brings the caravan to the well. The verse takes that image and reverses only its destination, keeping everything else — the order, the trust, the following — intact.

What makes the reversal sharp is that the followers' behaviour is unchanged. They are doing what followers are supposed to do. They are behind their leader, going where he goes, expecting what followers expect. The verse does not say they were tricked at the last moment or that they realised too late. It says he led them and they were led, and the place was evil.

**The Continuity with the Previous Verse**

The two verses work as a pair. Verse 97 says they followed the command of Pharaoh and that the command was not sound. Verse 98 says he will go before them and lead them to the Fire. The unsoundness named in the first verse is given its destination in the second.

The sūrah's argument about authority is therefore complete in two lines. A command is not sound when it leads somewhere evil. Those who follow it arrive where it leads. The leader arrives first and is responsible for the direction; the followers arrive behind and are not excused by the fact that they were following. Both claims are stated without qualification, and the verse does not soften either.

The commentators' note on these two verses adds that the people were pursued in this world by a curse — read either as a curse in general (Z) or specifically as their drowning in the Red Sea (Ṭ; see 7:136). The verse itself does not specify, and the two readings are left open. What both share is that the consequence is not deferred entirely to the Hereafter: something pursued them here, and the leading described in v98 is its completion rather than its beginning.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not say how Pharaoh will lead them — whether by command, by example, or simply by being in front. It does not say whether the people will follow willingly or be driven. It does not describe the Fire, and does not say what happens to them on arrival.

The verse also does not say that Pharaoh will be punished more severely than those he led, though the sūrah's treatment of leaders elsewhere implies a greater accountability. What it states is the order of arrival and the identity of the destination, and it leaves the distribution of responsibility to the reader's inference from the two verses together.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:99

> **And they were pursued in this [world] by a curse, and [shall be] on the Day of Resurrection. Evil indeed is the gift that will be offered them!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Were Pursued in This World by a Curse" — The Double Verdict, Repeated in Its Invariant Form**

The sūrah states the *fate* of the *Pharaoh and his notables* in the *same form* stated for the *ʿĀd* in *verse 60*: *wa-laḥiqat-humu-l-laʿnatu fī hādhihi wa- yawma-l-qiyāmah* — "they were pursued in this world by a curse, and [shall be] on the Day of Resurrection." The *identity of the double verdict* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *fate of the Pharaoh and his notables* is *not a* *different kind of fate* from the *fate of the ʿĀd*; it is *the same fate* in the *sense of being* *the fate that is pursued in this world and in the next*, in the *sense of being* *the fate that the curse holds in the two times. The sūrah is stating, in the identity, that the *Pharaoh and his notables* are *not* *a people with a different curse* from the *ʿĀd*; they are *the ones whom the same curse pursues*, in the way described in *verse 18's statement of the curse as exile from the mercy. The *commentators* *note* that the *curse in this world* can *refer to a curse in general* or *specifically to their being drowned in the Red Sea*, and that the *drowning in the Red Sea* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *curse in this world* is *not a* *abstract* *curse*; it is *the specific event that the curse holds*, in the way described in *7:136. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *double verdict* *applied to the Pharaoh and his notables*, and that the *applied to the Pharaoh and his notables* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans* are *in the same double verdict*, in the way described in *verse 49's statement of the accounts of the unseen.

**"Evil Indeed Is the Gift That Will Be Offered Them" — The Word That the Sūrah Closes With**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *sawāʾin ʿaṭāʾin yuʿṭāna* — "evil indeed is the gift that will be offered them!" The *ʿaṭā* — the *gift*, the *offering* — is the *word* that the *sūrah* *uses* for the *destination* of the *wrongdoers* in the *form of the offering*, and the *use* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *destination* is *not a* *destination that the wrongdoers can receive without the evil*, in the *sense of being* *the offering that the sūrah characterizes as the evil*, in the *sense of being* *the gift that the curse of the double verdict holds. The sūrah is stating, in the word, that the *Makkans'* *destination* — the *destination that the Makkans' refusal holds* — is *not a* *destination that the sūrah characterizes as the good*, in the way described in *verse 23's statement of the inhabitants of the Garden. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* are *hearing* the *word of the evil offering* *applied to the Pharaoh and his notables*, and that the *applied to the Pharaoh and his notables* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *offering* is *the same offering*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the share undiminished. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *statement of the stories of the towns* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *narrative middle is not over* by the *story of Moses*, in the way described in *verses 100–102.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:100

> **These are among the stories We have recounted unto thee of the towns. Among them are some that remain and some that have been mown down.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"These Are Among the Stories We Have Recounted unto Thee of the Towns" — The Sūrah's Summary of the Middle**
