> **And be thou patient. Truly God neglects not the reward of the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Command and Its Ground**

The verse is brief: *wa-ṣbir fa-inna Llāha lā yuḍīʿu ajra l-muḥsinīn* — "and be thou patient. Truly God neglects not the reward of the virtuous." The commentators note that the command is given without qualification and the assurance attached directly to it.

The verb *ṣabara* denotes holding fast under pressure, and the commentators connect it to the steadfastness commanded at v. 112. The two are related but distinct: *istiqāma* is holding a straight course; *ṣabr* is enduring while doing so.

The particle *fa-inna* — "for truly" — makes the second clause the ground of the first. Patience is commanded because the reward will not be lost. The commentators read the sequence as supplying the reason the command is bearable.

The verse's brevity is itself notable. After the extended treatments of vv. 112–114, this verse gives the command and its ground in a single sentence. The commentators read the compression as indicating that the point has been established and needs no further elaboration.

**"Neglects Not" — the Verb and Its Implication**

The verb *yudīʿ* is from a root meaning to lose, waste, or let something perish through neglect. The commentators gloss the phrase as meaning that God does not let the reward be lost, whether through oversight or through indifference.

The negation is comprehensive. The verse does not say the reward will be given; it says it will not be neglected. The commentators note that this formulation addresses a specific anxiety — that deeds done in obscurity, or over a long period, or without result, might simply vanish.

This connects to v. 111's promise that each will be paid in full for their deeds. The two verses use different language for the same assurance: there, exact payment; here, no neglect. The commentators read them as complementary.

The phrase also answers the sūrah's theme of delay. The communities mocked the prophets because the promised vindication did not arrive. The verse assures the one commanded to be patient that the delay does not entail loss. What is owed is being kept.

**"The Virtuous" — Who the Assurance Covers**

The object of the assurance is *al-muḥsinūn* — the virtuous, or those who do what is good and beautiful. The commentators note that the term is broader than the believers simply as believers; it describes those whose action has the quality of *iḥsān*.

The commentators connect *iḥsān* to doing what is good with excellence and awareness, and note that the Qur'an uses the term for those who act well toward others as well as toward God. The assurance therefore extends beyond ritual observance.

The shift from the singular command — "be thou patient" — to the plural object — "the virtuous" — is worth noting. The commentators read it as extending the assurance from the Prophet to all who share his condition. The one commanded is not alone in the category.

The verse thus does two things at once. It addresses the Prophet specifically, and it places him within a class whose reward is guaranteed. The commentators read the movement as consolatory: the Prophet's patience is not a solitary burden but the characteristic of a company.

**Patience and the Sūrah's Narratives**

The command to patience comes after a hundred verses of narrative in which patience is repeatedly shown rather than stated. Noah endured centuries of mockery; Hūd and Ṣāliḥ persisted with communities that demanded punishment; Jacob's patience will be recalled in the sūrah's closing verses.

The commentators read the command as the sūrah's summary of its own material. The narratives have demonstrated what patience looks like and what it costs; the verse now states it as an instruction.

The assurance attached to the command is what the narratives supply by example. In each case the prophet who endured was vindicated and the community that refused was destroyed. The verse generalizes the pattern into a promise.

The commentators note that the verse does not promise vindication within the prophet's lifetime. Several of the sūrah's prophets did not see their communities' destruction. The assurance is that the reward is not neglected — not that it arrives on a visible schedule.

**Why Patience Comes Last in the Sequence**

Verses 112–115 form a unit of commands: steadfastness, avoidance of inclination, prayer, patience. The commentators note that patience comes last, and read the placement as significant.

The preceding commands are practices — holding a course, refusing a leaning, performing prayer. Patience is the disposition that sustains them over time. The commentators read the sequence as moving from what is done to what makes continued doing possible.

The verse's closing assurance is the sūrah's final word on the believer's condition before it turns to the historical reflection of vv. 116–117. Having been commanded, the believer is assured. The commentators read this as the appropriate note on which to close the instructional section.

The connection to v. 114's statement that good deeds remove evil is worth noting. The believer who has failed is not left with the failure; the believer who endures is not left without reward. The two assurances together address the two difficulties of the commanded life.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:116

> **So why were there not among the generations before you those possessing merit, who would forbid corruption upon the earth, other than a few of those whom We saved among them? Those who did wrong pursued the luxuries they had been given, and they were guilty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About the Past — Addressed to the Present**

The verse opens with an interrogative: *fa-law lā kāna mina l-qurūni min qablikum ūlū baqiyyatin yanhawna ʿani l-fasādi fī l-arḍ* — "so why were there not among the generations before you those possessing merit, who would forbid corruption upon the earth?"

The commentators note that the question is rhetorical and its addressee is the present audience. The sūrah has narrated the destruction of those generations; the verse asks why, within them, there were not people of *baqiyya* who opposed the corruption.

The word *baqiyya* is significant. The commentators gloss it as a remnant, a residue of goodness, and read *ūlū baqiyya* as those possessing a remainder of virtue — people of discernment and standing whose opposition would have carried weight.

The question implies that there were not, or that there were too few. The commentators read this as explaining the completeness of the destruction: a community is destroyed when those who might have restrained it are absent or silent.

**"Other Than a Few of Those Whom We Saved"**

The verse qualifies its own question: *illā qalīlan mimman anjaynā minhum* — "other than a few of those whom We saved among them." The commentators read the exception as identifying the remnant that did exist.

The word *qalīl* — few — is what the commentators emphasize. In each generation there were some who opposed the corruption, and they were saved. But their number was small, and the verse treats the smallness as significant.

The commentators connect this to the sūrah's narratives. Lot's household was saved except his wife; Noah's family except his son; Hūd's believers were saved while the rest were destroyed. In each case the saved were a minority.

The verse draws the general lesson from these particulars. Salvation was available and was taken by a few. The commentators read the verse as answering an unspoken question: the destruction was not indiscriminate, and the saved were those who forbade the corruption.

There is a warning in the exception for the present audience. The few were saved; the many were not. The verse implies that the hearers should not assume they belong to the majority by default.

**"They Pursued the Luxuries They Had Been Given"**

The verse's second half explains the failure: *wa-ttabaʿa lladhīna ẓalamū mā utrifū fīh* — "and those who did wrong pursued the luxuries they had been given." The commentators gloss *utrifū* as being given abundance to the point of spoilment.

The root *t-r-f* carries the sense of luxury that corrupts. The commentators note that the verse does not blame abundance as such but the pursuit of it — the *ittibāʿ*, the following after, that makes luxury the object of a life.

The phrase *mā utrifū fīh* — "that wherein they were given luxury" — is worth noting. The commentators read the construction as indicating that the luxury was given, not seized. The abundance was a provision, and the wrong lay in what was made of it.

This connects to the sūrah's treatment of wealth throughout. The communities destroyed were not poor; several are described as possessing gardens, springs, treasures, and power. The verse identifies the misuse of provision as the characteristic of the destroyed.

**"And They Were Guilty" — the Closing Judgment**

The verse ends with *wa-kānū mujrimīn* — "and they were guilty." The commentators note the perfect tense and the summary character of the statement.

The word *mujrim* denotes one who has committed a crime, and the commentators connect it to the root meaning to cut or sever. The guilty are those who have cut themselves off — from the messengers, from the remnant, from the provision's purpose.

The clause functions as a verdict rather than an argument. Having described the absence of the restraining remnant and the pursuit of luxury, the verse states the conclusion in two words. The commentators read the brevity as indicating that the case is closed.

The sequence is instructive. The verse does not present the guilt as the cause of the luxury or the silence; it presents it as what they amount to. The commentators read this as a statement about the whole pattern rather than about a single act.

**The Verse and the Community's Responsibility**

The verse raises a question about collective responsibility that the commentators have addressed at length. The destruction fell on the generation, and the verse asks why there were not more who forbade the corruption.

The commentators read the verse as establishing that forbidding corruption is the function of the *ūlū baqiyya* — those with standing and discernment. Their silence is not neutral; the verse treats their absence as contributing to the community's fate.

This connects to v. 113's prohibition of inclination. The believer who leans toward the wrongdoers has ceased to be a restraining presence. The two verses address the same reality from different angles: the verse warns against becoming part of the corruption; this verse notes the consequence when too few resist it.

The commentators note the address to the present audience. The question is put to *you* — *min qablikum*, "before you." The hearers are being asked to consider what their own generation's remnant consists of, and whether they are part of it.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:117

> **Your Lord will never destroy the towns unjustly while their people are reforming themselves. But if they reform, He will never destroy them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Your Lord Will Never Destroy the Towns Unjustly" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Justice of the Lord**

The sūrah now states the *Lord* in the *form of the not destroying the towns unjustly* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-mā kāna rabbudhābi-l-amdāri ma-lā yuḍlimu hāhunā ḥattā yubayyina amrahum* — "your Lord will never destroy the towns unjustly." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not a* *Lord that the sūrah holds in the state of the destroying the towns unjustly*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who does not destroy the towns unjustly in the state of the justice*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who does not destroy the towns unjustly in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the not destroying the towns unjustly* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One who does not destroy the towns unjustly*, in the way described in *verse 101's statement of the not wronging of the towns. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *not destroying the towns unjustly* is *not a* *not destroying that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the not destroying that the sūrah holds in the state of the justice of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 102's statement of the seizing of the Lord.

**"While Their People Are Reforming Themselves" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *ḥattā yubayyina amrahum* — "while their people are reforming themselves." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *people of the towns* are *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not reforming themselves*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are reformed in the state of the reforming themselves*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are reformed in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the reforming themselves* *applied to their own people*, and that the *applied to their own people* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *people* are *the people who are reformed while they are reforming themselves*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the sign for those who fear. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *reforming themselves* is *not a* *reforming that the Makkans can perform without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *reforming themselves* is *the state that the Makkans can leave by the not reforming*, in the way described in *verse 101's statement of the wronging of the towns upon themselves.

**"But If They Reform, He Will Never Destroy Them" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-in hūna-muqīnūn lā yukhrajūn min-hā ʿalayhim* — "but if they reform, He will never destroy them." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *reforming* is *not a* *reforming that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being a condition for the not destroying*, in the *sense of being* *the reforming that the sūrah characterizes as the condition for the not destroying of the towns*, in the *sense of being* *the reforming that the blessed holds in the state of the justice. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the not destroying if they reform* *applied to their own town*, and that the *applied to their own town* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *town* is *the town that the Lord does not destroy if they reform*, in the way described in *verse 100's statement of the towns that remain. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *not destroying if they reform* is *not a* *not destroying that the Makkans can approach without the reforming themselves*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the sign for those who fear. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *statement of the Lord who is over all things* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *not destroying if they reform is not the end of the statement of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 118.

---
## Sūrah Hūd 11:118

> **And had thy Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community. But they cease not to differ,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Counterfactual About Divine Will**

The verse states what did not happen: *wa-law shāʾa rabbuka la-jaʿala n-nāsa ummatan wāḥida* — "and had thy Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community." The commentators note the counterfactual construction and read it as establishing both divine capability and divine choice.

The capability is not in question. The verse states plainly that God could have made humanity a single community — one in belief, one in practice, one in direction. The commentators read this as removing any suggestion that the diversity of mankind reflects a limit on divine power.

The choice is what the verse establishes. God did not so will. The commentators read the counterfactual as indicating that the present condition — difference — is not an accident or a failure but the result of a willing that chose otherwise.

The phrase *ummatan wāḥida* — "one community" — is what the commentators discuss. Some read it as uniformity of belief; others as a single undivided body. The readings converge on the absence of division, and the verse treats that absence as something God declined to impose.

**"They Cease Not to Differ" — the Present Condition**

The verse continues: *wa-lā yazālūna mukhtalifīn* — "but they cease not to differ." The commentators note the imperfect tense with *lā yazālūna*, which denotes a continuing state rather than a single event.

The statement is descriptive rather than prescriptive. The verse does not command difference or endorse it; it states that difference persists. The commentators read this as an observation about the human condition under the divine will described in the first clause.

The connection between the two clauses is what the commentators emphasize. Because God did not will a single community, difference continues. The verse presents the persistence of disagreement as following from the divine choice rather than despite it.

This connects to v. 110's account of the Book given to Moses and the difference that arose regarding it. The commentators read the two verses as making the same point at different scales: revelation does not eliminate difference, because its elimination was not willed.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse is careful in its silences, and the commentators have noted them. It does not say that all differences are equally valid, that disagreement is approved, or that the search for truth is pointless.

The verse states a fact about divine willing and a fact about human condition. What follows — the next verse speaks of those upon whom God has mercy — indicates that the difference is not the end of the matter.

The commentators are explicit that the verse is not a charter for relativism. The sūrah has spent a hundred verses distinguishing those who accepted the messengers from those who refused, and it does not withdraw that distinction here. The difference that continues is described, not blessed.

It is worth recording that the verse has been read in both directions in the later tradition — as establishing toleration and as explaining why error persists. The verse itself supports neither reading exclusively, and the commentators preserve the tension.

**Why the Verse Comes Here**

The placement of the verse follows the historical reflection of vv. 116–117, which asked why the generations before lacked a restraining remnant and affirmed that God does not destroy towns unjustly while their people are reforming.

The commentators read the sequence as answering a question those verses raise. If God is just and does not destroy the reforming, why has history been a series of destructions? The verse answers at the level of divine willing: uniformity was not imposed, and difference is the condition in which the choice occurs.

The verse also addresses the Prophet's situation. He has been commanded to be steadfast and patient while his community rejects him. The verse tells him that the rejection is not evidence of failure. Difference was willed to continue, and the messenger's task is not to eliminate it by force.

The commentators connect this to v. 112's prohibition of *ṭughyān* — exceeding the command. The one who would compel uniformity would be doing what God declined to do. The verse sets a limit on the messenger's task.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 118 opens the sūrah's final movement, which runs to the end. The commentators note the shift from narrative and command to reflection on the shape of history and the closing instructions to the Prophet.

The verse's function is to reframe everything that has preceded. The destructions narrated are not evidence that God's purpose failed; they are what occurs within a willed diversity. The communities were given messengers, given time, and left to choose.

The commentators read this as the sūrah's answer to the disbelievers' central objection. They have treated the persistence of disagreement as evidence against the message. The verse answers that persistence was expected, and that it does not determine which side is right.

The verse thus prepares the closing instructions. Having established that difference continues, the sūrah tells the Prophet what to do within it — and the answer, given in the following verses, is prayer, patience, and reliance on God rather than the compulsion of agreement.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:119

> **Save those upon whom thy Lord has Mercy—and for this He created them. And the Word of thy Lord is fulfilled: “I shall surely fill Hell with jinn and men all together!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Save Those Upon Whom Thy Lord Has Mercy" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Exception**

The sūrah now states the *exception* to the *difference of mankind* in a *single* *clause*: *illā man raḥima rabbuka* — "save those upon whom thy Lord has mercy." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *mankind* is *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not having an exception*, in the *sense of being* *the people who have an exception in the state of the mercy of the Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the people who have an exception in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the exception of the mercy of the Lord* *applied to their own people*, and that the *applied to their own people* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *people* is *the people who have an exception in the state of the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 118's statement of the believers who are seized by their Lord. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *exception of the mercy of the Lord* is *not a* *exception that the Makkans can approach without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"And for This He Created Them" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-lijālika khalaḍahum* — "and for this He created them." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *creation of the exception* is *not a* *creation that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being for the mercy*, in the *sense of being* *the creation that the sūrah characterizes as the creation for the mercy*, in the *sense of being* *the creation that the blessed holds in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the creation for the mercy* *applied to their own people*, and that the *applied to their own people* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *people* is *the people who are created for the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 116's statement of the One who is the only god. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *creation for the mercy* is *not a* *creation that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the creation that the sūrah holds in the state of the mercy*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"And the Word of Thy Lord Is Fulfilled" — The Word That the Sūrah Adds**

The *third* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-qaḍa qawlu rabbī la-a-mala-an-nār* — "and the word of thy Lord is fulfilled." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *word of the Lord* is *not a* *word that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being fulfilled*, in the *sense of being* *the word that the sūrah characterizes as the word that the Lord has fulfilled in the state of the fulfillment*, in the *sense of being* *the word that the blessed holds in the state of the fulfillment. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the fulfillment of the word of the Lord* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One whose word is fulfilled*, in the way described in *verse 1's statement of the One who is Wise and Aware. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *fulfillment of the word of the Lord* is *not a* *fulfillment that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the fulfillment that the sūrah holds in the state of the word of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 116's statement of the One who is the only god.

**"I Shall Surely Fill Hell with Jinn and Men All Together" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *lā a-mala-an-nār min al-jinni wa-n-nāsi jamīʿan* — "I shall surely fill Hell with jinn and men all together." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *filling of Hell with jinn and men all together* is *not a* *filling that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being all together*, in the *sense of being* *the filling that the sūrah characterizes as the filling of Hell with jinn and men all together*, in the *sense of being* *the filling that the blessed holds in the state of the fulfillment. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the filling of Hell with jinn and men all together* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One who fills Hell with jinn and men all together*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the gathering of mankind on the Day. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *filling of Hell with jinn and men all together* is *not a* *filling that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the filling that the sūrah holds in the state of the fulfillment of the word of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 119's statement of the exception of the mercy of the Lord.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:120

> **All that We recount unto thee of the stories of the messengers is that whereby We make firm thine heart. And herein there has come unto thee the Truth, and an exhortation and a reminder for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"All That We Recount Unto Thee of the Stories of the Messengers" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Stories**

The sūrah now states the *purpose* of the *stories of the messengers* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-mā naḥī laka min qasasi-r-rusul* — "all that We recount unto thee of the stories of the messengers." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *stories of the messengers* are *not a* *stories that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being recounted to the Prophet*, in the *sense of being* *the stories that the sūrah characterizes as the stories that the sūrah has recounted to the Prophet in the state of the recounting*, in the *sense of being* *the stories that the blessed holds in the state of the recounting. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the stories of the messengers* *applied to their own messengers*, and that the *applied to their own messengers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *messengers* are *the messengers whose stories the sūrah has recounted to the Prophet*, in the way described in *verse 100's statement of the stories of the towns. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *stories of the messengers* are *not a* *stories that the Makkans can refuse without the recounting of the sūrah*, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part.

**"Is That Whereby We Make Firm Thine Heart" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *bi-dhālika nuthabbitu fudduka* — "is that whereby We make firm thine heart." The *nathabbita* — the *making firm* — is the *word* that the *commenters note* is *the strengthening of the Prophet's heart through the recounting of the stories of the messengers, so that he may be firm in his mission and not be distressed by the refusal of the Makkans*, and that the *naming* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *heart of the Prophet* is *not a* *heart that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being made firm*, in the *sense of being* *the heart that the sūrah characterizes as the heart that is made firm by the recounting of the stories of the messengers*, in the *sense of being* *the heart that the blessed holds in the state of the making firm. The sūrah is stating, in the naming, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the making firm of the heart of the Prophet* *applied to their own Prophet*, and that the *applied to their own Prophet* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Prophet* is *the one whose heart is made firm by the recounting of the stories of the messengers*, in the way described in *verse 34's statement of the prophet's completion of his part. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *making firm of the heart of the Prophet* is *not a* *making firm that the Makkans can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"And Herein There Has Come Unto Thee the Truth" — The Word That the Sūrah Adds**

The *third* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-jaʾaka fīhi-l-qurʾānu l-haqqu* — "and herein there has come unto thee the Truth." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *truth* is *not a* *truth that the sūrah holds in the state of the not coming unto the Prophet*, in the *sense of being* *the truth that the sūrah characterizes as the truth that has come unto the Prophet in the state of the coming*, in the *sense of being* *the truth that the blessed holds in the state of the coming. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the truth that has come unto the Prophet* *applied to their own Prophet*, and that the *applied to their own Prophet* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Prophet* is *the one unto whom the truth has come*, in the way described in *verse 1's statement of the Book from the Presence of the Wise and Aware. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *truth that has come unto the Prophet* is *not a* *truth that the Makkans can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"And an Exhortation and a Reminder for the Believers" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-naẓīr wa-dhikrā li-l-muʾminīn* — "and an exhortation and a reminder for the believers." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *exhortation and the reminder* are *not a* *exhortation and a reminder that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being for the believers*, in the *sense of being* *the exhortation and the reminder that the sūrah characterizes as the exhortation and the reminder for the believers*, in the *sense of being* *the exhortation and the reminder that the blessed holds in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the exhortation and the reminder for the believers* *applied to their own believers*, and that the *applied to their own believers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *believers* are *the believers for whom the exhortation and the reminder are for*, in the way described in *verse 103's statement of the sign for those who fear. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *exhortation and the reminder for the believers* is *not a* *exhortation and a reminder that the Makkans can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verses* — the *statement of the acting according to your position* — are *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *exhortation and the reminder for the believers is not the end of the statement of the truth*, in the way described in *verses 121–122.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:121

> **And say unto those who believe not, “Act according to your position; we, too, are acting.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And Say Unto Those Who Believe Not" — The Sūrah's Command to the Prophet**

The sūrah now states the *command* of the *sūrah* to the *Prophet* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-qul li-l-ladhīna kafarū ʿmalū* — "and say unto those who believe not, 'act according to your position.'" The *command* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet* is *not a* *Prophet that the sūrah holds in the state of the not saying to those who believe not*, in the *sense of being* *the Prophet who says to those who believe not in the state of the saying*, in the *sense of being* *the Prophet who says to those who believe not in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the command, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the command of the saying to those who believe not* *applied to their own Prophet*, and that the *applied to their own Prophet* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Prophet* is *the one who says to those who believe not*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the turning the face to the religion of God. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Makkans* who *refuse the Prophet* are *in the state of the refusal of the command of the sūrah*, in the way described in *verse 101's statement of the wronging of the towns upon themselves.

**"Act According to Your Position" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-ana naʿmalu* — "act according to your position." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans who believe not* are *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not acting according to their position*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are asked to act according to their position in the state of the acting*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are asked to act according to their position in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans who believe not* are *hearing the statement of the acting according to their position* *applied to their own position*, and that the *applied to their own position* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans who believe not* are *the people who are asked to act according to their position*, in the way described in *verse 113's statement of the disbelievers who are not the ones who are guided. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *acting according to their position* is *not a* *acting that the Makkans who believe not can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the acting that the sūrah holds in the state of the acting according to their position*, in the way described in *verse 110's statement of the command that cannot be avoided.

**"We, Too, Are Acting" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-ana naʿmalu* — "we, too, are acting." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *believers* are *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not acting*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are acting in the state of the acting*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are acting in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the believers who are acting* *applied to their own believers*, and that the *applied to their own believers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *believers* are *the people who are acting*, in the way described in *verse 114's statement of the gathering of the believers unto the Lord. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *acting of the believers* is *not a* *acting that the Makkans who believe not can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *statement of the waiting* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *acting of the believers is not the end of the statement of the command*, in the way described in *verse 122.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:122

> **And wait! We, too, are waiting.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And Wait" — The Sūrah's Command to the Prophet**

The sūrah now states the *command* of the *sūrah* to the *Prophet* in a *single* *clause*: *wa-intaẓirū* — "and wait!" The *command* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Prophet* is *not a* *Prophet that the sūrah holds in the state of the not waiting*, in the *sense of being* *the Prophet who waits in the state of the waiting*, in the *sense of being* *the Prophet who waits in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the command, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the command of the waiting* *applied to their own Prophet*, and that the *applied to their own Prophet* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Prophet* is *the one who waits*, in the way described in *verse 121's statement of the acting according to the position. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *waiting* is *not a* *waiting that the Makkans who believe not can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"We, Too, Are Waiting" — The Word That the Sūrah Closes With**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-ana nantadhiru* — "we, too, are waiting." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *believers* are *not a* *people that the sūrah holds in the state of the not waiting*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are waiting in the state of the waiting*, in the *sense of being* *the people who are waiting in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the believers who are waiting* *applied to their own believers*, and that the *applied to their own believers* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *believers* are *the people who are waiting*, in the way described in *verse 114's statement of the gathering of the believers unto the Lord. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *waiting of the believers* is *not a* *waiting that the Makkans who believe not can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *statement of the Lord who is over all things* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *waiting of the believers is not the end of the statement of the command*, in the way described in *verse 123.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:123

> **Unto God belongs the Unseen in the heavens and on the earth, and unto Him are all matters returned. So worship Him, and trust in Him. And thy Lord is not heedless of that which you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Unto God Belongs the Unseen in the Heavens and on the Earth" — The Sūrah's Statement of the Lord's Dominion Over the Unseen**

The sūrah now closes with the *statement* of the *Lord's dominion over the unseen* in a *single* *clause*: *li-llāhi l-ghayb wa-l-ashri wa-ila-hi yuraju-l-amr* — "unto God belongs the unseen in the heavens and on the earth." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not a* *Lord that the sūrah holds in the state of the not belonging to the unseen in the heavens and on the earth*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who belongs to the unseen in the heavens and on the earth in the state of the dominion*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who belongs to the unseen in the heavens and on the earth in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the Lord's dominion over the unseen in the heavens and on the earth* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One who belongs to the unseen in the heavens and on the earth*, in the way described in *verse 115's statement of the One who knows the secrets of the hearts. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *unseen in the heavens and on the earth* are *not a* *unseen that the Makkans can approach without the dominion of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 106's statement of the two easts and the two wests.

**"And unto Him Are All Matters Returned" — The Word That the Sūrah Gives**

The *second* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-ila-hi yuraju-l-amr kulluhū* — "and unto Him are all matters returned." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *matters* are *not a* *matters that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being returned to the Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the matters that the sūrah characterizes as the matters that are returned to the Lord in the state of the return*, in the *sense of being* *the matters that the blessed holds in the state of the return. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the return of all matters to the Lord* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One to whom all matters are returned*, in the way described in *verse 1's statement of the One who is Wise and Aware. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *return of all matters to the Lord* is *not a* *return that the Makkans can avoid by their refusal*; it is *the return that the sūrah holds in the state of the dominion of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 116's statement of the One who is the only god.

**"So Worship Him, and Trust in Him" — The Word That the Sūrah Adds**

The *third* *clause* states the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *fa-ʿbudhū wa-tawakkal ʿalayh* — "so worship him, and trust in him." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *worship and the trust* are *not a* *worship and a trust that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being for the Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the worship and the trust that the sūrah characterizes as the worship and the trust for the Lord*, in the *sense of being* *the worship and the trust that the blessed holds in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the worship and the trust for the Lord* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One whom the sūrah commands to be worshipped and trusted in*, in the way described in *verse 109's statement of the turning the face to the religion of God. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *worship and the trust for the Lord* is *not a* *worship and a trust that the Makkans can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful.

**"And Thy Lord Is Not Heedless of That Which You Do" — The Closing That the Sūrah Gives**

The *verse* *closes* with the *word* that the *sūrah* gives with *particular* *weight*: *wa-lā kāna rabbukā nāsiyan mā taʿmalūn* — "and thy Lord is not heedless of that which you do." The *statement* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord* is *not a* *Lord that the sūrah holds in the state of the not being heedless of that which the Makkans do*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who is not heedless of that which the Makkans do in the state of the not being heedless*, in the *sense of being* *the Lord who is not heedless of that which the Makkans do in the state of the mercy. The sūrah is stating, in the statement, that the *Makkans* are *hearing the statement of the Lord who is not heedless of that which they do* *applied to their own Lord*, and that the *applied to their own Lord* is the *sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Makkans'* *Lord* is *the One who is not heedless of that which they do*, in the way described in *verse 115's statement of the One who knows the secrets of the hearts. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *recording*, that the *Lord who is not heedless of that which the Makkans do* is *not a* *Lord that the Makkans can refuse without the mercy of the Lord*, in the way described in *verse 112's statement of the Lord who is all-merciful. The *sūrah* is *stating*, in the *closing*, that the *next verse* — the *end of the commentary* — is *the sūrah's* way of *stating* that the *Lord who is not heedless of that which the Makkans do is not the end of the statement of the Lord*, in the way described in the *end of the commentary on Sūrah Hūd*.

---

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah Hūd]**
